She did not open the bag and she did not close it either, and it lay there with the catch resting shut and not fastened, which he noticed and did not look at.

Neither of them said anything until the ramp.

"Well," said Annie.

"May I give you the whole of it?"

"That's what the evening was for."

"Then I'll give it to you in the order it arrived and not in the order it matters, because those are different, and the sorting is yours."

"Go on."

---

He gave it to her from seven twenty-two.

He did not shape it. He did not lead with the good part or hold the bad part for the end. Twice he said that he did not know what something meant, and left it lying there without a theory on top of it.

It took fourteen minutes.

Woo first, because Woo was first: four minutes on Yeongjong, the tonnage financed by somebody he was pleased about and would not name, the word Incheon twice without meaning it. Then Friday. Then two approaches to buy the terminal, April and six weeks ago, and the second man had known Woo's numbers better than Woo's own finance director, and Woo had not enjoyed that.

And the dinner.

"I asked him who introduced him to his financier. He said *somebody at a dinner*, and then he waved at a woman across the room, and that was the end of it."

"You didn't push."

"No. He'd told me no, and he'd been polite about it, and pushing would have cost me Friday." A pause. "I thought that was a good decision for about three hours."

"And now?"

"Now I think a man who deflects a harmless question isn't being private. He's covering somebody." Georgij looked out at the expressway lights going over. "That's all I have. I have a name in my head and nothing at all that puts it behind Woo's money, and I am not going to make that connection on the way home because it would be convenient."

"You've thought of one."

"I've thought of one, and it fits, and things that fit at half past midnight are exactly what I ought to be frightened of." He let it go there and did not say it. "I'll get it properly. His terminal took ten months and there was a bank in it, and banks leave a syndicate, and a syndicate is eleven or twelve people who all had to sign something. One of them will have talked to somebody, and I want it out of a document, not out of a mood."

"How long?"

"Two weeks. Possibly three."

"Take three," said Annie. "Go on."

---

Then Kang.

"I went to him. He didn't come to me." He said it plainly. "I asked him for guidance on land and zoning and port status, because it's a real gap and asking is cheaper than covering it. It was the second time I'd used that tonight and the first time had worked extremely well."

"Hong."

"Hong. And Hana had told me thirty minutes earlier that it was a good tool and that I should be careful how many times it fit, and I did it anyway."

"Say the rest."

"Hong is sixty-one and it flattered him. Kang is forty-three and he heard a man telling him where the hole was." He kept his chin level. "He asked how he could reach me. I declined to give him anything, which was the only sound thing I did in the whole exchange, and it will not matter, because he has a ministry and I have a face."

"He'll call."

"He'll call. I don't know what he wants and I don't know what he'll ask." Georgij said it flatly. "But I know where he'll aim it. Land, permissions, port status. That is the one subject in the world I told him I was empty on, and I told him myself, standing at the north bar, in about half a minute."

He stopped.

"He didn't have to find the gap. I walked across a room and showed it to him. There is nobody else to put that on. You are getting it tonight and not in a fortnight, when the telephone goes and you work out for yourself who made the call worth making."

"Yes," said Annie. "You would."

---

He gave her the girl from the foundation and the fourteen sites and the three that had not spent it on staff. He gave her the two directors at the Paradise, and the February sale, and the March dinners a month afterwards, one man at a time, four hundred kilometres from where either of them worked.

He gave her the word *container*, on its own, off a terrace, in the middle of a sentence about something else.

He told her he could make nothing of it.

"Hana talked," he said. "Not carelessly. She has never once had to pay for anything she has said on a terrace and it has made her about three per cent louder than she would otherwise be. I got Incheon five times, the eastern parcel, a date in the spring, an approach she thinks was a discourtesy and not a threat, and a minister who already knew and did not perform surprise."

He looked out at the road for a moment.

"And she gave me something to carry. She has known you twelve years and says you have never once brought anybody anywhere. In about a month the two of you are going to need each other rather badly, and by then she will have to know whether I am the strongest thing you own or the softest. She spent the evening finding out."

"And?"

"She said she does not know yet, and that this was the part she wanted me to take away with me."

Annie looked out of her own window.

"She told you the month."

"She did."

"Then she has already decided to come up this drive, and she was telling me so through you."

"Yes, Mistress."

"And the Kims?"

"Nine of them, all in one room on a night they had a choice about. Customs in their logistics building six days, which Hana told me and which she should not have told me." He paused for the length of a breath. "Two of the nine are worth something. One of them kept fetching drinks and laughed a quarter of a beat behind everybody else."

Annie turned her head.

"And the other?"

"A woman in her fifties at the back, who did not speak once in four minutes and had the whole group arranged round her. Twice somebody said something and looked at her before going on, and neither of them knew they had done it." A pause. "She is the one I want. He is the one I can find."

"Which of them is which?"

"That's the difficulty." He said it without any embellishment at all, because it was not a good answer and dressing it up would not have made it one. "I have all eleven of their names. Two of them have been photographed. The other nine have spent their entire lives in a business that nobody points a camera at, and I stood eight metres from them for four seconds and could not put a single name on a single face."

"So you have two descriptions."

"Late thirties, the shortest of the men, fetched drinks twice while I watched, laughed a quarter of a beat behind the room. And a woman in her fifties who said nothing and had the group built round her. Four or five names each." He turned his head. "Paper gives you names. It does not give you faces, and I have been building this on paper for three weeks."

"So how do you close it?"

"Somebody's mouth. It always is." A small pause. "Do you want to give me the name, or shall I go and get it?"

"Go and get it," said Annie.

"Yes."

---

Then Sang-hoon, because Sang-hoon had been last.

He gave her the table, and the four questions that were not conversation, and the fact that the man had watched his hands while he answered all of them and not his face.

He gave her that none of it had worked. Not the smile, not the recent thing, not one single instrument he had used successfully on eight hundred people that evening.

Then he gave her the dinner. After the fifteenth, at Sang-hoon's office's convenience, and the last thing the man had said before he stood up.

"He said: bring whatever it is you're going to bring. He'd rather see it over food than have it slid under a door."

"That's a good sentence."

"It's an instruction. He has decided in advance that he will take whatever I bring him, and he has therefore stopped caring what it is."

"Which is exactly what I want. I've been trying since twenty past eleven to find the part of that which is a trap, and I can't, and that bothers me a great deal more than finding one would have."

The car came off the expressway and into the tunnel.

"There's one more thing at that table," said Georgij, "and I want to say it while we're still moving."

"Say it."

"He asked me what you paid for me."

Annie did not move at all.

"He doesn't know," said Georgij.

"No. He wouldn't." She said it almost before he had finished, and she had not been at the table when it was asked. "He has had the word adviser at him since nine o'clock and he prices people for a living. He was asking about a wage."

"Yes."

"What did you tell him?"

"Two hundred and twenty million."

The tunnel ended.

"He asked whether that was for the year," said Georgij. "I said no."

Annie was quiet for a long moment.

"That's the truth."

"Every word of it. He laughed, and he said you don't waste money, and that if you paid that then you believe it." A small pause. "He also said it would be a great pity if I were ordinary."

"And you're pleased with yourself."

"No, Mistress." He said it without any performance in it at all. "I gave a man the exact figure. He put it in the wrong column, and he'll keep it in the wrong column for as long as nothing else lands next to it, and I have no control at all over what lands next to it. That was the cheapest available answer and it was not a safe one."

---

They were on the road up when he gave her the last of it.

"There is one more from the floor, and it comes before that one." He looked at the trees. "I took her hand and kissed it. Over the fingers, in the European way, in front of four or five people who will describe it inside the week and use that word."

"Why?"

"Because eight hundred people had spent the evening deciding what I am. I gave them an answer that is not one and let them keep it." A pause. "She said it was the first defensive thing she had seen me do, and that she would have done exactly the same."

"Does it hold?"

"A fortnight. Then somebody who was not there asks somebody who was, and the answer gets shorter every time it is passed on, and short answers are the ones people check."

---

"I broke the rule you gave me in this car."

Annie turned her head.

"Which rule?"

"Annie in public, and the other word nowhere at all." His hands stayed where they were on his knees. "I used the other word to Hana. Once, on the floor, at about twenty-five to eleven, before I went to Woo. She had been kind to me for eleven minutes and I had just told her something true that I had not intended to say, and it came out of me before I heard it going."

The cypresses started.

"What did she do?"

"Nothing. She said *yes*, and then she put her glass down on a table, which is not a thing a woman of that sort does, and then she sent me across the room to stand near you." He looked at the trees. "She didn't ask about it. She won't. She'll keep it, and she'll be kind about it, and it will cost me every time she is."

"What did you tell her that was true?"

"She asked where my people come from."

The car went on up.

"I don't have an answer to that," said Georgij. "I've never had one. I said so."

Annie did not say anything for perhaps two hundred metres.

"Thank you for telling me," she said.

That was all of it. She did not say it warmly and she did not say it coldly, and there was nothing after it, and Georgij discovered that he would very much rather have been shouted at.

---

"Now say the other thing," said Annie.

"Mistress?"

"You've been holding something since Woo. You've given me fourteen minutes of everything you have and most of it was against your own interest. So say the last one. I am not sitting through you deciding whether you're allowed to."

Georgij inclined his head.

"On the first night I told you the auction house would talk for about three weeks and then agree a version between them. It's been three weeks and a day." The bag was still lying between them with its catch resting shut. "You are not a woman who lets a window shut. I think you have the name of whoever stood behind the man in the second row. I think you've had it for most of the time I've been in your house."

Annie looked at him.

"Yes," she said.

"Thank you."

"That's it?"

"You asked me to say it and I've said it. Whether you give it to me is yours." He turned his hand over, smaller than he had done it in a car on the first night. "I'd take it. I think it would save me a fortnight. But I've watched you decline to answer four times now and I've stopped thinking the not answering is laziness."

The car slowed for the turn.

"I'm not going to give it to you," said Annie.

"No, Mistress."

"You haven't asked why."

"No."

"Ask."

"Why?"

"Because I've looked at it," said Annie, "and it changes what I am going to have to ask you to do."

She said it looking straight ahead, in exactly the tone she had used for the seating and the coat and the tailor, and she did not add anything to it, and the car came onto the gravel and stopped.

---

He got out and went round and held her door, and she got out, and she took the clutch with her.

Mrs Seo had left the hall light on and gone to bed.

Georgij stood just inside the door for a moment with his coat over his arm, in a hall he now knew better than anybody in that house except the woman who had drawn it.

He looked at the stone shelf beside the door. The shallow bowl was on it, and there was nothing next to the bowl.

Annie went down the hall without taking her shoes off.

He heard the study door.

Then, a few seconds later, he heard a drawer.

---

# Book One · Chapter 12: You are better when you don't know
*Lot Fourteen* · Version 1.7 · EN

## Day Twenty-Three · Sunday 26 October

He was in the kitchen at seven, in the clothes that had been left outside his door on the first morning, and nobody said anything about the night before.

Mrs Seo put a plate in front of him and took the tray up at four minutes past, the way she had for nine years.

Ji-won came in at ten past and said good morning and then said, in the manner of somebody who has spent an hour deciding whether to, that she had seen a photograph.

"Where?"

"On my telephone. It was in the news part." She put the cutlery down and picked it up again. "You were going in. With ma'am."

"Was it a good photograph?"

"You looked very serious."

"That's the collar," said Georgij, and Ji-won laughed before she had decided whether she was allowed to, and then went and did the flowers.

Mr Yeo came in at twenty past for his coffee, looked at Georgij's throat for slightly too long, and took the coffee outside.

Eun-ju arrived at eight and had clearly also seen the photograph, and did not mention it once in eight hours, which Georgij found considerably more interesting than if she had.

---

Jang came up from the security office at nine with two sheets of paper.

"Your evening," he said, and put them down. "Mine, not yours. Take it."

Georgij read it.

Two of Jang's men on the cars. One inside the building who had not gone within forty metres of Georgij all night, exactly as agreed. The times in and out. A note that the hotel's own people had been competent and unhurried, which from Jang was extravagant praise.

At the bottom, in smaller writing, a list of four vehicles that had been in the ramp area for longer than made sense, with plates.

"Three of those are drivers waiting," said Jang. "The fourth is a private hire that sat there from eight to eleven forty with nobody in the back of it and did not pick anybody up."

"Did you photograph it?"

"I photographed all four. It's a hire company in Mapo. I have not telephoned them and I am not going to, because a hire company in Mapo would have passed on the caller's name within an hour."

Georgij looked at him.

"Mr Jang."

"I know."

"No, you don't. I am about to say something that will annoy you."

"Say it."

"That is a better piece of work than anything I did between seven and midnight," said Georgij, "and I was working the entire time."

Jang collected his two sheets, and squared them, and did not look particularly pleased, because a man of that sort never does.

"I'll leave you the plates," he said, and went back downstairs.

---

Annie did not send for him.

Not on the twenty-third and not on the twenty-fourth. He saw her twice, once in the hall and once from a window, and on both occasions she nodded and went on.

So he worked.

The syndicate on Woo's terminal turned out to be nine institutions, which meant nine sets of filings. By the second evening he had reduced the nine to three that had taken a position large enough to require an internal approval. An approval requires a name at the bottom.

It was slow and it was extremely dull and it was exactly the kind of work he had been doing since he was seventeen.

He did not enjoy it.

He had noticed at the auction, and again in the ballroom, that he was becoming a man who preferred rooms to documents, and he did not think that was a good thing to be becoming.

---

## Day Twenty-Five · Tuesday 28 October

The telephone rang at twenty past four in the afternoon.

Georgij looked at the number for two seconds, which is a very long time to look at a number, and then answered it.

"Yes?"

"Mr Kang Ji-hoon, Ministry of Land, Infrastructure and Transport." As though they had not stood eight feet apart for four minutes. "We met."

"We did. Thank you for finding me."

"It took my office six minutes." A small pause, into which Georgij was expected to say something and did not. "I hope you don't mind."

"You told me you had a ministry."

Kang laughed, the same short one, over in a moment.

"I am going to ask you something and it is going to sound like a test, and I would like you to know in advance that it isn't one."

"Then it's a test," said Georgij pleasantly, "and you and I can both live with that. Go on."

He had put the smile on before he said it. Nobody could see it, at either end, and he built it anyway. A voice carries a smile, and a pleasant thing said with a flat mouth arrives flat.

"There has been some interest in a change of designation on the eastern side at Incheon. Port land. It would be a fairly technical thing." Kang's voice did not go anywhere at all. "What is your side's position on it?"

---

There it was, on a Tuesday afternoon, four days after he had walked across a room and shown a man exactly where to put it.

He could not say *I don't know*, because he did know five fragments and *I don't know* was not true.

He could not say *we have no position*, because he had no idea whether that was true and would have been guessing.

He could not deflect, because Kang was listening for a deflection and would have heard it in the shape of the breath.

And he could not lie.

"I have no position," said Georgij.

"That isn't what I asked."

"I know. It's what I have." He let it be as plain as it was. "Land is the subject I told you I was empty on, four nights ago, standing at a bar. I have not become full since. Whatever my side's position is, I would be inventing it for you, and I'd rather be useless to you than inventive."

There was a silence on the line that went on slightly too long.

"That is a much better answer than the one I expected," said Kang.

"It's the only one I have."

"You could have said you'd find out and come back to me. That's what everybody says."

"Then you'd have had two conversations with me instead of one, and the second one would have been worse."

Another pause.

"May I say something," said Kang, "and you can take it as rudeness if you like."

"Please."

"On Saturday you asked me for guidance. Chairman Woo has told several people that you asked him the same thing, in almost the same words, some minutes earlier." The voice remained entirely pleasant. "I found that interesting for four days and I have decided to tell you that I found it interesting, rather than to go on finding it interesting quietly."

Georgij closed his eyes for a moment, in a room where nobody could see him do it.

"That's fair."

"Is it a technique?"

"It's a habit and it's a new one." He said it lightly, and every word of it was true. "I am standing in rooms full of men who have been doing this since before I could read, and I would be a fool not to ask them things. I asked two of them in one evening and it did not occur to me until afterwards that they might compare notes." A small pause. "I'll be more careful. Not because it wasn't sincere. Because it looked like something."

"Yes," said Kang Ji-hoon. "It did."

"Thank you for telling me."

"You'll want to read up on port designations."

"I've started."

"Then read the 2019 amendment first, and don't read the guidance notes, because the guidance notes were written by somebody in my building who has never seen a port." Kang sounded almost warm. "Good afternoon."

The line went dead.

Georgij sat with the telephone in his hand for a while.

He had come out of it about as well as it could have gone. He had not lied once, and he had been handed a piece of genuine help at the end of it.

He had also given something away, and it took him a further ten minutes to work out what.

He had not told Kang his side's position, because he did not have it. What he had told him, in his own voice, twice, was that Annie's adviser had not been briefed on Incheon.

That was the useful sentence. Kang could take it to his minister on any Tuesday of the year, and it had two readings and both of them were worth something. Either that side was keeping it away from a man they had bought expensively, in which case it mattered. Or there was nothing there at all, in which case a great deal of somebody's caution had been wasted.

Georgij did not know which of those two Kang would carry, and he could not correct either without inventing.

He also did not know whether the line had been recorded. He assumed it had, which cost nothing, and which he had assumed from the second the number came up.

---

## Day Twenty-Six · Wednesday 29 October

She sent for him at eight in the evening, which she had never done.

The study had one lamp on, and the machine under the window was off, and there was nothing on the desk at all, which in that room meant she had cleared it before he came in.

"Sit down."

He sat down.

"I've had the name for nineteen days," said Annie. "You worked that out in the car and you were right, and I've spent four of those days deciding what to do about the fact that you were right."

"Yes, Mistress."

"I'm still not going to give it to you."

"I didn't think you would."

"In the car you asked me why because I told you to ask. Ask because you want to know."

"Why not?"

"Because if I say it, you will spend the next two weeks deciding how to behave in a room. You will get it exactly right. Getting it exactly right is the one thing that would tell him what you are." She had her hands flat on the desk. "You are better when you don't know. That is not a compliment. It should not be true and it is."

Georgij did not say anything.

"Now the other part," said Annie.

---

"There is a man I need in a room with me. He does not go to rooms. He has not stood in one with more than six people in it for as long as I have known he exists, and there are two people in this city who could describe his face."

"And you know what he wants."

"I know exactly what he wants," said Annie, "and I have exactly one of it in this house."

The lamp hummed.

Georgij sat with his hands loose on his knees and did not move at all, and worked it through twice, and it came out the same way both times.

"You want him to look at me."

"Yes."

"With the collar."

"With the collar," said Annie. "Not shown. Not announced. Found." She said it in the flattest voice she had used in twenty-six days. "He would have to be the one who worked it out, and he would have to believe he had worked it out against me, or it's worth nothing."

"And then he comes to the room."

"Then he comes to the room."

Georgij looked at the desk for a moment.

"May I ask what I would be for, in that room?"

"You would be the reason he was in it."

"That isn't what I asked."

"No," said Annie. "It isn't."

---

She let that stand there for a while, and did not soften it, and did not take it back, and did not explain it.

"You can refuse," she said. "I want to be clear about that before you answer, and I am telling you before you answer and not after. If you say no tonight I will find another way in and it will be slower and worse and I will not raise it again."

"And if I say yes?"

"Then I will not tell you his name, or the date, or what he has done, and you will walk into a room having been told less than anybody in it." Annie's face did not move anywhere. "You would be doing it on my word."

"That's the whole of the contract."

"Yes," said Annie. "It is."

Georgij was quiet for a long moment.

He was aware, sitting in that chair, that he had spent twenty-six days building an instrument out of paper and rooms and other people's mouths.

He was being asked to put the whole of it down on a table and be an object again, on purpose, for a man whose face two people in this city had seen.

He was also aware that she had told him he could refuse, in advance, in a room with no witnesses, and that nobody had ever done that before.

"I'd like to sleep on it," he said.

"Yes."

"Not because I'm going to say no."

"I know why," said Annie.

---

He stood up.

At the door he stopped, and turned round, and asked the only thing he had left.

"Is it a man who was in the room when you bought me?"

Annie looked at him across the desk for what felt like a considerable time.

"No," she said.

And it was true, and both of them knew it was true. Georgij went up the stairs understanding perfectly well that it was also the most carefully constructed sentence he had heard since the auctioneer said the word *unverifiable*.

---

# Book One · Chapter 13: The man with the open hand
*Lot Fourteen* · Version 2.5 · EN

*Days 27 to 28 · Thursday 30 to Friday 31 October*

She came into the kitchen at seven the following morning.

She had done it twice before in twenty-seven days, on the first two mornings, and not once since. The tray had gone up at seven every day for nine years and had gone up at seven yesterday. Ji-won put down the plate she was holding and picked it up again, which was what she had done the first time.

Mrs Seo said good morning without turning round and went on with the eggs, and the whole kitchen understood that this was not a woman who had come down for breakfast.

She had come down because there was an answer waiting and she was not going to take delivery of it in her own study, where she would have been sitting and he would have been standing.

Georgij got up.

"Yes, Mistress."

Annie stopped with the cup halfway off its hook.

"That's all of it?"

"That's all of it."

"You haven't asked me for anything."

"No."

"Everybody asks for something." She took the cup down. "The four before me asked for things and they had a great deal less to lose than you do."

"I know what I'd be asking for," said Georgij, "and I know what it would cost you to give it, and I'd rather find out what you do when nobody has put a price on the table."

He said it entirely pleasantly, and sat back down at the end of the long table, and Mrs Seo put a plate in front of him.

Annie did not sit. She stood at the window with her coffee for slightly longer than she needed to, and then she took the cup with her and went, which she had not done either of the other two times.

She did not like it. He watched her not like it, from the far end of the long table, over eggs. He found that he could not tell whether that was a good outcome or a bad one. He decided he was content to find out.

---

Jang had left him four registration plates and had declined, correctly, to telephone a hire company in Mapo.

Georgij did not telephone the hire company either.

He went at it from the other end, which took him a day and a half and consisted almost entirely of being polite to people whose job nobody respects.

A private hire vehicle that sits in a hotel ramp for three hours and forty minutes has not been flagged down in the street. It has been booked, and booked for a block. A block booking is placed on an account, and an account generates a docket. A docket goes to the hotel because the hotel charges the ramp by the hour to anybody who is not a guest.

The hotel's ramp is run by a facilities company. The facilities company has a supervisor. The supervisor has an office the size of a wardrobe under the north stair, and a laminated list of account numbers taped to the wall beside his kettle. The system that was supposed to replace the laminated list has never worked.

Georgij spent forty minutes talking to that man about his son's military service.

He came away with an account number, and the name of the company holding it. And the four other evenings in the last fourteen months on which the same account had booked the same category of vehicle for a block.

---

The company was a corporate services provider in Jung-gu with six employees and no website worth the name.

That was the end of it. He knew what that sort of company was for. It exists so that other companies do not have to appear on dockets. Behind it there would be another one, and behind that a third. And somewhere at the end of it a person he was not going to reach through a laminated list.

The four dates were another matter.

Three of them meant nothing to him at all. A Thursday in June. A Wednesday in September. A Monday last month.

The fourth one he did not have to look up.

It was the night a woman at the back of a room three floors under an empty shopping complex in Gangnam had raised her hand and said *two-twenty*.

---

He sat with that for a while.

The car had waited outside the hotel for three hours and forty minutes with nobody in the back of it and had picked nobody up. Whoever it belonged to had not gone in.

On the fourth date, whoever it belonged to had presumably also not gone in, because a man who will not go into a ballroom does not go down into a basement either.

So there was somebody who waited outside.

That was the whole of it, and it was not very much. He had a company that existed in order not to be a name. He had four dates, and three of them were a Thursday in June, a Wednesday in September and a Monday last month, and they meant nothing whatsoever to him and probably meant a great deal to somebody.

He went down and gave Jang the account number, and the company, and all four of the dates, and thanked him for the plates.

He did not take any of it to Annie.

There was nothing there to take. He had told her in the car that he would not hand her things that merely fitted. This was a parked car and a shell company. A man who walks into that study with a shape and calls it a finding does it once and is never listened to properly again.

He wrote the three unusable dates on the inside cover of the file instead.

Three dates are three evenings, and three evenings happened somewhere, and at some point he was going to be sitting at a table with people who had been alive on all three of them.

---

By the Thursday evening the paper had got him as far as paper goes.

Nine institutions in the syndicate. Three of them large enough to have needed an internal approval, and an internal approval has a name at the bottom of it, and not one of those three publishes the name.

Two were ordinary houses. The third was a fund whose beneficial ownership ran into a chain of law firms in Singapore and stopped there. Behind Singapore, according to a filing made for an entirely different purpose in 2021, was a private equity company registered in Hong Kong.

So he had a company behind a chain behind a fund.

He had no person at all. Since the second day he had known that he was not going to get one this way. That was the third time in four weeks that the same wall had come up in front of him.

---

The restaurant was in Seongbuk-dong and had eight tables and no sign on the outside, and Chairman Woo had been going there since 1991.

"Sit," he said. "You'll have the soup. It's the only thing here that isn't trying."

"Then I'll have the soup."

"My doctor would tell you that everything on this table is going to kill me."

"Is he wrong?"

"He is not wrong. He is simply not the one eating it." Chairman Woo tucked his napkin into his collar, which no man of that fortune has done in public in forty years, and was entirely happy. "Now. You wanted the whole thing from before the harbour board."

"From the beginning. And I'd like to be boring about it, Chairman, so please don't spare me the parts you think are dull."

"Everybody wants the ten months."

"Everybody wants the ten months because it's the part with a number in it," said Georgij. "I want the two years before them."

---

It took an hour and ten minutes and he did not have to steer it once.

Woo had bought the ground in a year when nobody wanted it, on the advice of a man who was now dead.

Then he had sat on it through two administrations, one currency scare, and about eight months during which his own board had tried twice to make him sell it. He named both of the board members. He named the year. He described a meeting in 2016 in which somebody had said something to him about his father that he had not forgiven and was clearly never going to.

He talked about concrete for nine minutes and it was the best nine minutes of the evening. Georgij, who had no use for any of it, listened to all of it properly, because there was no version of the evening in which he pretended to.

Then the money.

"You couldn't fund it," said Georgij.

"I could fund it. I could not fund it and also sleep." Woo pushed his bowl away. "Eighty per cent I could carry. The last twenty was going to have to come from somewhere that wasn't a bank, because the banks in that year would lend you eighty and not one won more, and they were all correct to."

"And it took how long to find?"

"Fourteen months of nothing. Then six weeks."

There it was.

---

Georgij did not change anything at all.

He did not lean forward. He did not put his glass down. He asked the next question in exactly the tone he had used for the concrete, which is the whole of the art and takes about twenty years to learn.

"Six weeks is very fast after fourteen months of nothing."

"It always is. That's how you know it was never about the money."

"Then what was it about?"

"Somebody deciding you're worth the trouble." Woo shrugged. "After that it's paperwork."

"Who came to the room?"

"Hm?"

"When it turned. When the fourteen months became six weeks. Somebody sat down with you." Georgij had not moved since he asked it the first time. "That's the part I actually want, Chairman, and it's the part nobody ever writes down. Who was in the room."

Chairman Woo looked at him for a moment.

Then he laughed, and it was not a warm laugh, and it was the first thing all evening that had not been.

"You're still on this."

"I am."

"I told you on Saturday I wasn't going to tell you."

"You did, and I didn't ask you again on Saturday, and I've thought about very little else since." Georgij said it without any pressure anywhere in it. "I'm asking you now because we are two men eating soup and not two men in a ballroom. And because you can tell me to stop and I will stop. We can talk about concrete for another hour and I will enjoy it."

The old man sat back.

"Why do you want it?"

"Because you built something in ten months and you are proud of it and you should be, and I cannot work out why the man who made that possible has never once been photographed standing next to you." A small pause. "That is either modesty or it is not, and I have been in this business a very short time and I already know how rare modesty is."

Woo said nothing for a while.

Somebody at the next table laughed at something.

"There were three of them in the room," he said. "Two were lawyers and I have forgotten both of their names on purpose. The third one did the talking and he was about fifty and he had a Hong Kong accent when he spoke English and none at all when he spoke Korean."

"Did he give you a card?"

"He did not give anybody a card. He was not that sort." Woo turned the spoon over. "He asked me one question and I have never forgotten it, because nobody had ever asked it and everybody should. He asked me what would happen to the terminal if I died on the Tuesday."

"And you told him."

"I told him it would be sold within a year and split three ways and probably one of the three would be Sang-hoon. And he said *yes*, and then he lent me the money." Woo looked up. "That is the entire conversation, and it is the only conversation about that terminal I have ever repeated to anybody, and I am now going to eat and you are going to let me."

"Thank you, Chairman."

"Don't thank me. I have not given you a name."

"No," said Georgij. "You've given me a question."

Woo looked up.

"A name I could have had wrong. Names are written down by people who were not there." He said it evenly. "A man who sits down opposite a stranger and asks him what happens to his business if he dies on the Tuesday has asked that before, and will ask it again. Every single person he has asked will remember it for the rest of their lives." A small courteous gesture. "Nobody forgets being asked that. All I have to do now is find somebody else who has been."

"That's a strange way to look for a man."

"It's the only way left. I've spent six days looking for him in filings." Georgij turned his glass a quarter turn. "The filings end in Singapore, and behind Singapore there is a company in Hong Kong, and behind the company in Hong Kong there is nothing whatsoever, because that is what it is for."

Chairman Woo did not react to Hong Kong.

He did not react to it so completely, and so briefly, and with such perfect timing, that Georgij understood he had just been given a second thing and that the old man had not intended to give him either.

---

They ate.

At some point Woo told a story about a shipping agent in Busan in 1994 that was very funny, and Georgij laughed properly at it, which was not work.

At some point after that, over something with a great deal of salt in it, Georgij said:

"May I ask you something that isn't about you?"

"You may ask."

"If a family in your line of business had customs in a building for six days, and it was not an inspection, what would you do?"

Chairman Woo put his chopsticks down.

"Whose building?"

"I didn't say."

"You didn't have to. There were nine of them at that dinner and every single person in that room counted." He was not laughing now. "I would do nothing at all, and I would do it very visibly, and I would make certain that everybody could see me doing nothing. Which is what they did. They came, they brought the children, they laughed at the speeches."

"And after that?"

"After that it depends entirely on whether the thing in the container was theirs."

The room went on being a room.

Georgij, who had not said the word *container* and had not been going to, kept his face precisely where it had been.

"And if it wasn't theirs?"

"Then somebody has gone to a very great deal of trouble," said Chairman Woo, "and the interesting question is not who put it there. The interesting question is who is going to be standing there with an open hand in about six weeks, offering to make it all go away."

He picked his chopsticks back up.

"I have been in this trade for fifty-one years," he said. "It is always the man with the open hand."

---

Georgij went out into the street at twenty past eleven with a great deal more than he had come in with, and did not feel particularly good about any of it.

He had a man of about fifty who spoke English with a Hong Kong accent and Korean with none, and who carried no cards. The man asked people what would happen to the thing they had built if they died on a Tuesday.

He had a fund that ran into Singapore and came out in Hong Kong, and an old man who had not blinked at the word.

He had a corporate services company with six employees, and somebody who waits outside buildings, and three dates that were still worth nothing at all.

And he had a chairman who had just described the shape of the next six weeks without being asked, and who had done it because he liked him.

The car was waiting at the top of the lane.

Georgij stood on the pavement in the cold for a moment.

At some point in the next month he was going to need Chairman Woo to do something expensive.

He already knew which four minutes of this evening he would use to get it.

---

# Book One · Chapter 14: In the same size type
*Lot Fourteen* · Version 7.6 · EN

## Day Thirty-One · Monday 3 November

He closed the faces with photographers.

It should have occurred to him in the ballroom and it had not, which annoyed him for two days. Four consecutive years of the same evening, two firms covering all four, and neither of them had ever deleted anything, because no photographer in the history of the trade has ever deleted anything.

He bought a licence for the whole of that year's set from the smaller of the two firms. An adviser buying pictures of an event his employer had attended, which is a boring and entirely ordinary thing to do.

Two thousand one hundred and six frames.

Then he laid them against the seating plan, and against the account the hotel had raised for the wine, and against four years of the same faces standing in the same clusters.

By the second evening he had names on all nine of the Kims.

The quiet woman at the back of the group was Kim Ye-rin. Fifty-four. No seat on the board of anything. No signature on any filing. No title in any document he could find.

And in sixteen of the twenty-three frames in which the family appeared, she was standing where she could see the doors.

The one who kept fetching drinks was Kim Do-yun.

---

He had been wrong about Kim Do-yun and it took him about forty seconds with the photographs to find out.

In the ballroom he had read it as weakness. A man who goes twice to the bar in four minutes, and laughs a quarter of a beat late, is a man who is not comfortable in the group he is standing in.

Georgij had put him at the top of the pile on that basis, and had been rather pleased with himself for it.

The frames said otherwise.

Both of his routes to the bar took him past other people. Not the shortest way and not the same way twice. On the first trip he passed within a metre of two men from a shipping line. There was a frame of it, taken for somebody else entirely, in which he stood behind them with a glass in each hand and his head not quite turned.

On the second trip he went round the long side, past a group of four, and stopped for nine seconds to say something to a waiter that took about three.

And the laugh that came a quarter of a beat late came late because he was listening to something happening two metres behind him.

Georgij sat with that for a while.

He had been wrong about a man's character on four seconds of evidence, in a room he had spent three weeks preparing for.

The only reason he had found out at all was that somebody else had happened to point a camera in the right direction.

It was the second time in a fortnight that his reading of a person had been handed back to him with a correction on it.

He wrote Kim Do-yun's name at the top of a clean page and did not put anything under it.

---

## Day Thirty-Three · Wednesday 5 November

Hanseong came out of the wrong drawer.

He had been mapping shipping routes for ten days, because the Kims' logistics arm ran seven of them. He wanted to know which were profitable, which were sentimental and which existed because somebody's cousin lived in a port town.

Route work means looking at everybody else on the same water.

And on the tenth day of it, working down a list of operators who held berths on the same two lanes, he found a group that owned four routes and could not pay for them.

Hanseong.

Six months of capital going out. A board that had split into two halves that no longer sat in the same meetings, and had stopped pretending otherwise in March. Two separate rumours about money missing at a subsidiary, neither of them printed anywhere, both of them repeated by people who had no reason to talk to each other.

And underneath all of it, four shipping routes with real berths and real slots and a genuine, unambiguous value.

Georgij read the whole thing twice and then sat very still.

Then he began to write.

---

## Day Thirty-Four · Thursday 6 November

He went to her on the second day of the writing, with eight pages of it done and nothing in his hands.

He had thought about finishing it first and had decided against it inside about a minute. A man who arrives with nineteen finished pages is a man asking for a signature, and a man asking for a signature has already made the decision and is dressing it as a question.

He put eight pages on her desk. No cover, no title, corrections in the margins in his own hand.

"Hanseong," he said. "Four shipping routes, real berths, real slots. Six months of capital going out of the door. A board that split in March and has stopped pretending it hasn't. Two separate rumours about money missing at a subsidiary, from two mouths that have no reason to know each other."

"And it's all true."

"Every line of it, and where it isn't a fact it says what it is." He stayed where he was, at the corner of the desk, with his hands behind his back. "I want to give it to Park Sang-hoon on the eighteenth."

Annie turned the first page over and did not read it.

"Say the rest."

"He will not tell me I am wrong about it. He does not correct people with sentences." Georgij said it flatly and without any decoration anywhere. "He will take the routes. He will take them in the next four to six weeks, because a split board sells cheaply and sells fast. He will have to put real money against them, and he will have to put it in early enough that he still believes it was his own idea."

"How much?"

"Between four hundred and six hundred billion for control, and it will not be the purchase that costs him. It will be what comes after." He took his hands out from behind his back. "Four routes with berths he cannot let go idle, in a group with no working board. He will spend the first three months finding out that nobody at Hanseong can sign anything, and while he is finding that out he will be paying slot fees on lanes that are running at sixty per cent."

"And Incheon?"

"And on the day he needs to be liquid enough to take a logistics company off a family under customs investigation, he will be a man with four routes and a fight."

"That doesn't stop him."

"No, Mistress. On its own it stops nothing at all." He said it immediately, because it was the correct objection and because she had reached it in about a second. "It buys weeks. Weeks are the only thing I can buy him out of."

"Then say the whole shape of it."

"There are three things and none of them works alone."

He put one finger on the desk.

"The first is money. What the Kims need is a balance sheet that survives an investigation. You put money behind them and you take back shares, security and a veto over routes. They keep the name. You get the control, and they will be told that in those words, by me, before anybody signs anything."

A second finger.

"The second is time, and that is Hanseong. He cannot move quickly and buy four routes in the same quarter, and he will buy the routes, because he cannot help it."

"And the third?"

"The third is the only one that is urgent, and it is the one nobody has mentioned in this house." A third finger. "Customs has been sitting in that building for eighteen days. Their problem this month has nothing to do with who owns them. Their customers are leaving. Three cancellations last week, which Hana had on a terrace and did not think was the interesting part."

He put one hand flat.

"And you are one of the customers."

Annie did not say anything to that, because there was nothing in it she did not know.

"Six of your plants make specialty chemicals and coatings, and the two that matter make the intermediates that everything else in the group is built on." He said it in exactly the tone he had used for everything else. "Those intermediates arrive on a schedule, through four ports, and two of the routes that carry them exist because the Kims hold permits that took eleven years to get and that nobody is issuing again."

"Go on."

"A reactor that stops does not simply start again on Thursday." He put one finger down and left it there. "You lose the batch in it. You lose the two behind it. A plant that has been cold for a week is four weeks away from being useful again, Underneath every one of your supply contracts there is a delivery clause with a number in it. The number is not there to be reasonable."

"You have read my contracts."

"I have read the eleven that are public, and I have read what your customers file, which is more useful. A man tells you a great deal more about his supplier when he is explaining himself to his own shareholders. If that company goes to Park Sang-hoon, then in eighteen months a man who has spent this year buying his competitors decides what arrives at your plants and on which Tuesday. He would tell you, politely, once a quarter, what it costs that year."

"Yes," said Annie.

"I have known that since my fourth day in your house and it is the reason I have never once asked you why you care about a shipping family."

"And customers come back for money."

"Customers never come back for money. They come back because somebody they trust is standing next to you." He let that sit. "Which is Chairman Woo. Fifty-one years in that trade, a terminal at Yeongjong, and the last man in this city anybody would suspect of being anywhere he had not thought about first. If he puts his name next to theirs, on anything, for any reason, the cancellations stop that week."

"And why would he?"

"Because twice this year somebody has tried to buy his terminal and would not say who was behind him." Georgij said it flatly. "And because he told me at dinner that if he died on a Tuesday, his terminal would be sold within a year and split three ways, and one of the three would be Sang-hoon."

Annie sat back.

"You have not told me the part I asked for."

"No, Mistress."

"Then tell me now."

"I am going to hand a man a document in which every single word is true," said Georgij, "so that he ruins himself on it."

And then he smiled.

It was not one he had built. It came up slowly, from somewhere behind the courtesy, and it took its time, and it was entirely without cruelty and entirely without apology, and it was the face of a man who has found work that suits him.

It was also the first time in thirty-four days that he had aimed anything of the kind at her.

He was aware of it about a second after it was on him, and he did not put it away. Putting it away would have been a second lie in a room where he had just refused the first one.

Annie looked at him for rather longer than a moment.

"Does it work because it is true?"

"Yes. It works for no other reason. He has been lied to by professionals for fifteen years and he is very good at it. He has almost certainly never in his life been handed a paper that says *this is a guess* in the middle of it."

"Could you have written it if it had not been true?"

There it was.

"I could have written something," said Georgij. "It would have been a different document with a hole in it somewhere, because they always have one. He would have found it in an hour and I would have found out about it in a year." He did not soften it. "But that is not the answer you asked for. The answer you asked for is no. I would not have written it."

"Because you don't lie."

"Because I don't lie, and because I would like that to go on being a thing I do not do, and because I have noticed that this is the first time it has ever been the more dangerous option."

Annie did not say anything to that at all.

She read the eight pages, properly, from the top, which took her four minutes, while he stood at the corner of the desk and did not fill any of it.

Then she squared them and put them down.

"Finish it," she said.

---

"There's a second thing."

"Go on."

"I need Hana in a room, and my preference is that she comes here."

"You want her here," said Annie.

"I want you to decide whether she comes here. Those are different and I'd like to give you the second one." Georgij stood at the corner of the desk with his hands behind his back. "May I put the cost of it in front of you first?"

"Go on."

"If she comes to this house, thirty people know that Hana had dinner here. Mrs Seo, who I would trust with a great deal. Mr Baek and two of his kitchen. Ji-won and Eun-ju. Fourteen security, of whom Jang has been here a month and Bae I would be sorry to be wrong about." He said it flatly, as a list. "There are four of those thirty I cannot read at all. One of them is a gardener who has not spoken to me in five weeks, and all that tells me is that he does not like what I am."

"And outside?"

"Outside it is fewer people and worse ones. A restaurant, a driver, a car standing somewhere, and a reason. A meeting in a restaurant needs a reason, and a reason can be wrong, and when a reason is wrong somebody goes looking for the right one." A small pause. "In this house it needs nothing. Two women who have known each other for twelve years had dinner. That explains itself and nobody has to do any work to believe it."

"You've already decided."

"I've already worked it out. That isn't the same thing, and it isn't mine to decide, and I have no wish for it to become mine."

Annie looked at him for a moment.

"There is one more thing on your list and you have not said it."

"No."

"Say it."

"At the gala you and she did not exchange one word until eleven o’clock," said Georgij. "Both of you had arranged that in advance. Anybody who was watching that room and can count has that. If she comes up this drive on Thursday, then whatever the two of you were not doing at the Grand Hotel, you have stopped not doing."

"Yes."

"That's the whole of the cost."

"Next Thursday," said Annie.

---

# Book One · Chapter 15: Four thousand two hundred
*Lot Fourteen* · Version 2.7 · EN

## Day Forty-One · Thursday 13 November

Hana came at seven and did not bring anybody with her.

She stopped in the hall for a moment beside the stone shelf with the shallow bowl on it, the way people do in a house they have been in before. Then she looked at Georgij and said:

"You didn't do what I told you to do."

"No."

"And you're not going to apologise for it."

"No."

"Good," said Hana, and gave him her coat.

---

They ate in the small room off the long one. Mrs Seo had laid it for three without being told how to lay it for three.

Mr Baek sent up something with a great deal of salt in it, which Georgij suspected was aimed at somebody other than him.

They talked about nothing at all until the plates went.

Then Annie put both hands on the table.

"Everything," she said. "In whatever order it comes."

---

He gave them the room first, because the room was what they did not have.

The Kims first, with names on the faces, because that was the piece that had been missing since the gala. Kim Ye-rin, fifty-four, no seat and no signature and no title, and in sixteen frames out of twenty-three standing where she could see the doors.

"I can show you why I think it. I cannot prove one word of it," he said. "There are seventeen frames in which somebody says something and looks at her in the middle of the sentence before going on. Two different people, four years apart, neither of them aware they are doing it. None of that proves anything, and it is all I have."

Hana laughed out loud.

"Ye-rin. Of course it is Ye-rin." She was delighted in a way that was not about Georgij. "Her brother has the title. She has the family."

"Then tell me the part I can't see."

"She holds one point four per cent." Hana said it flatly, the way you read out a bus number. "Her brother has eleven. Two cousins have nine and seven, a sister-in-law has six, and there are two nephews with four each, and nobody at that end has the faintest idea what a berth is worth."

"Forty-one across six people."

"Forty-one. Forty-two point four with hers, and not one of those six has voted against her since 2009." She turned her glass. "She does the family money. She has done it since the father died. Every one of them has been carried by her at some point, and none of them can read a set of accounts, and every single one of them telephones her before they sign anything at all."

"So she never has to sign."

"In twenty years nobody has been able to make that woman sign anything," said Hana, "because she has never once needed to. She tells other people what to sign."

"Then she is the one I have to be in a room with."

"She is the only one. She will not come to a room, and I have tried."

"Could you get me into one?"

Hana put her glass down.

"Yes," she said. "Not quickly and not twice. Once."

"Then I'd like to know what it costs."

"You are asking me at the table."

"Asking at the table costs us both less than my finding it out in March."

Hana looked at Annie. Annie gave her nothing at all to work with, and after a moment Hana turned back.

"I own warehouse space at Incheon. Sixteen thousand square metres on the eastern side, in my own name, bought over eleven years." She said it in exactly the voice she had used about the ceiling at the Grand Hotel. "All of its value sits on the designation, and I spent last year getting the designation."

"Yes," said Georgij.

Hana stopped.

"You knew."

"Since Tuesday." He put his hands flat on the cloth. "I have been reading the eastern parcel for a fortnight because Incheon is the whole of this, and the first thing you do with a parcel is find out who owns it. There are forty-one holdings on that side. Yours is the fourth largest and it stands in your own name, which nobody does while they are hiding something."

"And you have sat on it since Tuesday."

"A name on a plot tells you nothing about what somebody means to do with it. You could have owned it since 2013 and thought about it twice a year." He left his hands where he had put them. "So I put it with the other things I cannot read yet, and I waited to see whether you would say it at this table."

"And if I hadn't?"

"Then I would have asked you at this table, in front of her, in about ten minutes."

Annie put her glass down.

"Two days," she said. "The study is at the end of a corridor you walk along four times a day."

"Yes, Mistress."

"So say why you didn't."

"Because a piece of land with no explanation attached is worth less to you than the same piece of land with her explanation attached. I could not have the second one without waiting for it." He did not look away from either of them. "And because on Tuesday all I had was a name in a register. I have told you twice that I would not bring you a thing that merely fits, and on Tuesday that was the whole of what it was."

"You have."

Annie did not tell him he had read it wrongly, and did not tell him he had read it correctly either.

---

"Something closed while you were talking that has been open for three weeks," said Georgij. "I will say it now, before we go on."

"Go on."

"On the terrace at the Grand Hotel you told a minister that the approach on the eastern parcel was a discourtesy rather than a threat." He said it plainly. "Nobody is insulted on somebody else's behalf. A man is insulted by a price when the price was offered to him."

Hana looked at him for a moment.

"So the approach was to you."

"The approach was to me."

"You didn't say so on the terrace."

"You were ten minutes old to me on that terrace," said Hana, "and you were standing at my elbow being enchanting at a minister."

"That's fair."

"What I want is this." She put two fingers on the cloth. "If the Kims come out of the other side of this with their logistics and Annie's money behind them, they take my space. Long leases, twenty years, fixed, signed at the same time as everything else and not afterwards on a promise."

"And then you have a tenant instead of a predator," said Annie.

"Then I have a tenant whose ownership I can read, and whose veto sits with you." Hana shrugged. "I am not doing this out of affection for anybody at this table."

"No," said Annie. "That's why I'm still listening."

"May I ask something that is not about Incheon?"

"You may."

"On the floor at the Grand Hotel you told me that in about a month the two of you were going to need each other rather badly." Georgij looked at her. "It has been nineteen days. Why?"

Hana did not answer straight away.

"Because I knew what was in the consultation and she did not," she said. "And because she has money and I have twenty years of telephone numbers, and neither of those is worth anything on its own against a man who buys people one at a time." She turned the glass. "I was going to have to come up this drive whether I liked it or not. I would have chosen March."

"Then why now?"

"Because of him." She said it to Annie and not to him. "I told him on that floor that I would have to know whether he was the strongest thing you owned or the softest, and that I did not know yet." A pause. "He has spent this evening handing me things before I asked for them. That is not the softest."

Annie did not look at either of them.

"No," she said. "It isn't."

"And Do-yun," said Georgij.

He put it down plainly, because there was no way to put it down otherwise.

"I read him as the weak one at the gala. He is not the weak one. He was working that ballroom while I watched him and I mistook it for nerves, and I found out from a photograph taken for somebody else. You are both getting that from me tonight instead of in six weeks, when it would cost something."

Annie did not say anything.

Hana looked at him with her chin down for a moment, and then said, in a completely different voice from the one she had been using:

"That's twice."

"Twice?"

"You told me on the floor at the Grand Hotel that a tool you have used once should be used carefully. I told you that. And now you have handed me a mistake before I asked for one." She turned her glass. "Annie, he does this."

"He does," said Annie.

---

Then the rest of it.

The two directors at the Paradise in Busan, one at a time, four hundred kilometres from where they worked, a month after their company had already been sold. The pattern of a man who buys a company and then goes on working the people who stayed in it.

"That's Sang-hoon," said Hana. "That is exactly Sang-hoon. He never buys anything once."

"No," said Georgij, "and now I know why he cannot."

He put both hands on the cloth.

"There is no majority to buy. There is one point four per cent and forty per cent scattered across six relatives who cannot read a balance sheet and who all telephone the same woman. You cannot make an offer for that. There is nobody to make it to." He looked up. "So he is not buying a company. He is turning people, one at a time, in hotels four hundred kilometres from where they work, and he has been doing it for at least a year, and he already has one of them."

"Do-yun."

"Do-yun."

The hire car in the ramp. Three hours forty, nobody in the back, nobody collected. A corporate services company in Jung-gu with six employees. Four block bookings in fourteen months on the same account.

"Somebody who waits outside buildings," said Georgij.

"Give me the dates," said Hana.

He gave her the three that meant nothing to him. He gave her the fourth. Because it was on the same page and because he had told Annie in a car that he would not sort things before handing them over.

Annie's face did nothing at all.

Hana went through them twice.

"June, I don't have. September I don't have." She stopped at the third. "The Monday last month is the night before the ministry published the consultation on the eastern parcel. Which could be nothing. Consultations leak for a week beforehand and everybody knows a day early."

"And the fourth?"

"The fourth is nothing to me at all," said Hana, and looked up. "Should it be?"

There was about a second and a half in which Georgij could have said several things.

"It's the night I was bought," he said.

---

Mr Baek's salt cooled on the table. Somewhere below them a door closed and somebody went along a passage.

Hana put the paper down.

"Then somebody sat outside that building for the whole of it," she said, "and did not go in, and three weeks later sat outside a hotel for three hours and forty minutes and did not go in."

"Yes."

"And you have a shell company with six employees."

"Yes."

"That is not enough to do anything with."

"No. It is a parked car and a company that exists in order not to be a name." Georgij put the page flat. "Jang has had all four dates since the Friday and they have been worth precisely nothing since. There has never been anybody in a room with me who might have been able to place the other three."

He looked at the two of them.

"You are the first two people who have been."

Annie said nothing whatsoever.

She drank some water and put the glass down without a sound, and she had had the name of the man who stood behind the man in the second row since Georgij's first week in that house.

---

"One more thing, and I am going to say it in front of you rather than do it quietly," said Georgij.

Hana waited.

"I have the first section on those sixteen thousand square metres. Owner, dates, area. I have not pulled the second."

"Why not?"

"Because you pull the second section on somebody when you are about to rely on them, and until an hour ago I was not." He said it entirely without apology. "Now I am. On Monday I will look at what is charged against that land and who holds the paper."

He looked at Hana.

"I would do the same for anybody who put a number on this table, including her. I have already done it for two of the Kims."

"Most people would have simply done it."

"They would, and on Monday they would be holding exactly the same pages I will be holding." He put one finger down and left it there. "What they would not have is you knowing they had them, and that is the whole of the difference."

Hana laughed, and it was the real one, the one she had given him once before on a cold terrace.

Georgij gave her a face back that was not built for anything, and did not take it off until she had finished.

"None of my business how you work," she said. "Pull it."

---

Then he put the document on the table.

It had taken him six days and it ran to nineteen pages and it was the best thing he had produced since he came into the house.

Every figure in it was published. Every claim in it was true. Where something was a rumour it said so, and said how many mouths it had come through. Where he was guessing it said *this is a guess*, in the same size type as everything else. Which is a thing almost nobody does, because it makes a document look weaker and makes it worth a great deal more.

It was the most honest piece of work he had ever done and he had written all of it for one reader.

He gave them the whole of it in eight minutes. Six months of outflow, a board in two halves since March, two rumours from two unconnected mouths, and four shipping routes with real berths that were worth what they were worth regardless of any of it.

"Everything in there is true," he said. "There is nothing in it I have shaded. Where it is a rumour it says so and it says how many people it came through. Where I am guessing it says *this is a guess*."

"And what is it for?" said Annie.

"It's for Park Sang-hoon," said Georgij. "I am going to give it to him on the eighteenth and ask him where my analysis is wrong. He knows this trade and I do not, and a man like that does not correct anybody with words."

"He corrects you by doing it better."

"He corrects you by doing it. He will not tell me I am wrong about Hanseong. He will show me." He put one hand flat on the document. "And then his money is in four shipping routes and a board that hates itself, at the precise moment when he needs to be liquid enough to take a logistics company away from a family under customs investigation."

Hana was very still.

"That's good," she said.

"It is an apple," said Georgij. "Sweet, full of juice, and there is not a mark on the skin of it anywhere, because I have taken a great deal of trouble over the outside."

Hana put her glass down.

"The poison is that all of it is true. If I had shaded one line he would have found it in an afternoon, and I would never have got near him again."

"A poisoned apple," said Hana.

"Yes. And he is going to enjoy every mouthful of it."

"There is a part of it that nobody at this table has said," said Georgij. "I am going to say it now, because in March somebody who is not sitting here is going to say it for us."

"Go on."

"There is a second half to that veto, and we have spent this evening calling it safety. If you hold the routes through Incheon, your own intermediates arrive on the day they are supposed to arrive."

"Yes."

"So do everybody else's."

He let that stand for a moment before he filled it in.

"Four other groups in your business come in on those same two lanes, on those same permits, because there are no others. I have their filings. Kyeongil takes about a fifth of one lane. The Nam operation at Ulsan takes more than that and has nothing else. Two smaller ones split what is left." He did not look away from her. "A veto over routes decides which container goes on which sailing when a sailing is full, and sailings on those lanes are full nine months of the year."

Hana had stopped moving.

"You would not have to close anything," said Georgij. "It would be enough that you could. Two weeks late in that trade is a broken contract and a cold reactor, and you would be the group whose cargo was already loaded, and somebody else would do the explaining."

"And they would work that out."

"By the second quarter. Nobody would ever say it out loud and nobody would ever have to, and the price of everything they buy from you would settle at whatever you felt like, for as long as you held the paper."

"That is what I am buying," said Annie. "Yes."

"I know."

Nobody supplied a word for it, and nobody looked as though they were looking for one.

"And the third thing," said Annie.

"It is better than good, and it has a price, and I am going to put the price on this table before anybody agrees to anything."

"Then put it on the table."

"Hanseong has four thousand two hundred employees," said Georgij.

---

Nobody spoke for a moment.

"He will not close it," he went on, in exactly the same voice. "Men like that do not close things, they hold them and bleed them and sell the parts that move. So it will not be four thousand two hundred. It will be perhaps a third of them inside two years, and it would probably have been a fifth of them anyway, because that group is dying and has been dying since before I could find it."

"Probably," said Annie.

"Probably. I am not going to stand in your dining room and tell you the number I am adding is zero, because I don't know that and neither do you." He stopped. "Everything in that document is true, and I wrote all nineteen pages of it in order to hurt a man with it. Both of those are mine and I intend to carry both."

Annie looked at the pages.

"Will he take it?" she said, to Hana.

Hana did not answer straight away, which Georgij noticed and was glad of.

"He will take it," she said at last. "It is honest, and nobody has handed that man an honest document in fifteen years. He will spend the first hour looking for the trick in it and the second hour deciding there isn't one." She looked across at Georgij. "That is what will finish him. Not the document. The hour."

"Yes."

"Do you know what you are, at that table?"

"I'm the only thing at it that has already been sold," said Georgij, "and he has already asked me what I went for."

---

Hana put both hands on the table.

"Then I want it on paper," she said.

Annie looked at her.

"Tonight there is nothing to put on paper. There is no company, no money in it, no signature from any Kim, and Ye-rin does not know this table exists."

"I know what a conditional agreement is, and so do you." Hana said it entirely pleasantly. "A lease, signed now, that takes effect on the day your capital goes into that logistics arm. Twenty years, rent fixed, the buildings named. If the money has not gone in by the end of March it lapses and none of us ever mentions it again."

"That costs me nothing today."

"That is the point," said Hana. "It costs you nothing today and everything in April. A woman who intends to pay signs it in ten minutes."

Nobody said anything for a moment.

"May I put the other side of it down," said Georgij, "since neither of you is going to ask me to."

"Put it down."

"That document will exist." He said it in exactly the tone he had used for the thirty people in this house. "A lease is drawn by somebody. It is typed by somebody. It sits in a firm, and if it is ever registered it sits somewhere worse than a firm, and every one of those people is a person who can be asked a question."

"And what does it say to whoever asks?"

"It says that in November two women had an arrangement about Incheon, four months before anything happened, and that one of them was already sure enough of the other to write down what the rent would be." He looked at Annie. "That will not kill anybody. It gives away a great deal more than a handshake does, and if I told you which of the two carried the worse risk I would be inventing it, because I do not know."

"So you would advise against it," said Hana.

"No. I would advise you both to know what you are buying with it, which is what I have just done." He sat back. "The decision belongs to the two of you, in halves, and I have no standing in either of them."

Annie was quiet for what felt like a considerable time.

"Draw it," she said.

"You'll sign it?"

"I will sign it on Monday, in front of you, and you will not have to ask me twice." Annie's face did not move anywhere. "You want to know why I am agreeing this quickly."

"I know why. You need the ministry quiet and you need Ye-rin, and I am the only door to either." Hana turned her glass once. "I want you to know why I am asking."

"Go on."

"Because I have sat at this table for three hours and watched the two of you decide to ruin a man with nineteen pages in which every word is true." She said it without any heat at all. "You have both been extremely straight with me all evening. I believe every single thing either of you has said. And I would be a fool to be the only person in this room without a piece of paper."

Georgij inclined his head, and did not say anything, because there was nothing to say to that which would not have been an argument against her being right.

"Yes," said Annie. "You would."

---

They finished at ten past eleven.

Hana took her coat herself, from the hall, and stopped for a moment beside the stone shelf with the shallow bowl on it, in the way of somebody who has been in a house before.

"That was worth the drive," she said, to nobody in particular.

Then, to Georgij, at the door:

"You are in."

"I'm sorry?"

"At the Grand Hotel I told you I did not know yet whether you were the strongest thing she owns or the softest, and that I wanted you to take that away with you." She put her gloves on. "You have been carrying it for nineteen days. You can put it down."

"And which is it?"

"You told me tonight that you were wrong about a man's character and you told me before I asked you anything," said Hana. "The softest thing in this house would not have done that."

She went out to her car.

---

"The eighteenth," said Annie, when the door had closed.

"Yes, Mistress."

"Take Jang's people to the door and not through it. Come home afterwards and not in the morning."

"Yes."

"And Georgij."

"Mistress."

"Four thousand two hundred," said Annie. "Do not lose that figure. I am going to ask you for it again in a year."

---

# Book One · Chapter 16: Where the walls are
*Lot Fourteen* · Version 1.16 · EN

*Day 46 · Tuesday 18 November*

The office was in Yeouido on the ninth floor of a building Park Sang-hoon had bought in 2011 and had never bothered to put his name on.

There was a room off it with a table for six in it and two places laid at one end. That told Georgij before anybody had said a word. Nobody else was coming, and nobody was going to be taking notes.

Jang's two men came as far as the lifts and no further.

That had been Annie's instruction and Georgij had intended to follow it anyway, because a man who brings people through a door into a room laid for two has said something about the evening before he sits down.

---

Sang-hoon was already there.

He did not get up. He was in a chair with his back three quarters to the window and the city behind him, and he indicated the seat opposite with two fingers, and the seat opposite had the window in its eyes.

"Sit down."

Georgij sat down.

The food came almost at once, which meant it had been ordered before he arrived, which meant the length of the evening had been decided by somebody else, which was fine.

He ate.

He ate properly and without hurrying and he said so when it was good, and at one point he asked what the thing with the perilla in it was called and wrote nothing down and remembered it. A man who does not eat at another man's table has told him that he does not trust the table. Georgij had spent seventeen years in houses where that was the cheapest information you could give away.

He drank one glass and did not refuse the second and did not finish it.

---

"You ask people to guide you," said Sang-hoon.

Twelve minutes in. Before the second dish.

"I do."

"Hong. And a man from Land and Infrastructure who talks to my people about other things." He said it the way a man reads out a delivery note. "Two in one evening, in almost the same words."

"Three, if you count tonight." Georgij put his chopsticks down. "I asked you for this dinner in order to be told how a man takes something that isn't his. That is the same request in a better suit."

Something happened at the corner of Sang-hoon's mouth.

"Most people would have said it was a coincidence."

"It isn't one. It is the only tool I own that costs nothing and works on men who have thirty years on me." He said it flatly. "I used it twice in one evening because it worked the first time. It has now been reported to you by two separate people, which is roughly what I deserved."

"You could have stopped using it."

"I have not stopped, because I am actually asking," said Georgij. "That is the whole of it. It is why it works and it is why it looks like something."

---

"Before we go any further," he said, "I would like to tell you how I am going to conduct this evening, because it will save us both a great deal of time."

"Go on."

"Three things." He put both hands on the table. "The first is that I will not say anything to you that is untrue. Not this evening and not afterwards. If I am guessing I will say I am guessing."

"Everybody says that."

"Yes. The second is what makes it worth anything." His hands had not moved off the table. "There are things I am not permitted to say. When we reach one, I will tell you that we have reached one. I will not change the subject, and I will not give you a smaller answer and let you think it was the whole of it."

Park Sang-hoon looked at him for the first time since he had sat down.

"You understand what that gives me."

"It tells you where the walls are. Yes."

"Most men prefer that you never find the wall at all."
