Kim Ye-rin signed last, and she signed on page eleven, and it took her about four seconds.

Nobody in that room had ever watched her sign anything.

Mr Chae witnessed each of them in turn, and initialled the counterpart, and said the words about the counterpart being one instrument, and closed his case, and that was the whole of the noise he made all day.

---

Kim Do-yun came over to Georgij at the door while the others were standing about the way people do.

"You said something in November about who would be at whose door."

"I said it to somebody else."

"She told me on Saturday." Do-yun had his coat over his arm. "You said the day this was written down and directed, my employer had lost, and I would be at my uncle's door inside a fortnight offering to be useful."

"I did."

"It is six days."

"I know," said Georgij. "I would have said a fortnight to be safe. You are quicker than I gave you credit for, and that is the second time."

---

Do-yun did something with his mouth that was almost a smile again.

"He telephoned me on Friday morning about the photograph. He wanted to know what my uncle had been doing standing next to that old man." He put his coat on. "I have worked for that man for four years and he has never once asked me anything about my own family. On Friday he asked me twice in one call."

"And you told him."

"I told him what is in two newspapers." Do-yun looked at the table where the pages were. "There is nothing else to tell him. That is the part I have been sitting with all afternoon."

"There is nothing to announce," said Georgij.

"No." A pause. "That is a very good sentence and I am going to be using it for the rest of my life."

---

The house emptied.

Sung-ho shook his hand at the door and held it about a second longer than the handshake needed, which from that man was a great deal, and said nothing whatever, which was also correct.

Mr Ku had the car turned round already.

---

Kim Ye-rin was standing in the drive with her coat on and her driver twenty metres away with the door open, and she was not going anywhere yet.

"Mr Georgij."

"Mrs Kim."

"On the terrace you told me I would become the place to press." She had her gloves in one hand and had not put them on. "You did not tell me the other thing."

---

He waited.

"That they would all turn round." She was looking at the house and not at him. "I have kept that from happening in front of a stranger for twenty years. It took my cousin nine seconds this afternoon."

"I know. I watched it."

"You watched it and you did not say anything, and there was something to say." She turned. "Why not."

"Because you would have heard a man being clever about the worst minute of your afternoon," said Georgij, "and you would have been right."

---

Kim Ye-rin put her gloves on, one and then the other, and took her time about it.

"You will send the January money through my brother's office and you will never once send anything through me. If a document comes to my house I will send it back unopened, and I will not explain why, and you will not ask."

"No."

"Good." She went two steps towards the car and stopped. "It was worth it. I want you to hear me say that today, and not in March when you have started to wonder."

Then she got into the car, and the car went down the hill, and that was the whole of it.

---

# Book One · Chapter 33: That is five
*Lot Fourteen* · Version 1.11 · EN

## Day Eighty-Seven · Monday 29 December

He came up the drive.

That was the first thing about it, and Georgij thought it was the whole of it until he was proved wrong about an hour later. In November this man had gone over the east wall, at a corner where a camera turns away for eleven seconds. He had walked into a lit room without being announced, and he had been correct about every part of it.

On the twenty-ninth of December he telephoned at half past ten, was given two o'clock, and arrived at four minutes to.

The gate opened for him. Jang was in the hall and did not need to be.

Kim Do-yun was in the car and stayed in it.

In November there had been a man in that seat too, and nobody had got out of it then either.

---

Annie had Georgij in the room before Sang-hoon was out of the car.

She did not say why and there was no need. In November the man had asked his question in front of him on purpose, and had said so, and had been right that asking it any other way would have been worse.

Whatever was coming was going to be said in front of the same three people as last time.

---

They gave him twenty minutes of nothing at all first, because that is how it is done.

The weather. A ferry company in Mokpo that had gone under on Christmas Eve and had been going under since June. A man they both disliked, who had become chairman of something.

Park Sang-hoon was entirely pleasant and his coat was over the back of a chair he had not been invited to use, and Georgij watched him not look at the collar even once, which took work.

---

Then he put his cup down.

"I am going to make you an offer," he said to Annie, "and it is a serious one, and I have been three weeks deciding whether to come and say it out loud."

"Then say it out loud."

"Two billion two hundred million won." He did not put anything around it. "For his contract, his release, his papers, whatever the correct word is in whatever arrangement you have. Today, in one payment."

---

The room was very quiet.

"He told me what you paid, in November, over dinner, without being asked twice," said Sang-hoon. "I asked him whether it was a yearly figure, which is the stupidest thing I have said out loud in some years, and he let me finish the question." He was entirely level about it. "That is ten times. In thirty-one years I have never watched anything amortise like that, and I am offering you the whole of it in an afternoon."

He was not looking at Georgij at all, which was also correct.

"I want to be clear that this is not a compliment to him. It is an accounting judgement. I have run the number twice and I would run it a third time and get the same answer."

---

Annie did not move.

Her hands were on the arms of the chair where they had been for twenty minutes, and they stayed there, and they went nowhere near the pocket of anything.

Georgij was aware of that the way you are aware of a change in air pressure.

In November her hand had been in her jacket before the question finished. Today the jacket was on the back of a chair in another room. She had put it there herself at half past one, and he had watched her do it and had thought nothing about it.

---

Nobody said anything.

Sang-hoon waited, because he had asked a woman a question and she had not answered, and that is her right.

Annie let it go on long enough that it stopped being a pause and became something else, and then she turned her head, about fifteen degrees, and looked at Georgij.

That was all she did.

---

Georgij stood up.

"Mistress."

"Yes."

"In about a minute I am going to break something you told me in a car in November." He was already turning while he said it. "I am telling you now so that it is not a thing you watch happen."

Annie did not answer him and she did not stop him.

---

Park Sang-hoon had not moved since he put the number down. He had let the silence run for a full minute without once trying to fill it, which is a thing very few men can do and none of them by accident.

"Chairman Park."

"Mr Georgij."

"I would like to ask you for guidance."

---

Sang-hoon did not smile.

He sat back in the chair he had not been offered, and put one hand on each knee, and something went across his face and did not stay.

"That is five."

"It is five."

"Two of them are mine. Three, now." He put his cup back on the saucer without having drunk from it. "In November I told you that you could have stopped using it."

"You did."

"And you told me that you had not stopped because you were actually asking, and that this was the whole of it, and that it was why the thing worked."

"I did."

---

"Are you actually asking?"

"No," said Georgij.

---

The house made no noise whatever for about two seconds.

"That is the first untrue thing you have ever built out of me," said Sang-hoon, "and you have just told me it is untrue before you use it, which means it is not untrue, which means you have thought about this for some time."

"Thirty-seven days."

"Then it will not work again."

"It will not work again on anybody, and it will not work on you ever." Georgij said it plainly. "It was the only thing I own that costs nothing, and after this afternoon nobody will take it from me, because you will tell four people and every one of them will tell four more. I am spending the whole of it here, once, and I am doing it knowingly."

---

"Then spend it," said Park Sang-hoon.

---

"Please guide me," said Georgij.

"How does a man let go of a sweet, sweet, beautiful, poisoned apple that he has already bitten into? Chewed, and found absolutely tasty?"

He did not raise his voice anywhere in it.

"Please, Chairman, tell me. I prepared this apple to look and to taste very delicious."

---

Nobody in that room said anything at all.

Jang, in the hall, would say afterwards that he heard nothing and had not been listening, and both halves of that were untrue and he was never asked again.

Park Sang-hoon sat entirely still with one hand on each knee for what Georgij counted at eleven seconds.

Somewhere in the fifth of them, Georgij began to smile.

He noticed it the way you notice that it has started raining. It was in the face before it was anywhere else, and then it went on arriving after it should have been finished.

He had no name for this one.

Annie was behind him and could not see his face and would not need to, and he did not stop.

---

Then Park Sang-hoon laughed.

It was one syllable and it was not a pleasant sound and it was completely genuine, and it was the first thing that man had done in three months that had not been decided in advance.

"Hanseong."

"Hanseong."

"Every line of it was true."

"Every line of it was true," said Georgij, "and you checked most of it yourself, and you will not find a bad page in there in March, because there is not one."

---

Sang-hoon nodded slowly, twice, at nothing in the middle of the room.

"The routes are good. The berths are good. The fight is winnable and I am going to win it." He was working out loud and not defending anything. "It takes a quarter and it takes all of it, and there is not one thing in this world I can do about the calendar."

"No."

"December to March."

"December to March."

---

"And in that quarter," said Sang-hoon.

He stopped.

Georgij watched a man with thirty-one years in a trade walk the length of it in about four seconds without moving. He arrived where he had already arrived at some point in the last eleven days, and found it confirmed, and took it without one word of complaint.

"The Kims."

---

"There is nothing to announce," said Georgij.

---

"That is not an answer."

"It is the whole answer and it is true in every part," said Georgij. "There is no filing. There is no announcement. There is no purchase of a company and no change of a name, and there will not be one in January or in March or at any time. If you send four people to look for it they will find nothing, because there is nothing, and they will come back and tell you they found nothing."

Sang-hoon looked at him for some time.

"And that is how I will know."

"That is how you will know."

---

He got up and went to the window and stood with his back to both of them, which is a thing this man had not done in any room Georgij had ever been in with him.

"Ye-rin."

Georgij did not answer that, and it was not a refusal, and Sang-hoon did not turn round and did not ask again.

---

"Your offer," said Annie.

It was the first time she had spoken in eleven minutes.

"My offer stands until I am in the car," said Sang-hoon, still at the window. "It will not stand after that, and I am not saying so to be difficult. I have just watched him do something I would not have believed if it had been reported to me, and I am now certain that two billion two hundred million is too little. I am not able to go higher today, and I do not like paying twice."

---

Annie's answer took five words.

"He was never for sale."

---

Park Sang-hoon turned round then.

"I have been in a great many rooms," he said, "and I have heard people say that about a horse, a building, a football club and a daughter. Every one of them was for sale about eighteen months later, at a price they were embarrassed by."

"I know," said Annie.

"Then you know why I asked."

"I know why you asked, and I know why you came up the drive instead of over the wall, and I am going to think about the second one for longer than the first."

---

He collected his coat from the back of the chair.

At the door he stopped, the way he had stopped in November, and he did not offer his hand then either.

"Mr Georgij."

"Chairman."

---

"Two things, and you will get no more from me after today."

"I am listening."

"The first is that the apple was very good, and I would eat it again on the information I had. No man in this country would have done otherwise, and I want you to know that I know it." He put his coat on. "It was not a trick and I will not have it called one. Everything in it was true, and it was put in front of me at the one hour it could cost me most, by somebody who understood the hour better than I did. There are perhaps nine people alive who could have built it."

---

Georgij inclined his head and said nothing.

"The second is for you and not for her." Sang-hoon's voice did not change at all. "You spent your only free thing this afternoon on eleven seconds of my face. I would have paid you for it. I offered, in fact, about forty minutes ago, and you were in the room."

"I was."

"You gave it away instead, to be pleased for eleven seconds, in front of the person who told you not to be." He opened the door himself. "I have done that twice in thirty-one years, and both times it was the most expensive thing I did that decade, and both times I would do it again. That is not advice. There is no advice in it. It is the only guidance I have ever had that was worth anything and it is yours."

At the car the door was already open for him, which it had not been on the way in.

Georgij watched from the window. A man who had put his name on that document a week ago was holding a door for the employer who had lost by it, and doing it properly, with the other hand at his side.

Nobody said anything out there either.

Then the car went down the drive, and the gate did what the gate does.

---

The room was very quiet again and the light had gone from the window.

Georgij stood where he was and found that his hands were not entirely steady and that this had been true for some minutes.

"Sit down," said Annie.

He sat down.

---

"There is something I owe you and I want to say it before you ask me for it."

"Then say it."

"On the eighteenth of November, in the car coming back from Yeouido, you told me to be pleased with it in that car and nowhere else." He had his hands on his knees and they had stopped. "Because a man who looks pleased in a room stops being shown off to. I broke it this afternoon, in front of you, and I told you thirty seconds before I did it." He did not make it sound better than it was. "That is a warning and it is not a question. I did not ask you, because you would have had to say yes in front of him, and then it would have been yours and not mine."

---

Annie's face did nothing at all.

"Why."

"Because there has been nothing left in that man for me to get since the fourth of December, and he is not showing off any more and he is not going to." He did not hurry it. "That is the reason and it is a true one. It is also not why I did it."

"No."

"No. I did it because I have wanted to since the twenty-second of November, and because it was the one afternoon in my life when it was going to be available."

"That is thirty-seven days."

"Yes, Mistress."

---

Annie let that stand where it was.

"You have asked me for one thing for yourself since October, on a bridge, and you told me at the time what it was for." She was counting and not accusing. "This afternoon is the second, and you did not ask for it. You warned me, you took it, and you were in this chair explaining yourself inside four minutes."

"Yes."

"Then we will both remember that this is how you do it." She looked at the window. "It was not the wrong afternoon."

---

"Was it worth it."

"Yes."

"Say why, and do not be clever about it."

"Because in November he asked what I would cost in front of me and I did not get to answer him." He looked at the carpet and then up. "Today he asked again and I answered, and the answer was not a number, and he heard it, and he understood every part of it before I finished the sentence. That is all it was. That is the whole of what I wanted for thirty-seven days."

---

Annie was quiet.

"You understand what he did to you at the door."

"He put a number in the room," said Georgij. "Then he told me it was too low, and then he told me that he would not pay it twice, and then he walked out with all three of those facts standing up."

"And in March I am going to ask you what you want."

"Yes, Mistress."

"And now there is a figure in your head that was not there this morning, and you did not want it there, and it was put there by a man you had just finished taking apart." She was not making a point. She was reading it out. "He has never once done a stupid thing in front of me. That was not one either."

---

Georgij looked at the window for a while.

"No," he said. "It was not."

---

The light had gone and neither of them had put a lamp on.

"Was I useful?"

He had not planned to ask it. He heard it leave and there was no version of the afternoon in which he could take it back, and he sat in the chair and let it stand in the room.

---

Annie did not answer for some time.

"You were useful in November," she said. "You were useful on the sixteenth of December, and this afternoon you were useful in a way I will be able to put a figure on by March. That is the whole of what the word covers."

"Yes, Mistress."

"It is also not the question you asked me."

---

She got up, and she did not go to the door.

She came round the low table and stood beside his chair, which put her where he would have had to turn his head to see her face, and she did not give him the time to turn it.

"On the twenty-seventh of November I was upstairs in front of a screen, watching a man go over my east wall at the one place on it where I would see him do it." Her hand came down on the back of the chair, four or five centimetres from his shoulder, and stayed there. "I went and got my coat before he was off the top of the wall."

---

Georgij did not move at all.

"You have never asked me why I came out to that bridge," said Annie, "and you are not going to ask me tonight."

"No, Mistress."

"That is the answer to your question. It is the only one I have and I am not going to put it in the other words."

---

Her hand came off the back of the chair.

It did not go away. It came round and settled along the side of his face, cool from the room, the heel of it just under the bone.

---

He had not moved towards anybody in seventeen years.

He had been moved and positioned and sent for. He had stood where he was put, in a hundred halls, and the not moving was most of what he was good for, and he had been told so in four languages by people who meant it as praise.

He turned his head into her hand and shut his eyes.

---

He had counted all afternoon.

Four minutes to two at the gate. Eleven seconds of a man's face. A full minute of silence that somebody else refused to fill. He counts the way other people breathe and he has done it since he was old enough to be sold.

He did not count this.

---

Annie did not say anything and did not take her hand away, and after a while the last of the light went off the window frame and neither of them got up to do anything about it.

---

Mrs Seo came in at six and made up the fire, which she does not do on a Monday, and nobody said anything about that either.

---

# Book One · Chapter 34: Then take it off
*Lot Fourteen* · Version 1.8 · EN

## Day Ninety-One · Friday 2 January

On the second she was in the small room at eight with her coat over the back of the chair, an hour before the car, and she had not sent for anybody. She was there when he came past.

"There is nothing for you between now and March," said Annie. "I am not asking whether you have found something. You have. You always have." She put the cup down without having drunk out of it. "You will not open anything new. You will not go and look at anybody. If this house is telephoned and the caller wants you, you will take it, give them nothing, and tell me who it was."

---

"Does that include what is already open?"

"It includes going and looking."

"Yes, Mistress."

---

Annie took her coat off the back of the chair and did not put it on.

"You have not asked me why."

"No."

She went out past him with the coat over her arm and did not stop on the way, and that was the whole of it.

---

He worked out the reason on the stairs, ten minutes later, and the reason he arrived at was a good one.

The structure was eleven days old. Money was going to move through it in three pieces before the end of the month. A man who goes and turns something over in January is a man who might turn over the wrong thing in the one quarter in which nothing is allowed to move that was not already on a schedule.

That was competent. It was the sort of thing she would do. He was satisfied with it for about a week.

---

In November he had been wrong about this woman twice in four days, on facts he was holding at the time.

This one took ten weeks.

---

## Day One Hundred and Forty-Five · Wednesday 25 February

The first cargo under Chairman Woo's seven years crossed the quay at Yeongjong on the ninth of January, in weather that shut two other terminals for the morning.

Georgij heard about it four days later from Jang, who had it from a man on the gate.

That was the shape of the whole winter. Things happened, they happened correctly, and nobody needed to tell him.

---

The Kim block voted twice.

Both times the six telephoned Kim Ye-rin first, the way they had for twenty years. Both times she told them what the document said. Both times the document said what Georgij had spent November making it say, and her name was on page eleven, and it had been on page eleven since the twenty-second of December.

Annie's capital went into the logistics arm in the second week of January, in three tranches, on a schedule drawn by four people in an office in Yeouido whom Georgij had never met and had no reason to meet.

The lease held. The rent was fixed. The buildings were named.

---

Twice he brought her something anyway.

The first was four lines about a haulier in Gimpo who was three weeks from losing a contract the Kims could have had for nothing. He put it in front of her as a note on a thing already open, which was true and was not the point, and both of them knew which of the two it was.

Annie read it. She turned it face down on the desk, went on with what she had been doing, and did not say one word about it then or afterwards.

---

The second time was the middle of February, and he did not reach the end of the first sentence.

"No," said Annie.

Georgij closed his mouth and left it closed.

"No," she said again, and she was not unkind about it, and that was the part he could not get past.

---

Unkindness he had eleven houses of. He had no procedure at all for a woman who refused him gently, twice, about work, and then put her hand on the back of his neck in an empty hall on her way past.

She did that twice, and both times she went straight on to whatever she had been going to.

---

The instrument was used once.

On the twenty-third of January a renewal at the corporate services firm in Jung-gu came up against a date. Annie was in Yeouido until nine, and Georgij was the only person in that house entitled to put a name on it. Mr Chae's office sent two pages and a man on a scooter.

The signature came out the same as it had come out in December. He had one now, and this was the thing it turned out to be for. He gave the pages back and the man on the scooter went away, and the whole of it took four minutes.

That was the whole of what he ever did with it.

---

He walked the house at night the way he had walked it on the fourth of October, and the glass was the glass and the garage door was the garage door. Jang's night man went along the east side twice between midnight and four and had been doing it since the week of the gala.

Georgij went and put his hand on the east latch anyway, at half past one on the Wednesday, between the two of them.

Then he stood in the dark with his hand on it and worked out what he was doing.

---

The house took a boy for the kitchens in the first week of January.

He was seventeen, his name was Yun, and he carried things with his forearms instead of his hands, which is what a man does when nobody has taught him and he is afraid of dropping.

Georgij had him graded inside a week. Adequate, and then good, on his own, at the speed such things happen. There was no room he needed steering into and no man he needed keeping away from.

By the middle of February there was nothing to add, and there had been nothing to add for six weeks.

---

Annie worked.

She was up before six and in Yeouido four days out of seven, and there were people in the small room until nine at night who had never been in that house before Christmas. She was doing the thing the whole autumn had been for, and she was doing it very well, and every part of it had been taken out of his hands in one sentence on a Friday morning.

He carried her coat twice a day and stood in rooms where nobody asked him anything.

---

The figure was two billion two hundred million won, and it would not go down.

It arrived at odd hours and never while he was working, because he was not working. It arrived on the stairs. It arrived while he was waiting for a kettle.

He had carried other people's numbers his whole life and had never once had one come to him without being sent for.

And he had an opinion about whether it was correct. That was what Park Sang-hoon had actually left in the room, and it had cost him one minute at a door on his way out to his car.

---

On the night of the twenty-fourth of February he counted the stairs on the east side and got twenty-two.

He had known it was twenty-two since October.

---

## Day One Hundred and Forty-Nine · Sunday 1 March

Mr Chae came at ten with the same leather case and without the younger man.

"Mr Georgij."

"Mr Chae."

"It expires at midnight, and I said there was no grace period." He put the case on the low table. "I would rather collect the counterpart in daylight than explain on Monday why I did not."

---

They did it at the same table and it took eleven minutes.

Georgij laid the counterpart down with the signature facing the lawyer.

"I told her in November that I would put this in her hand on the second."

Mr Chae read both pages against his own and dated a line at the foot of each.

"Then you will have to tell her instead." He put the two of them in the case. "It is not the same thing and I am aware that it is not."

---

"One instrument executed under it."

"One," said Georgij. "A renewal in Jung-gu, on the twenty-third of January, because she was in Yeouido until nine and I was the only person in the building. Three counterparties, three months, and the ceiling was never anywhere near."

Mr Chae wrote the number at the foot of the page and drew a line under it.

---

"On the twenty-seventh of November I asked her for this on a bridge, and I told her why." Georgij said it in the voice he had used for the rest of it. "Four rooms between then and February. In two of them I would be asked something that could only be answered with a name on a line, and the answer would have to be that I telephoned a woman from a shed."

"And?"

"There were four rooms. I have counted them since." He did not make it easier than it was. "Not one of them came down to a line. Every one of them was decided by somebody saying something out loud, and I was in three of them, and in the third I did not open my mouth about the thing that mattered."

---

Mr Chae took that without any expression at all.

"Then you were right about the rooms."

"I was right about the rooms."

---

"That is what it was for." Mr Chae closed the case and did not open it again. "In December I told you the instrument was smaller than you thought it was. I have never been that exactly right about one before and I did not enjoy it."

---

Georgij watched him square the case and close the two catches in the order he had closed them in December.

"May I ask you something that is none of my business?"

"You may ask."

"On the first of December you wrote two words on a pad and did not show me which."

---

Mr Chae did not look up.

"No fee," he said.

---

"That is all of it?"

"That is all of it. I write a note about every beneficiary and it is nearly always about money, because money is the part that goes wrong later." He straightened the case on the table so that it sat square to the edge. "In twenty-three years of drawing instruments for that woman I had never written those two words on a pad, and I wanted to be able to find them again."

---

Something happened in Georgij's chin and got no further, and it was the same thing that had happened at a tailor's in October and across a dining table in November.

It was aimed at a lawyer's two catches and at nothing else in that room, and Mr Chae did not see it.

---

There was an envelope at the far end of the table that had been there when Georgij came in.

It was not large. It was not sealed. There was no writing on the outside of it at all.

Mr Chae looked at it for about a second on his way past.

"That is not this instrument," he said.

"No."

"Then I have not seen it." He put his coat on without help. "I am at home today. The number is the same one."

---

Annie came in after the car had gone down the drive.

She sat in the chair on the other side of the low table, which is not where she sits, and she did not touch the envelope.

"I said I would ask you in March," she said. "It is March."

"Yes, Mistress."

"What do you want."

---

He had had seventy-five days.

He had known the date since the sixteenth of December. That evening he had refused the same question twice, once to a woman on a terrace and once to this one in the small room. He had given both of them the same reason and had meant it both times.

He had turned it over on stairs and in cars and in the dark with his hand on a latch.

And what came up his throat, when he opened his mouth, was a figure.

---

He shut his mouth again.

"Say it," said Annie.

"No."

"Say it anyway."

---

"Two billion two hundred million won."

---

"That is not what I want. It also did not arrive here by accident." He had his hands flat on his knees. "You asked me what I want, so I went and looked. The only part of me with a figure attached is the part somebody else was willing to pay for. It has been there since the twenty-ninth of December and it is the only unit I have got."

---

Annie was not surprised, which was worse.

"On the twenty-ninth I told you he had never once done a stupid thing in front of me."

"You did."

"He put it in the room so that when I asked you this, you would answer me in his currency." She was looking at the envelope now. "He did that at the door, on his way out, after he had lost, for nothing. It was the most expensive minute of his year and he spent it on a man he had no further use for."

---

"Open that."

---

It was one sheet and it was a photocopy.

Three columns, the way such things are set out. A number, an owner and a date. There were other lines above and below and somebody had put a hand over them before the copy was made, so that the whole page was the one row and a grey smear at either end of it.

The number was fourteen.

Across the row, in a hand that was not Annie's, somebody had drawn a single stroke of a pen. At the end of the stroke there was a stamp, and inside the stamp a second date, and the second date was the nineteenth of February.

---

"Struck," said Annie. "It took from the fourth of January to the nineteenth of February. I am not going to tell you what it cost and I will not give you the figure if you ask me for it."

Georgij did not ask her for it.

"Four times," she said. "I sat in that building four times. The third time was the one that did it and it was not the money."

---

He put the sheet down on the table and set his hand beside it and not on it.

"Hear the reason," said Annie. "The whole of it, because the nicer version is available and you would take it."

---

"On the twenty-ninth of December a man sat in that chair and offered me two billion two hundred million won for you, and I said no and I meant it. Then he told me the figure was too low." She had not moved in the chair. "He was right. He is right about most things and he will be right about that one inside a year, and he does not like paying twice, and there are four other men in this country who do not mind at all."

Annie let the sheet lie where he had put it.

"A line in a book in a building I do not own was the only route by which any of them could have taken you off me. While it stood there was a price and a procedure and a counterparty. There is no line now, so there is no procedure, so there is nothing to buy. It was the last open position I had and I have closed it."

---

"And the second of January," said Georgij.

He had been carrying it since the stairs on the second of January, and this was the first time he had put it down where she could see it.

---

"The same account." Annie did not move in the chair. "Every room you go into and come out of ahead puts something on top of what that man offered me for you in December. I was going to be in that building four times. I was not going to be in it while you got more expensive."

"I thought it was the schedule."

"I know what you thought. You had it by the Friday and you were pleased with it." The sheet was still face up on the table between them and she did not look at it. "It was not the schedule. And I did not tell you, because you would have priced the difference and found a way to be useful that I could not see from this house."

---

"Is that all of it."

"It is all of it that has a figure in it."

---

Georgij did not thank her for it. It is his small change and he spends it on lamps and coats, and she would have heard it land.

"And there is the other half, and you know what it is, because you were standing on the bridge when I said it."

---

She said it the way she says things she has had ready for some time.

"There is a register with your number in it in a building I do not own, and until that is dealt with, taking it off you would be the most dangerous thing anybody has ever done to you."

---

"It is dealt with."

"It is dealt with," said Annie. "The man who fits them can be here on Tuesday. He will not ask a single question, and it takes about forty minutes."

She turned the sheet round on the table so that it faced her.

"So we will take it off."

---

Georgij put the sheet back into the envelope and did not pick the envelope up.

"No, Mistress."

---

Annie did not get out of the chair she never uses.

"Say why."

"I cannot use the reason I gave you on the bridge."

She waited.

---

"On the bridge I told you it was the only thing standing between me and a room with nineteen other people in it. I told you a man who belongs to nobody is stock. I told you it is what makes it too much trouble to take me off you." He had both hands flat and they had gone very still. "Every word of that was true on the twenty-seventh of November."

"And this morning."

---

"Two of the three stopped. The middle one did not." He did not hurry any of it. "A man who belongs to nobody is stock, and since the nineteenth of February I belong to nobody. What is round my neck is a mark with nothing behind it. It goes on working for exactly as long as nobody looks in that book."

---

"Then keep it for that," said Annie.

"It would hold." He did not take it. "It is not why."

---

"You have taken the half of it I could use in a room, and you did it deliberately."

"I did."

"You did it in that order so that whatever I said next would have to be the other one."

"Yes."

---

He did not answer that for some time.

The gate was shut and the drive was empty and out beyond the wall a Sunday was going on without either of them in it.

---

"I'm afraid of Tuesdays."

---

Annie did not help him.

"Say it so that I can use it."

---

"Until the nineteenth of February, if you had finished with me, you would have had to sell me." It did not come out level and he did not try again. "Set a price. Find a room. Put me in front of people and sign something in front of a man like the one who was here at ten. It takes weeks, and somebody has to help you do it, and you would have had to look at it every morning while it was going on. On about the ninth of those mornings you might have found that you did not want to."

"And now."

"Now there is nothing to sell." He was sitting forward, which he does not do. "You have had the line struck, so there is no procedure and no counterparty and no price and nothing to sign. You tell me on a Tuesday, in a hall, between two other things, and it is finished before you have got to the end of the sentence."

---

"You are describing being free," said Annie.

"I am describing how cheap it has become to be finished with me." He did not soften the next part either. "Those are the same thing. I know that they are. I have had it in my hands since you turned that sheet round, and I cannot make myself want it."

---

Annie let the whole of it get to the floor before she said anything.

"On the bridge I said that neither of us would have to be brave about it."

"You did."

"That was the last morning either of us could say it."

---

She got up and did not go anywhere.

"You have just told me that the only thing keeping you in this house was that leaving it would be inconvenient for me."

---

"No, Mistress."

Georgij turned his hand over on his knee.

"On the bridge I told you the collar is not what holds me. I have not changed my mind about that in ninety-four days. It is not what holds me and it never was."

Georgij said the last of it at the same speed as the rest and no faster.

"It is what holds you. I have known that since about the second week of October and I have never said it out loud. There was no afternoon in this whole autumn in which saying it would have been anything other than a demand."

---

Annie looked at him.

"That is the most expensive thing you have ever said to me."

"I know."

"And you have chosen the one morning on which I cannot answer it with paper."

---

"Then it stays on."

"Then it stays on," said Georgij.

---

"There is more of it," said Annie. "Say the rest."

---

"I will not stop calling you Mistress."

---

Her hand went to the arm of the chair and stopped there.

"There is no line, no owner and no date. Nobody in this country can produce one page that says you are mine, and the man who fits them can be here on Tuesday, and you have got nothing at all requiring you to say that word."

"No."

"And you are going to go on saying it."

"Every day, until you tell me not to." The envelope was on the table between them with nothing in it. "And on the day you tell me not to I will stop by that afternoon, and I will not ask you why, and I will not make you say it a second time."

---

Annie went as far as the window and stood with her back to the room.

There was nothing out there. The drive was empty, the gate was shut, and the rose beds on the east side were cut back to nothing at all, the way they are in the first week of March.

"Come here."

---

He came.

He went down onto the floor beside her, which took about two seconds and which nobody in that room had asked for, and sat back on his heels with his shoulder against her leg.

He had been put on his knees in four countries by people who had a reason.

---

Annie's hand came down on the back of his head and stayed there.

The light on the window frame moved about as far as it moves in a quarter of an hour, and nobody came to the door, and nobody was going to.

---

Behind them the sheet was on the low table with the stroke of a pen across it and the stamp at the end of the stroke.

A number, an owner, two dates.

There is no column on that page for what a man is called. There has never been one, because a name is the one thing in the transaction that nobody on either side of it needs.

He has two other documents in the world. Her compliance people tidied up a set of papers in the second week of October, so that he would be able to sign things. The other will be dead by midnight and is already in a leather case in Jung-gu. His name is on both, and both of them are about what he is permitted to do.

The sheet on the low table was the only one that had ever been about what he was, and it had got all the way through him in three columns without once saying who he was.

---

His knee began to hurt at some point and he did not move.

And for the first time since the ninth of January he was doing the only thing in that house that needed doing.

---

# Book Two · Chapter 1: Nothing to sign with
*Lot Fourteen* · Version 1.4 · EN

## Day One Hundred and Fifty · Monday 2 March

Two things ran out, a day apart, and they pulled in opposite directions.

The sentence from the second of January ran out when March did, and March came in on the Sunday. He spent the end of that Sunday on the floor at a window, against her legs, and it did not once occur to him that he was free to go and look at somebody.

The instrument died at midnight at the end of that same Sunday, on the date he had asked for himself.

So on the Monday he was permitted to work and had nothing to work with.

---

Mrs Seo's tray went up at seven, the way it has gone up at seven since October.

The staff table was laid for four and sat six. The boy from the kitchens ate standing at the end of it, because Mr Baek had never told him to sit down and had never once told him not to.

Yun had been in the house eight weeks. In January he carried plates on his forearms. In March he carried them in his hands, and nobody in that kitchen had said a word to him about it.

The boy said good morning to him, and had said it every day since the second week, which nobody had told him to do either. Two of the four maids still do not.

Georgij had graded him in the first week and had had nothing to add since the middle of February.

This was the third Monday running on which he had come down early enough to watch another man work.

---

She was in the small room at eight with her coat over the back of the chair.

The same chair and the same hour as the second of January. On the second of January she had been sitting in it an hour before the car with nothing in front of her, waiting for him to come past. This morning she was working, and he was the one who came.

"Mistress."

"It is March," said Annie.

"Yes."

---

"On the twenty-seventh of November I asked you for the smallest instrument that works, with a date on it, and I said that after the date it would be dead and I would ask you again." He stopped inside the door and stayed there. "It died at midnight."

Annie put down the page she was holding and did not turn it face down.

"Ask, then."

"I am asking."

---

"What for."

---

Georgij had his hands at his sides.

"On the twenty-third of January a renewal in Jung-gu came up against a date and you were in Yeouido until nine. Two pages came up the drive on a scooter. I put a name on them and it took four minutes."

"That is what it turned out to be for."

"Yes."

---

"Then I will give one to Mrs Seo." Annie sat back. "Nine years in this house. She runs the staff and the accounts and the outside firms, and when a man on a scooter comes up that drive at six in the evening she is already in the building. It costs me one afternoon in Jung-gu and it does not expire."

There was nothing wrong with any part of that.

"Yes, Mistress."

---

"Say the rest of it."

"There is no rest of it that I can put a figure against."

---

Annie turned her chair a few degrees, so that she was facing him and not the desk.

"Mr Chae told you in December that the instrument was smaller than you thought it was. Yesterday he told you he had never been that exactly right about one before."

"He did."

"He was right about the size of it, and he does not know what you wanted it for." Her hands were still on the arms of the chair. "I do. So do you. Neither of us has said it out loud, and one of us is about to."

---

Georgij did not have anywhere to put his hands and put them behind his back.

"In November, in the office at the end of a shed at Yeongjong, you told an old man that you had nothing to sign with. You said it was not modesty. You said it was the arrangement."

"I did."

"There is no arrangement."

---

Her coat was still over the back of the chair.

"There is a set of papers your compliance people tidied up in the second week of October," said Georgij. "My name is on them. Yours is not."

Annie waited.

"There was a line in a book with an owner on it and a date, and my name was not on that either, because there is no column for it." His hands stayed where they were. "The instrument that died at midnight had both. It is the only thing that ever has."

---

Annie got up and took the coat off the back of the chair.

"Mr Chae draws instruments with counterparties in them. He asks what it is for, he writes the answer down, and it goes into a leather case and into a room in Jung-gu, and in twenty years a stranger reads it." She had the coat over her arm and did not put it on. "What you have just said to me is not a thing that goes in a case."

"No, Mistress."

"Then ask me again when there is a room in it."

---

She went out past him with the coat over her arm, the way she had gone out past him on the second of January.

At the door she stopped, which she had not done then.

"You are working again," said Annie. "Nobody has told you what to want."

---

Georgij stayed where he was for a moment after she had gone.

On the second of January he had come off the stairs with a reason inside ten minutes, and he had been satisfied with it for about a week, and it had been wrong for ten of them.

This morning he had nothing at all. That is not the same thing as having nothing to do, and he had spent the winter learning the difference.

---

The catalogue came at eleven in a flat parcel with a printed label, addressed to the house and not to anybody in it.

Mrs Seo put it on the desk in the small room with three other things. It lay there until two. Annie was in Yeouido, and there was no reason on earth for Georgij to be in that room.

He went in at two.

---

He picked it up, which he had not done in October.

In October it had lain forty centimetres from his hand at the corner of that desk. He had read it upside down at twenty-two seconds a page and Annie had counted. Neither of them had said out loud what he was looking for.

It was the same size and the same weight. The paper was better.

---

The house used to renumber every month, so a number meant nothing at all. He had worked that out in October in the time it takes to go down one page, and knowing that a number means nothing is the kind of thing a man keeps.

The numbers had stopped meaning nothing.

---

At the foot of the first page, in six point, under a rule that had not been there before:

*References are permanent and are not reissued. Withdrawn and completed entries retain their reference.*

---

He went down the descriptions the way he had gone down them in October, which takes a particular kind of reading, because the whole thing is written in the flat abbreviated language of a livestock list.

Female, twenty-eight. Male, thirty, with a trade. Male, twenty-four, with a mark against him for a second return.

The mark goes in the book now and it stays there, and the man it is against will carry it into every room he is ever stood up in.

Nineteen lots.

He read the whole of it once. He did not read it a second time backwards, the way you check figures, because in October he had been looking for somebody and this morning he was not.

---

At the back there was a page that had never been at the back of anything in that house.

It was a list and it went back four years. A reference, a season, and one word.

The references were not catalogue numbers. Catalogue numbers are made up fresh every month and thrown away when the month is over. These were the numbers out of the book. They stand in a column with an owner and a date beside them, and he had looked at one of them yesterday morning on a photocopy with a grey smear at either end.

*Completed* against most of them. *Withdrawn* against some.

And against four of them, in the same six point, three words in place of the one.

*Retained, not disclosed.*

---

He found eleven, and then he found fourteen, four lines further down in the same column, with the same three words against both.

Four in four years. Two of them were hers.

---

Under the list there was a short paragraph about the keeping of records, and at the bottom of the page a name and a title.

He read the paragraph twice, which he had not needed to do with anything else in that book.

It was four sentences long and every one of them was about method. References are permanent. Entries are retained. The practice is reviewed once a year and the review is minuted. Prior seasons have been brought into the same series.

Nowhere in the four sentences was there a word about what the references were references to.

His chin did what it does in front of work that is better than it has to be. There was nobody in the room to see it, and the work was a paragraph about filing.

---

Georgij put the book down and squared it to the edge of the desk.

Then he stood in that room and did the arithmetic he had been putting off since the foot of the first page.

A reference that is never reissued cannot be taken back. A gap in that list is a gap for as long as the list exists. Every gap carries a season beside it. The list goes out in a flat parcel to everybody who buys.

The line was struck on the nineteenth of February. The striking of it is now the most durable thing about it.

Yesterday he had told her that what was round his neck went on working for exactly as long as nobody looked in that book.

Somebody had made the book portable. It had come up the drive that morning in a flat parcel with a printed label on it.

---

Annie came back from Yeouido at ten past eight.

The catalogue was where he had left it, closed and square to the edge, with the other three things beside it.

"You have read it," said Annie.

"Yes."

She took her coat off, put it over the back of the chair, and sat down in the chair she sits in.

---

"They kept the numbers," said Georgij. "In October a number meant nothing, because they renumbered every month. Now a number is a number for good, and the ones that are gone are printed with the ones that are not, so that a buyer can see that nothing has been quietly lost."

"I know what is on that page."

"Eleven and fourteen. Four lines apart, the same three words against each."

"I know which two they are."

---

Georgij stayed on the far side of the desk.

"You paid to have a line struck out of a book, and the book has begun printing a list of the lines that have been struck out of it."

"Yes."

"Then it is worth more tonight than it was in October." His hands were at his sides again. "Whoever owns that book has the name of every person in this country who has ever paid to make a line go away. There are four lines. I am one of them."

---

Annie looked at the window and not at the desk, and for a while she did not say anything at all.

"There is a name at the bottom of that page," said Georgij.

"There is."

"Did you meet him?"

---

Annie put her hand flat on the arm of the chair.

"Ask me something else."

---

He asked her about the haulier in Gimpo. It was four lines on a sheet she had turned face down in January without a word, and it was two months stale now, and worth about half of what it had been.

She answered him for twenty minutes, in detail, and sent him to find out two things he could only find out by going and looking at somebody.

Neither of them said anything more that evening about the page at the back of the book.

---

In November he had stood in an office at the end of a shed with the sea on three sides of it. He had told an old man that he had nothing to sign with, and that it was not modesty, it was the arrangement.

It was the first evening on which only the first half of that was true.

---

# Book Two · Chapter 2: The order of loading
*Lot Fourteen* · Version 1.0 · EN

## Day One Hundred and Fifty-Two · Wednesday 4 March

Mr Ku drove him out to Gimpo on the Wednesday morning and did not ask what for.

The yard was off a road with three yards on it and the other two were bigger. Room for eight and four trucks in it. One of the four had its bonnet up, and there was rain standing in the engine bay, so it had been up since the weekend and nobody had put anything over it.

Shin came out himself. There was nobody to send.

---

Georgij had four ways of opening with a man he needed something from. He stood in that yard for about three seconds and put all four of them down.

Three were for rooms in which the other man was still deciding what Georgij was. Shin was not deciding anything about anybody. He was standing in his own gate in March with four trucks and five weeks of nothing coming. There is no version of charm that does not read, to a man in that position, as somebody from a large house enjoying himself.

The fourth was the question about who to be careful of, and that one belongs to a newcomer.

---

You cannot take anything from a man who has nothing. You can only pay him.

So he paid first, before he had asked for anything, which is not an order he had ever used.

---

"You lost Daehan at the end of January," said Georgij. "I am going to tell you why. Then I am going to ask you two things, and you can decide afterwards whether you answer them."

Shin wiped his hands down the front of his coat and said nothing at all.

"It was not you. It was not your price and it was not the two loads in October." He gave it flat and in order, the way he gives everything. "In August their insurer rewrote one line about carrier concentration. Above a tonnage they may use one named haulier and only one, from the first of January. You are under the tonnage. Nobody at your size in this country kept that contract this year, and the man who took it off you is not better at this than you are."

---

Shin stood there for a while.

"I went and asked them," he said. "Twice. Second time a fellow came out to the car park to tell me there was nothing to tell me."

"There was nothing he was permitted to tell you." Georgij took a folded sheet out of his coat and put it into Shin's hand. "It is in their own filing and it has been public since August. Page twenty-six. The paragraph is four lines long and your name is not in it."

---

Shin looked at the sheet in his own hand and then at the yard.

"I put two men off in February," he said.

"I know."

"I told them both it was the two loads in October." He said it evenly enough. "That's what I had."

Georgij did not tell him that he could go and say something else to those two men now. A man who has just been handed a page works that out on his own, and saying it out loud would have been the second thing given and the first thing taken.

---

Shin put the sheet away in an inside pocket without unfolding it, which was what Georgij had expected.

"Ask your two," he said.

---

The first was what he still had. Four trucks running and a fifth that would run for the price of a pump. The yard on a lease with two years left on it. Two licences that take a year and a half to get, and he had held both for twelve years. One of the two is the whole reason anybody in Seoul would ever look at a road haulier in Gimpo.

The second was what he owed, and Shin gave him that one at the same speed, which not one man in four does.

It was worse than the four lines Annie had turned face down in January had assumed. It was also all in one place, to one lender, and secured on the trucks and not on the licences, which is the difference between a man who is finished and a man who is having a bad year.

Georgij wrote none of it down while he was standing there.

---

Georgij thanked him for them and did not ask anything else.

Shin went on anyway, walking him back over the yard, and this was the part that was not in anybody's note.

---

"You want to know what it's actually like now, go and stand at the Incheon gate at four in the morning." Shin had his hands in his coat pockets. "I'm there twice a week. Twelve years. It's not the same yard it was in November."

"What is different?"

---

"The order of loading." Shin stopped walking. "Nine months of the year that place is full. It has always been full and everybody has always waited, and waiting is the job. What's different since January is that the waiting has a shape to it. Same name goes on first. Every sailing. And there's three or four of us who've worked that out from being one of the ones that doesn't."

---

"There's a fellow out of Ulsan I've known eight years. Chemicals. One lane, nothing else, never had anything else." Shin was looking at his own trucks and not at Georgij. "His boxes have rolled twice since the middle of January. Two sailings. He doesn't sleep at home any more. He sleeps in the cab at the gate on a Tuesday night so he's first through in the morning."

He turned his head.

"Being first through that gate has got nothing whatever to do with what goes on the ship. He knows that. He does it anyway."

---

"Somebody bought something over the winter," said Shin. "Nobody knows what and nobody in Gimpo is ever going to be told. But you can read it off the quay, and it reads the same every week."

---

Georgij stood in a yard in Gimpo with the rain going into an engine bay eight metres away and read his own handwriting off a stranger's account of a Tuesday night.

He had written it out in November at a dining table with three people sitting at it. *A veto over routes decides which container goes on which sailing when a sailing is full.* He had said it out loud, in that order, and he had been pleased with how clean it was.

He had put it forward as a reason to do it.

A man sleeps in a cab now, on the strength of it.

---

At the gate Shin asked what house he was from, which is what a man asks when he does not want to ask for a name.

"Hers is not on anything you have read," said Georgij. "I advise one person, and I have done it for less than a year."

"And she wants my trucks."

"She wants to know what is in this yard. What is done about it is not decided, and I would not tell you if it were."

Shin took that the way a man takes the first straight answer he has had since January, which it may well have been.

---

Mr Ku had the engine on before Georgij reached the car, because it was cold and because that is the standard of the man.

Georgij sat in the back with nothing in his hands for most of the way and did the arithmetic on it instead.

He had given a stranger in Gimpo a piece of reasoning that perhaps four people in this country would have gone and done, and he had given it away unasked, on a Wednesday, for two facts about trucks.

It was the correct trade. He had been in that yard under a minute when he understood it was the only trade on offer there, and he had made it, and it had come back at about six times what he went for.

It also has a shape. A man who pays in one particular currency, three times, has signed something.

---

Annie came out of the small room at half past eight.

He gave her the two things first, because those were the things that had been asked for, and she had them in under a minute and did not look surprised by either.

---

"And I paid him for them," said Georgij. "Before I asked, not after."

Annie did not put anything down.

"He lost Daehan at the end of January because of one line in an insurer's requirement, and he has spent five weeks believing it was his own fault. I told him it was not. I left him the page."

---

"It cost you nothing." He had not sat down. "It is public, it has been public since August, and there are perhaps four people in this country who would have gone and read it in order to answer that particular question. That is the part that cost something. He will repeat it, and he will repeat where he had it."

---

Annie looked at him for a moment.

"What else."

---

So he gave her the gate at four in the morning, and the shape of the waiting, and the man out of Ulsan who sleeps in a cab on a Tuesday night.

She took that one differently. She sat down for it, in her own chair, and she asked him six questions.

Two of them he could not answer. One was how long the Ulsan one had been rolling before Shin noticed, which nobody at that gate would know and which decides whether it is eight weeks of trouble or five months of it. The other was whether anybody had gone to him yet.

---

"How many are there like him."

"Shin says three or four out of Gimpo alone, and Gimpo is one road with three yards on it." Georgij was still standing. "I do not know the number. I know the mechanism, because I wrote it out for you at a table in November and I was pleased with it at the time."

---

"You wrote it out correctly," said Annie.

"I did."

"And you are telling me that it works."

---

"I am telling you that it works exactly as far as we said it would." He turned his hand over. "And about four hundred metres further than anybody at that table went to look."

---

Annie did not argue with any part of that, which he had not expected either.

"Bring me the Ulsan one by name," she said. "Not from Shin. From the filings, and then from somebody who has stood at that gate."

"Yes, Mistress."

---

She was already going back into the small room when she stopped.

"You will find that they are all four of them worth having, and that two of them will be gone by June." Her hand was on the frame. "That is not a reason to hurry. It is the shape of the thing."

---

Later, on the stairs, he worked out that she had said nothing at all about the page he had left in a yard in Gimpo.

Not yes. Not no. Not that it was hers to leave.

That was the second time in two days that a question he had put down in front of her had been stepped over rather than answered, and the first time had been about a name.

---

Yesterday he had asked her for a piece of paper saying what he was permitted to do, and she had not given him one.

Today he had given something of hers to a stranger without one, and had been right, and she had let it stand.

He went up the east stairs and did not count them.

---

# Book Two · Chapter 3: We haven't met
*Lot Fourteen* · Version 1.0 · EN

## Day One Hundred and Fifty-Five · Saturday 7 March

Nobody had asked him for any of it.

On the Wednesday night Annie had asked for the Ulsan one by name, from the filings and then from somebody who had stood at that gate. That is three days of work for a man who is quick, and he had it by Thursday afternoon.

He spent Thursday evening, Friday and Saturday on something else.

---

He closed the Ulsan file at about four on the Thursday and had three days in front of him with nothing in them.

On the Monday she had stopped at the door on her way out and told him he was working again, and that nobody had told him what to want. She had meant it as a release. He had taken it as one, and he is fairly certain she did not mean this.

Then he went down and took the catalogue off the desk in the small room and carried it upstairs, which is the first thing he has ever taken out of that room without being handed it.

---

What he had was four references and three seasons, and no column for who paid.

Two of the references were the fourth of October. Eleven and fourteen, four lines apart, and he had been in the room for both of them and had been one of them.

The other two were older. One was three years back. One was the year before that.

---

The house was never going to tell him anything and he did not go near it.

What he went at instead was the outside of an evening.

An auction of that kind is four hours in a building with a public stair and a private entrance. The people who come to it arrive in cars that somebody has to park and to wait in. The men who wait get fed at somebody's expense and then invoiced for. There is a settlement desk and there are runners who go down to the seats. There is a florist. There is a firm that puts out the chairs and takes them away again.

None of those people are in the book. All of them have their own paper, and their paper has dates on it.

---

He had done this once before, in his first fortnight, for a charity gala with eight hundred people at it, and it had given him four hundred and thirty names and had not given him Choi Dae-ho.

He did it again for an evening three years old.

---

He did it at the desk in his own room, which has a window on the east side and a lamp that Mrs Seo replaced in November without being asked and without mentioning it.

The boy brought a tray up on the Friday at one because nobody had come down. He put it at the corner of the desk on the side where there was room, which is a thing that has to be worked out and not taught.

Georgij thanked him and did not look up, and that was the whole of the human part of Friday.

---

It took Friday and most of Saturday and it worked.

Cars, in the end. A firm in Jung-gu that supplies drivers by the hour keeps its dockets for seven years, on account of a tax dispute it lost and has not got over. A docket carries a pick-up address and a drop. A drop that is the same street four times in three years is a customer.

Then the drop is a building, and the building has one tenant that receives people in the evening.

Then it is a matter of which of the four dockets falls on the right night, and after that of whose account the hours went to.

---

The account was a company. The company was a nothing, two rooms and a secretary, and it took Georgij about an hour to get behind it because whoever set it up had not been trying very hard, which by itself said something.

Behind it was a woman of eighty-one who has been charming for forty years and has never in that time told anybody anything all the way through.

---

Georgij put the sheet down on the desk and did not do any more work for a while.

---

He had shaken her hand in a ballroom on the twenty-fifth of October. She had come at him from the left at the ten-minute mark. Three weeks before that she had sat two rows in front of the woman who bought him, and had turned round with everybody else when a man in the second row stood up.

*We haven't met.*

He had thought at the time that it was a courtesy, and that she was giving him the evening clean because she was the sort of woman who understood what an entrance costs.

It was not a courtesy. It was a woman with something on that page, being extremely careful in front of a man who had just come off it.

---

He worked out on Saturday afternoon what she had actually been doing, and it took him under a minute once he stopped being flattered.

She had not been protecting him. She had been finding out, in four words and without asking, whether the new one at that table knew what she was.

He had told her, with his face, that he did not. She had given him six or seven minutes of nothing at all after that and had gone away pleased.

---

What he does not have, and will not get out of a docket, is what was on the line.

A reference, a season and a stroke of a pen. Somebody stood in that building three years ago with a number over their head, and a woman of eighty-one bought them, and then paid a second time to have the fact taken out of the book.

There are two reasons a person pays for that, and one of them is the reason Annie had. From the outside of an evening they look exactly alike.

---

The other one, the fourth, is not there.

Nobody ordered a car. Nobody was fed. The chair firm invoiced for the room and the florist invoiced for the room and there is no third piece of paper anywhere with that evening on it.

Georgij went at it twice and then put it down and wrote a line to himself about the age of the records, which was reasonable.

---

He had three of the four and one name out of two, and the name is the wrong half.

Deterrence needs the other side of it. A page with Annie on it and a woman of eighty-one on it is a page with two people on it who can both be hurt, and that is not a deadlock, that is a queue.

What he needs is somebody so large that the page cannot be used at all, and that one is the one with no car, no dinner and no paper.

---

He considered going to her, for about as long as it takes to put a cup down.

Against it: she is eighty-one and has been careful since before he was born, and a woman like that gets warned once. Walk up her drive with that page in your head and she knows within a sentence that somebody has read it, and after that she is careful about a specific thing instead of about everything, which is worse.

For it: nothing at all. He has no currency she wants. He is a man with no money who has already given her, in a ballroom, the one piece of information she went there to get.

So it is one approach and he has not got it ready, and the rule for that is the one Hana said out loud on a Thursday in November about a different woman who receives nobody. Not quickly and not twice.

---

Annie came in at nine with the Yeouido people gone and her coat already off.

He gave her Ulsan.

---

The name is Nam Byung-hee. She has the firm, which is one lane and nothing else, and she has run it since her brother died. She also has the chair of the association of independent shippers on that lane, which has existed for twenty years and which nobody has ever wanted before, because until January there was nothing on that lane to negotiate about.

Six firms. She writes to them on the first of every month whether there is anything to write about or not.

Georgij had that last part from a man who has stood at the gate, which is what had been asked for.

---

Annie went through it and asked four things.

Two were about the six firms. One was about the association's own constitution, which he had read on the Thursday. It is mostly nothing, apart from a single clause about who may speak for the members in a dealing with a carrier.

The fourth was whether Nam Byung-hee had ever held anything outside this trade, and the answer is no, and Annie put her hand flat on the arm of the chair when she heard it.

"She writes on the first," she said.

"Every month. Twenty years of it. The last three say nothing, and she sent them anyway."

---

Annie looked at that for a moment.

"Then she is not somebody who is going to be told to be patient."

"No, Mistress."

---

"What have you been doing since Thursday afternoon."

---

Georgij had known the question was coming since about four o'clock and had decided what he was going to do about it, and doing it turned out to be worse than deciding it.

"Something of my own. I will tell you, and I am not going to tell you tonight."

---

Annie did not move at all.

"Is it going to cost me anything."

"Not yet. On the day it starts to, you will have all of it that day, and you will not have to ask me for it."

"And if I ask you now."

"Then I will tell you now." He said that at the same speed as the rest of it. "I would rather you did not ask, and I am saying so instead of letting you find it out."

---

The clock in the hall did the thing it does at the quarter.

"Keep it, then," said Annie.

---

She went past him and put her hand on the back of his neck on the way, the way she had twice in February, and went straight on to whatever she had been going to.

---

He stood in the hall afterwards and did the arithmetic, because he does.

He had given her a term. Not tonight, and on the day it costs you, and if you ask I will tell you. Three limits on a thing he was keeping back, all of them said out loud, all of them binding on him and on nobody else.

That is how he does it. He has done it since October: a ceiling, named counterparties, a date after which it is dead and I ask you again.

---

In eight days she had stepped over two of his questions and had not put a date on either.

He went up and did not make anything of it. There is nothing to make of it that would survive being said out loud. And a man who counts what he is owed by the person he belongs to has started keeping a different sort of book.

---

He got as far as the landing before he heard which word he had used.

*Belongs.* On the seventh of March, by a man with no line, no owner and no date anywhere in the world.

He did not correct it.

---

# Book Two · Chapter 4: I have nothing to put in it
*Lot Fourteen* · Version 1.3 · EN

## Day One Hundred and Fifty-Nine · Wednesday 11 March

He did not go looking for the man. He went looking for the calendar.

A man with thirty-one years in a trade that size is on a schedule whether he likes it or not, and most of it is private and about four days of it a year are not. Launches. Handovers. The opening of somebody else's terminal.

And funerals.

---

They are announced, and that is all it takes. A death in that trade goes into two papers and onto a hall's own board, with the room number and the three days, and anybody at all may come.

Georgij found one on the Sunday night in about twenty minutes.

---

The man had been seventy-six. He had run a coastal feeder line out of Mokpo for most of his life, sold it to somebody larger six years ago, and gone on going to things afterwards, which is what men do.

Georgij had never met him. He read what there was and it took under an hour, and at the end of it he knew four true things about a man he was going to use.

---

Mr Ku drove him and did not ask, again.

---

The hall was on the third floor and there was a queue at the desk outside the room, and the queue was moving because these things move.

At the desk there is a book.

Everybody who comes writes his name in it. Then he puts an envelope in the box beside it, and the amount goes in the book too, in the second column, in a different hand, later.

---

Georgij stood in the queue and understood at about the fourth person what he had walked into.

He had known there would be a book. He had not thought about the box, because he does not think about money, because thinking about money has never once been useful to him.

---

He wrote his name.

It is not a thing he has done often. There were papers in the second week of October so that he could sign, and there was an instrument in December, and both of those were his name on a line about what he was permitted to do.

This was a book with a name in it and nothing else next to it.

---

The man at the desk had one hand out for the envelope and was already looking past him at the next in the queue.

"I have nothing to put in it," said Georgij.

---

The hand stopped.

There is a particular quality to that half-second. Contempt would be easier. It is a man recalculating what he is looking at, in a good coat, at a funeral, in a queue.

"That is all right," he said, in the end.

"It is not. I am telling you because you will write it in the second column and somebody will think it was a mistake." Georgij did not move out of the way yet. "It was not a mistake. Put a line through it."

---

The man put a line through it.

Then Georgij went in and bowed twice and came out again, which took about ninety seconds and which he did properly, because a room like that is the last one in which a man ought to be economical.

---

Park Sang-hoon was in the corridor at the end, near the window where the smokers stand, with two men who wanted things from him and were not going to get them today.

He had seen Georgij come out of the room. He had probably seen him in the queue.

---

He came over himself, which was the whole reason it had to be a room and not a telephone.

"Two billion two hundred million," said Sang-hoon.

"Chairman."

"I have thought about it since December and I have not moved off it, and I am not going to." He had a paper cup of something he was not drinking. "I have moved up. Say a number."

---

Georgij put his hands behind his back.

"He was never for sale," he said. "Five words. They were hers and not mine, and I was in the room when she said them."

"I remember who was in the room."

---

"And thank you." He said it without any decoration on it at all. "You have not moved off that figure from December to March. On the day you set it you gave her a reason for it, and the reason was a thing you had watched me do."

"I remember what I said."

"So do I. It is the only compliment anybody has ever paid me that arrived with a number on it. I have thought about that more than is useful. And I would sooner have had it from you than from anybody in this country."

---

Sang-hoon laughed at that, once, and two men down the corridor looked over and then looked away again.

"It was higher than the one that is on anybody," he said. "That is the part your side has never understood. I do not pay what a thing is worth. I pay what it costs to stop having to think about it."

---

Then he stopped, and he looked at Georgij for slightly too long, and he did not say the next thing.

Georgij watched him not say it, and had a fair idea what it was, because there is only one fact about him that a man in that position would hold back and enjoy holding back.

It went across Sang-hoon's face for about a quarter of a second. Then it was gone, and it was still his.

---

"You did not come here for a man from Mokpo," said Sang-hoon.

"No."

"You did not know him."

"I did not know him. I read four things about him on Sunday night and came here on the strength of them." Georgij did not soften it. "You come to these. There is no other room in this country I can walk into and find you in."

"You could have written to me."

"Then you would have had a day to decide what I wanted."

---

Sang-hoon put the cup down on the sill.

"Ask."

---

"You will have had somebody look in a book for you, in a building near the river." Georgij said it flat. "A man who puts a figure on a thing and does not move off it from December to March has been at the paper on it. I do not want what was on the page. I want to know how you got the look."

---

That was the whole of the ask, and Sang-hoon took about four seconds over it, which for him is a long time.

"You want to know whether the door is still there."

"Yes."

"It is there. It is not going to be there in a month." He was not enjoying himself, which was new. "They have a new man. He has been through the room where they keep it and he has been through the people, and the ones who were useful were useful because nobody had ever looked at them."

---

"Who?"

"Settlement." Sang-hoon watched two men go past with their coats over their arms. "There is always somebody in settlement.
