It is the only desk in the building that touches every line and gets paid the least for it, and it has been that way in every one of these places since before either of us was born."

---

"And the price?"

"For me it was not money." He turned round. "And that is what you have come for, so we can stop walking round it. What do you have?"

---

Georgij had known since Sunday what he had.

"You want to know how it was done."

"I know how it was done. I have had two and a half months and I am not stupid." Sang-hoon's voice did not go up. "I know the report was true. I know the board could not sell and I asked you about it myself, on page fourteen, and bought it anyway. I have been over that evening more times than I have been over anything since I was thirty."

He put one finger down on the sill.

"What I do not have is why I bought it. I saw the wall. I asked you about it out loud, at your table, and then I signed."

---

The window was open two inches at the top and the corridor was cold at that end and somebody in the room behind them was crying properly, without any performance in it.

---

"Why it was you is the half you have had since December," said Georgij. "You were going to take the Kim logistics and she could not let you. There is nothing else in that part of it. If you had been after a chemical works I would have gone and found you a chemical works."

"I know why it was me. I am not asking you that."

"No."

---

"Then take the rest in order." The two men down the corridor gave up and went away. "You would have bought Hanseong from anybody. Routes, cheap, bleeding, and half a board that wants out will sign inside a fortnight. That is your trade, and it is the only reason the thing could be used at all."

"And I would have looked at the other half of that board."

"You would have looked at it for ten minutes and walked away, and I would have had nothing." He did not hurry any of it. "You did not look, because it was never in front of you as a thing to buy."

---

"On the eighteenth of November you talked to me for three and a half hours about how you work," said Georgij. "You were pleased to do it. I did not ask you one question I had not decided on in a car on the way there."

"Go on."

"You told me that when you take hold of a thing you do not put it down. You said it as a virtue, because it is one, and it is the reason you have what you have." He did not stop. "It is also the only part of you that can be aimed. Everything else is judgement, and judgement will not go where a stranger points it."

---

"So I did not put it in front of you as an opportunity. I put nineteen true pages in front of you and asked you whether the whole of it was nonsense, because you had more idea than I did."

---

Sang-hoon did not say anything for a while.

"It was you being wrong," he said.

"Page nine." Georgij did not look away from him. "Two rumours about money missing at a subsidiary, one from a shipping agent and one from a woman who audits and has never met him. I had them as two sources and I put my weight on them. You wrote one word beside it."

"Same."

"One mouth, walked round the long way and back. I received it at both ends and counted it twice, and I was pleased with myself for four days."

---

"That is the part I want you to have straight," said Georgij. "It was a real mistake and I did not know it was there. I did not need to know. I needed the nineteen pages to be true and the asking to be genuine, and both of those were."

---

"I found four," said Sang-hoon. "In pencil. I read the nineteen pages twice to do it, and I said so to her at her own door, because it was worth saying."

"Then somewhere in the second reading you stopped correcting a report."

---

"Say what I was doing."

"Winning." Georgij turned his hand over. "You had it won by page eleven, and there was nothing to show for it except three words and a figure in a margin. A man like you does not leave a thing won and unpaid."

---

"You were not valuing an asset when you signed. You had finished the argument two weeks earlier and you were putting the last word on it, and the last word cost several hundred billion won."

---

"And you knew that on the eighteenth."

"I knew it about forty minutes in. I stayed for the other two hours and fifty because it was the best thing anybody has ever told me about this trade, and I was enjoying it. I want that said, because it is the part I am least comfortable with."

---

Sang-hoon looked out of the window at a car park.

"Then I gave it to you," he said.

"You gave it to me. I went and got it, and you handed it over, and both of those are true and neither of them cancels the other."

---

"You know what the worst of it is." It was not a question and it did not get a mark. "I would do the three and a half hours again. With you, next week, if you asked. That is not a man who has learned something. That is a man who found out what he is."

---

"The name in settlement," said Sang-hoon, after a while, "is not one I am going to say in a corridor with a funeral in it."

"No."

"Come out to the car."

---

They went down, and it took four minutes. What Georgij came out with fitted into one line of a notebook, and he did not take the notebook out until he was in the back of his own car with the door shut.

---

Sang-hoon had one more thing at the car door, and he said it as though it had only just occurred to him, which with him it never has.

"When she is finished with you," he said, "you will not come to me. I know that, and I am not asking you to."

"Chairman."

"I am telling you where I am." He had the door open and did not get in yet. "Four or five of these a year, until I am the one in the room. You will not have to write to anybody. You will not have to ask a man for a favour or stand in an office and be looked at. You come and stand in a corridor, and I will come over, and it will cost you nothing to find out whether I am still saying this."

"Why?"

"Because I have wanted to buy you twice and been told no twice, and both times I went home and thought about the work and not about the price." He got in. "That is the whole of it. A man should say a thing like that once and then not go on about it."

---

Jang met the car on the gravel, which he does not do.

He did not say anything and he did not need to. He had known since half past nine where a car of that house had gone. A hall of that kind publishes the room number and the three days. And knowing where the cars go is his job, and he has never once been bad at it.

---

Annie was in the small room with the door open.

Georgij stood in it with his coat still on and did the only arithmetic that mattered. It took no time at all.

He had come home to tell her. She had it first. There is no version of the next ten minutes in which he can prove the order.

He had said it out loud on Saturday. *On the day it starts to cost you, you will have all of it that day.*

The trouble with a man who has never said anything untrue is that he has also never had to be believed.

---

"Take the coat off," said Annie.

---

# Book Two · Chapter 5: The east side
*Lot Fourteen* · Version 1.5 · EN

## Day One Hundred and Fifty-Nine · Wednesday 11 March

He took the coat off and put it over the arm of the chair by the door, and then he did the difficult part first, because leaving it any later would have turned it into something.

"Jang was on the gravel," he said. "He does not stand out there, and knowing where the cars of this house go is his job. There is no way for me to show you which of us was going to say it first."

"No."

"I am not going to try. I would only be asking you to take my word for the one thing my word is no good for."

---

Annie was at the window with the curtain not drawn and the drive dark behind the glass.

"The order is not what is wrong with today," she said. "Give me all of it."

---

He gave it in order and it took twelve minutes.

The catalogue off the desk in the small room. Four references and three seasons and no column for who paid. Two of them the fourth of October, four lines apart. Two of them older, one three years back, one the year before that.

Then the outside of an evening. A firm in Jung-gu that keeps its dockets for seven years on account of a tax dispute it lost. A drop that is the same street four times in three years. A company with two rooms and a secretary and nobody behind it who had been trying very hard.

Then the name.

---

"Sunwoo," said Annie.

"On the older of the two. I have not got the other one."

"You will not." She did not say it as a challenge. "Go on."

---

"The fourth is not there at all. No car, no dinner, no third piece of paper. The chair firm invoiced for the room and the florist invoiced for the room and that is the whole of the evening."

"And you put that down to the records."

"I put it down to the age of the records on Saturday, and I wrote myself a line saying so." He did not decorate it. "It was reasonable and it is probably wrong. Nobody eats and nobody parks. That is not an old evening, that is an arranged one."

---

Annie let that stand for a moment, and then she asked her first real question, which was not about any of it.

"What did you pay Park Sang-hoon with."

---

"Hanseong. All of it. Why he signed after he had seen the wall, and how the report was put in front of him, and what it was in him that could be aimed."

"Every part of it."

"Every part. He had two and a half months on it and he had the half that anybody could get. I gave him the other half in a corridor in about six minutes."

---

She did not say anything about that at all, which she had not in February either, and he had learned by now not to take the silence as either of the two things it looks like.

---

"And in return."

"There is a desk in settlement. There is always somebody in settlement, because it is the only desk in a building of that kind that touches every line and gets paid the least for it." Georgij kept his hands still. "I have a name. It is not written anywhere except in the book in my coat, and the coat is on that chair."

"How long."

"A month. They have a new man and he has been through the room and through the people, and the ones who were worth anything were worth it because nobody had ever looked at them."

---

Annie turned round from the window then.

"So you have three days of records, one name out of two, a hole where the fourth ought to be, and a door that shuts in April."

"Yes."

"And it cost you Sang-hoon knowing that this house is interested in a book by the river."

"Yes."

---

"Now tell me about the desk at the door."

---

There it was, and he had known since the gravel that it was coming, and he had spent the drive deciding not to have an answer ready.

"There is a book at those things," he said. "Everybody who comes writes his name in it, and puts an envelope in the box, and the amount goes in the second column later in a different hand. I wrote my name. I had nothing for the box and I said so, and I asked the man to put a line through the second column."

---

Annie came away from the window and stood on the other side of the desk from him.

"How many men were near enough to hear you."

"Thirteen. Four in front of me and seven behind, and two came up the stair while I was still at the desk."

"And how many of them will have it in a month."

"The man at the desk will have it for years. He has never had to draw that line before and it is a story he can tell at a table." He did not stop. "Of the other twelve, most of them by Friday. And two of them already knew who I was when I said my name, because they stopped talking."

---

"You have spent nine days making yourself into the one thing in this city that has no line and no owner and no date," said Annie. "This afternoon you wrote your name in a register with a date on it, at the door of a room with half that trade in it. Then you made certain that every man within hearing would remember which name it was."

---

"Yes."

"Do not agree with me. Tell me why you did it."

---

"Because the other version has a figure in it." He said it in the flattest voice he owns. "An envelope is money that is not mine, out of a house that is yours, and it goes into the second column under my name in a book that is kept. That is a number next to me, in ink, with a date. A line through it is nothing at all."

---

Annie put one hand down on the desk.

"That part is correct," she said. "It is the only part of today that is."

---

"In December you gave six hours to a woman who does not belong to you," said Georgij.

"I know what I did in December."

"You made a condition about how it was to be done, and the condition was that it must not be a gift, because a gift would have made her poorer and more careful." He did not hurry it. "What it did in the end was not take the threat off her. Nobody can do that. It took away the thing that made her worth threatening. The ground stopped being a dedication and started being a rent, and the third charge on it stopped being a cliff in the spring and became a line in a schedule."

"The east side."

"The east side. That is the whole of the method and it is yours, not mine, and I have been doing nothing else since Thursday afternoon."

---

Annie waited.

"A page with four entries on it and one name known is a page with a queue on it," he said. "A page with four entries on it and all four dull is not worth carrying up a drive. I am not trying to take the book away from anybody. I am trying to make it not worth the postage."

---

"And you could not ask me first."

"No, Mistress. Not could not. Must not."

---

She did not move.

"Say the rest of it."

"The one thing in this that has any value at all is that nobody sent me." Georgij looked straight at her while he said it, because there was no version of it that survived being said to the carpet. "The moment you tell me to go at that register, it is your hand on it. You are the one person alive who cannot touch that book. Everything you do about it is an answer to a question nobody has asked yet."

---

"That is a good argument," said Annie. "Now say the flaw in it, because you have known it since the gravel and you have been walking towards it for twelve minutes."

---

The clock in the hall did the thing it does at the quarter, and neither of them looked at the door.

---

"I am the line," said Georgij.

"Louder."

"There is no such man as the one I have been describing. A page with four dull entries on it is worth nothing. A page with four entries on it, one of them attached to a man out of your house, is worth a very great deal. What makes it worth that is not the entry. It is that I have been walking round the city since March asking about it."

---

"And there is the second half of it, and it is mine and not yours," he said. "I have been doing all of this wearing a mark that is empty. Every man in that corridor read it and left me alone. Not one of them has looked."

"And this afternoon."

"This afternoon I made thirteen of them curious about the one book that would tell them there is nothing behind it."

---

Annie sat down at last, on the arm of the reading chair, which is not sitting.

"That is why I have not moved since February," she said. "It was not patience and it was not cleverness. There is no move available to me that does not confirm the line matters, and there is no man available to me who is not visibly out of this house."

"There is one thing that mends my half of it, and I am going to say it once so that it is not sitting in the room."

"Say it."

"You could have me put back in the book." He said it evenly. "A mark with something behind it works on everybody. A mark with nothing behind it works until one man checks."

"And."

"And it costs your name on a live page to do it." He did not look away. "I looked at it on Saturday and I put it down. I am telling you so that you do not spend a week wondering whether I put it down."

---

Annie was quiet for a moment.

"My people in Yeouido could have had the dockets in two days," she said. "They would not have written their names anywhere. They are better at this than you are."

"They are much better at it than I am."

"Then give me the reason and make it a real one."

---

"Every one of them arrives from somewhere." Georgij turned his hand over. "They come in through a door and a diary and a fee, and a man like the one in settlement can see all three of those before he has finished standing up. Nobody comes over to them in a corridor."

"And somebody comes over to you."

"Twice he has tried to buy me, and both times he came to me to do it. Today he crossed a corridor to say it, in front of two men who wanted things from him."

---

"So the asset is that other men want you."

"Yes, Mistress."

---

She looked at him for slightly too long, the way Sang-hoon had, and unlike Sang-hoon she said it.

"That is a filthy thing to have to put in a report."

"It is the only true thing I have that is worth anything to you. I would rather it were the records work. It is not."

---

Annie stood up off the arm of the chair.

"Bring me the coat."

---

He brought it and she did not take it. She waited until he had got the notebook out of the inside pocket himself, and waited again until he had opened it at the page. Then she read one line of his handwriting while he held it, and never put a finger on it.

"Keep it," she said. "It is no safer with me and I am not going to pretend otherwise."

---

"Now the terms, and they are yours and I am giving them back to you." She stood where she was. "A ceiling. Named counterparties. A date after which it is dead and you come and ask me again."

"Mistress."

"The ceiling is that you do not spend yourself again without telling me the price first. Not the result first and the price afterwards, which is what I have had this evening. The price, before it is paid."

---

"And where there is no time to ask?"

"Then you decide, and you tell me the same night, and you tell me what it cost before you tell me what you got." She let that sit. "You have put those terms on other people since October, and you put them on me on Saturday. You know exactly how much they leave a man."

"Enough to work with."

"Enough to work with."

---

"The counterparties are Park Sang-hoon, the desk in settlement, and nobody else without my saying so. The date is the last day of March, because that is when your door shuts and I am not going to pay for a month you do not have."

---

Georgij put the notebook back in the inside pocket and the coat back over the arm of the chair.

"And the paper?"

---

Annie went to the drawer on the left of the desk, which he has watched her open perhaps four times, and put a folded document on the blotter and did not push it across.

"Mr Chae has had this since Monday afternoon," she said. "It is narrower than the one in December. It names three things and a month and it will embarrass you in any room that reads it properly."

"Then it is the right one."

"You asked me on Monday and I told you to ask again when there was a room in it." She put two fingers on the fold and left them there. "There is a room in it. There is a door, a name, and four weeks. I am not giving you this because you have earned it this afternoon. Some of this afternoon was very expensive."

"I know what it cost."

"You do not yet. You will in about a month."

---

Neither of them picked it up. It stayed on the blotter between them for the rest of the evening.

---

"The fourth one," said Annie. "The one with no car and no dinner."

"I have nothing on it at all."

"No."

---

And that was all she said about it.

She did not ask him what he thought it meant, and she did not ask him whether he had gone at it twice, and she did not ask him the second question. Annie asks second questions. He stood there with his hands at his sides and listened to her not ask it, and did the arithmetic, and put it where he puts them.

---

"Come here," she said.

---

He went round the desk. She did not have to say the rest of it, and he went down onto the carpet at her feet, which has stopped needing a word between them. Then she put her hand on the back of his neck the way she had on Saturday. This time she did not go past him and did not go on to anything else. After a while his shoulders came down about an inch, and he had not known they were up.

"You wrote your name in a book today," she said, above him.

"Yes."

"Put a line through it, you said."

"Yes."

"That was the correct thing to do and it was quick, and nobody taught you it." Her hand did not move. "I will not say that twice, and you are not to build anything on it."

---

He said nothing at all, which was not a decision.

---

Later, on the landing, he found that he had spent the whole twelve minutes of the report waiting for one particular sentence and that it had not come. Not once, in any form.

She had not asked him whether he would have told her if Jang had been in the kitchen.

She had decided not to find out. On the eleventh of March, about a man who has never said anything untrue in his life. Downstairs on the blotter there was a folded document with four weeks on it and no room in it for a second mistake.

---

# Book Two · Chapter 6: I have to ask
*Lot Fourteen* · Version 1.0 · EN

## Day One Hundred and Sixty-Four · Monday 16 March

He did not ask one person about the new man.

A man who is brought into a house to clean it out has cleaned out other houses, and those houses leave paper. Three of them, in six years. Two are still trading and one is not, and the one that is not filed everything it was ever going to file.

From that he got the order it comes in.

---

It is always the same order and it is not the order anybody expects.

The new man does not start with the desk that handles the money. He starts with the desk that handles the paper about the money, because that is where a house keeps the things it has decided not to write down properly.

And the tell, four to six weeks ahead of it, is a request in writing: would you set out, in your own words, how your filing works.

---

Nobody at a desk has ever read that sentence and understood it.

They sit down flattered and write four pages and hand over the whole architecture of their own protection, and six weeks later a man they have never met knows which drawer to open first.

---

Georgij knew the shape of it because he had built one in December.

He wrote that down on the Friday, in one line, and did not soften it and did not look at it again.

---

He went over the whole thing on the Sunday with the three filings side by side and the dates in a column, in that order, before he decided he was going.

---

The house is a building near the river with a public stair on the west side.

He had been in it once, in October. He had come in at the back and gone down. He had been in the cellar of it for four hours with a number on him, and had not seen the front of the building until it was over.

He came in through the front door on the Monday at eleven and nobody stopped him.

---

He had settled that on the Sunday and did not open it again on the day.

In that building a clerk with a terminal can look up what is behind the thing round his neck. It takes under a minute, and what such a clerk finds is nothing. He went in anyway. There was no version of the month in which he did not.

---

Settlement is on the first floor at the end, past two rooms with nothing in them.

There is a counter with a glass panel above it and a gap at the bottom for envelopes. There is one chair on the near side, which tells you how long the average conversation is meant to be.

Mrs Jeon was behind the glass. She is somewhere near fifty. She has been at that desk since the house moved into this building, and her whole value for all of those years has been that nobody looked at her.

---

"I am not here to ask you for anything yet," said Georgij. "I want to tell you something first, and it is yours whether the rest of this goes anywhere or not. It will take four minutes."

Mrs Jeon had a receipt book open in front of her and did not close it.

"Four minutes."

---

He gave it to her in order and he did not hurry any of it.

The new man. What he did at the three previous houses and in what sequence. That he does not start with the money and everybody thinks he does. That the letter comes first, and what the letter asks for, and what happens to the people who answer it well.

Then the dates. Six weeks from the letter to the first desk, and about twelve days from the first desk to the second.

---

"And the second desk is this one," he said.

---

Mrs Jeon looked at him through the glass for a while.

"Why are you telling me this."

"Because I am going to ask you for something in a minute, and I did not want to be holding it while I asked."

"That is the same thing."

"It is not, and you know it is not, or you would not have said it in that voice." He kept his hands where she could see them. "You have it now. You can send me down the stairs and you will still have it. That was the whole reason for the order I said it in."

---

She did not thank him.

That is not ingratitude. She had come in that morning with twenty-three years behind the glass and a job. She was going home that evening with twenty-three years and about two months. A man in a good coat had done that to her before eleven fifteen.

---

"Ask, then," she said.

---

"On the back page of the tidied catalogue there are four entries marked retained and not disclosed." He said it as though it were a shipping schedule. "I want to know who settled the oldest of them."

"You have seen the back page."

"I have a copy of the back page. It came up a drive in a flat parcel with a printed label on it, and so did one to every buyer on their list."

---

Mrs Jeon put one hand flat on the counter and left it there.

"You know what you are standing in," she said. "You know what this desk is."

"I know exactly what this desk is."

"Then you know what it costs a woman behind this glass to answer that question."

"I do not." He did not dress it up. "That is why I am asking you and not telling you. Say the price."

---

She took her time about it, which was correct.

"I want a name," she said.

"Whose."

"Anybody's, so long as it is not out of this trade." She said it quite steadily. "In eight weeks I go out of that door with twenty-three years that I cannot put on a single piece of paper. There is no reference from this house that is worth anything anywhere. What I need is one person who will take a telephone call and say one sentence about me, and be somebody whose sentence carries."

---

Georgij did not answer.

---

He knew who he could ask before she had finished the second sentence. He knew what it was worth to her. And he knew that saying yes in that room would have closed the whole thing in under a minute.

He also knew what Annie had said in the small room on the Wednesday. *The price, before it is paid.*

---

"I have to ask," he said.

---

There is a particular silence that follows that sentence at a counter, and he had heard it before from the other side of one.

"You have to ask."

"I am not going to stand here and promise you a thing I do not own." He did not look away and he did not soften his voice. "The name that would do this belongs to somebody else. I can go and ask for it today and I think I will get it. I am not going to say yes to you on the strength of what I think."

---

Mrs Jeon looked at him for what was probably four seconds.

"Sit down," she said.

He had been standing the whole time. He sat down on the one chair.

---

"Do you know how many men have stood on that side of this glass and promised me something." She had not moved her hand. "I do not mean this month. In twenty-three years."

"No."

"Neither do I, and I stopped counting when I was thirty." She let that stand. "Nobody has ever said the other thing. Not once, at this desk, in twenty-three years."

---

"I am going to give you one thing now," she said, "and it is not the answer to your question, and it is not a favour. It is so that you do not come back on Wednesday and waste both of our afternoons."

"Go on."

"The oldest of those four never came through this desk."

---

Georgij sat very still.

"Nothing at all."

"No invoice, no receipt, no clearing, no line. I have been through it twice, because it bothered me at the time and it has bothered me since." Her voice did not change. "Everything in this house comes through this desk. That is the entire point of this desk. Somebody stood in that room and took a person home and this house did not send anybody a bill."

---

"And that is not a thing that happens by accident."

"That is not a thing that happens at all." Mrs Jeon took her hand off the counter at last. "Come back when you have your name, and I will tell you the rest of what I know, which is not very much and is worth having."

---

Mr Ku had the car on the far side of the street and did not ask.

---

He was home before three and did not start anything, which is not like him.

When the car came up the drive at twenty past five he went and stood in the small room, because sitting down in it would have been a way of putting it off.

---

"The price first," said Georgij.

"Go on."

"There is a woman at that desk who goes out of the building in eight weeks with twenty-three years she cannot write down anywhere. She wants one name. Somebody outside the trade who will take a call and say one sentence about her and be worth listening to."

---

Annie put the coat on the arm of the chair by the door.

"And you did not give her one."

"I have nothing to give her. I said I had to ask, and I came home."

---

"Say the obvious one first, so that we are not walking round it."

"Yours." He did not hesitate over it. "It would work in an afternoon. It would also put your name in that woman's mouth, in that building, eight weeks before she is turned out of it and has to explain herself to somebody. If anybody ever goes looking for a reason why this house is interested in that house, that is the reason, standing at a bus stop in Incheon with her handbag."

"Yes."

"So no."

"So no," said Annie.

---

She went to the window and did not draw the curtain.

"Park Sang-hoon."

---

Georgij had got there in the car and had spent the rest of the journey wishing he had not.

"He is on your list," he said.

"He is on my list because I put him there on Wednesday, and I put him there for this sort of thing and not for company." She turned round. "And he is the only name in this that explains itself. A man who buys and sells for a living has forty reasons to know a woman who does settlements. Nobody has to be told why he would speak for her. If it were my name, the only question anybody could ask is why."

---

"And what it costs," said Georgij, because she was not going to say it and it had to be in the room.

"Say it, then."

"It is the third thing he learns. He has the fact that this house cares about that book. He gets the fact that it cares enough to go to the counter. And he gets to be the man who fixed it, which with him is the expensive one, because he does not forget and he does not put things down."

---

"And it uses the corridor," said Annie.

---

He had given her that on the Wednesday in the middle of twelve minutes of other things, and she had kept the word.

"It uses the corridor," he said. "He offered it once and said he would not go on about it. This is not what a man means when he says that."

"It is exactly what a man means when he says that. That is why it costs."

---

"Do it," she said. "Today, not tomorrow, and not by telephone if there is any way of not using a telephone. And Georgij."

"Mistress."

"You will tell him what she is going to be able to say about him afterwards, before you ask him. He will say it does not matter. Tell him anyway."

---

He did it that evening. It took seven minutes. It cost precisely what he had said it would cost and produced precisely what Annie had said it would produce.

---

What he did afterwards, on the Monday night, was the other half.

A settlement that never happened is still a fact about somebody. A house that does not send a bill has been told not to, and an instruction of that size comes from an owner.

The house has an owner. The owner is a fund, and the fund is four companies deep, and the fourth one is registered at an address in Jung-gu that also holds nine others.

---

He knew the shape of it before he was through the second layer.

He had seen it twice in the autumn, both times from underneath, both times without anybody's name at the end of it. It is not the ordinary kind of nesting. Somebody had built it to be dull, and had gone to a great deal of trouble to make each individual step look like the sort of thing a lazy accountant does.

---

So he stopped.

He did not order the fourth company's filings. He did not send for the register extract on the Jung-gu address. He did not put a single request into a system with a date on it, and he closed the whole thing at twenty past one in the morning and left it exactly as he had found it.

That was the correct thing to do and he did it quickly, out of the same instinct that had made him say *put a line through it* at a desk on the eleventh.

He had no way of knowing that it was the second time.

---

# Book Two · Chapter 7: Not the first
*Lot Fourteen* · Version 1.2 · EN

## Day One Hundred and Sixty-Six · Wednesday 18 March

Mrs Jeon had the receipt book shut when he came in, which was the whole of her greeting and was enough.

"He rang on Monday night," she said. "At ten past eight. He talked for four minutes and he did not once ask me a question, which I think means he had decided before he picked up the telephone."

"He would have."

"He told me what he was going to say to whoever called him, and then he said it to me first so that I would know it was not going to be flowery." She looked at her hands. "It was not flowery. I have written it down."

---

"Then here is yours."

---

She did not make him ask twice and she did not stretch it out.

"A lot cannot leave this building without a bill unless somebody signs a release for it. One page. It says what is going out and who has authorised it, and it does not say who is receiving it, because the receiving is what the bill is for and there is no bill."

"So I get the man who let it out of the building. Not the man who took it home."

"You get the man who let it out. Nobody at this desk has ever had the other one, on that evening or any other."

---

"Three times in twenty-three years," she said. "That is all. Two of them are old and one of them is the one you are asking about."

"And the name on all three."

"Hwang," said Mrs Jeon. "He was Mr Hwang then and he is Mr Hwang now, and in between he was Mr Hwang at three other houses."

---

Georgij had read that name once before.

It was in six point at the bottom of a page, under four sentences about method, in the small room at the house on the second of March. He had read the four sentences twice, because they were the best thing in that book. He had not read the name at all.

---

"He was here as a young man," she said. "Then he went away for eight years and did other houses, and he did them well enough that people still talk about two of them. He came back in the autumn."

"And he is the new man."

"He is the new man. He is going to put me out of this building and I am going to tell you the truth about him anyway, because you have not asked me to say anything unkind and I would not."

---

"He is the most honest person I have ever worked for. He does not take anything. He does not have a favourite. He came back to a house whose records were a disgrace and he has spent five months making them not a disgrace, and he is proud of it, and he has every right to be."

---

Georgij did not write any of that down.

"He tidied the catalogue," he said.

"He tidied everything. That was the first thing he did." She said it flatly and without any idea what she was handing over. "Permanent references, one series, the whole thirty years in one sequence. Before him you could not find a lot from four years ago without knowing which of the four systems it had been entered under. Now a girl of nineteen can find anything in the place in about a minute."

---

"And the back page."

"The back page is his as well. He put the retained ones together in one list because they were scattered and because a thing you cannot find is a thing that goes wrong later." Mrs Jeon put her palm flat on the shut receipt book. "He is not a man who would use it. He would not know how. He made it findable because findable is correct."

---

Georgij stood in the first-floor corridor afterwards for about as long as it takes to put a cup down.

A man had come back to a house that was in a mess. He had spent five months doing his job better than anybody had ever done it in that building. In doing it he had built the thing that is now pointed at the woman who owns him.

There is no fault anywhere in that sentence. That is what makes it the worst one he has had all month.

---

"One more thing and then you should go," said Mrs Jeon through the glass. "He keeps everything. Every note, every release, every letter that has ever come into this house about anything. He is proud of that too."

"Thank you."

"I have not done you a kindness. I have been paid, properly, by somebody who told me the price out loud before he agreed to it, and I would like that written down somewhere as well."

---

Mrs Seo met him in the hall at ten past two, which she does not.

"She has been in the small room since eleven," she said. "She asked whether you were back, twice, and she has not asked for anything else."

---

The envelope was on the desk and had been opened along the top with something proper, not a finger.

Annie was standing behind the desk with her arms folded and she did not say anything at all. She turned it round so that it faced him and moved one finger about an inch, which meant read it.

---

He read the date first.

The eighteenth of March. He looked at that for a second and a half before he read a single other word on the page, and the whole afternoon was already different by the time he got to the salutation.

---

The letterhead is an association of six independent shippers on one lane out of Ulsan. It has existed for twenty years. Underneath the name of the association there is a telephone number and no fax number and no address for electronic mail.

The letter is typed. It is signed by hand, and under the signature it says *Chair*, which is the only title the woman has.

---

> I have written to six people on the first of every month for twenty years. I am writing to you on the eighteenth because I no longer have a first to wait for.
>
> I know what stands against your name in the records of a house near the river in Seoul. I know it twice. I know what the words on the back page mean, and I know that neither of them can be read by anybody who does not already know what he is looking at.
>
> I am not going to sell it and I am not going to give it to a newspaper. Neither of those puts one container on my lane. I have thought about it for four days and I am telling you the truth about it so that you do not have to spend a week working out what I intend.
>
> Since January my members have moved nothing. I have not been told why. Nobody has refused me anything, because nobody has had to. I am fifty-eight and I have run one lane since my brother died, and I have never in that time been unable to find out what was happening to it.
>
> I want an hour. I want the six of them in the room, and I want somebody to answer them. Not me. Them.
>
> I will hold this until the twenty-sixth of March.
>
> If nobody comes, I will write again on the first, as I always do.

---

Georgij put the letter down on the desk in exactly the position it had been in, square to the edge, which took him a moment longer than it needed to.

---

"Who gave it to her," said Annie.

---

There it was, and he had known it was coming since the second paragraph, and knowing had not made it any easier to say.

"I do not know."

---

He has answered every direct question she has ever asked him. He has declined to answer twice and said so out loud both times. He has never once stood in that room and had nothing.

"Say what you do know."

---

"She has the meaning and not just the page." He stayed on his side of the desk. "Every buyer on that list got a copy of that back page in March. All of them looked at four references and a phrase and put it in a drawer. Somebody sat down with that woman and told her what she was holding."

"Go on."

"She has never held anything outside this trade. She has no reason to know anybody who could explain it to her, and no reason for anybody who could explain it to go and find her in Ulsan." He did not stop. "And it arrived in the fourth week of the worst quarter of her life. Not in January when she could still wait, and not in June when it would be over."

---

"So somebody chose her."

"Somebody chose her, and chose extremely well." Georgij looked at the letterhead. "She is the one person in this who will use it properly and will not be believed to be anybody's. She has a constitution that lets her speak for six firms. She has twenty years of writing letters that nobody answered, which is the most credible thing about her. And she has never met whoever did this and never will."

---

"I said something about her on the seventh," said Annie.

"You said she was not somebody who was going to be told to be patient."

"I did not think she would be this quick." She looked at the letterhead and not at him. "What do you make of her."

---

"There is not one untrue sentence in it." Georgij did not soften it. "She has not made what she has sound bigger. She has not pretended to be willing to do something she is not willing to do. She has not asked for money in order to look reasonable. She has told you what she will do and on what date."

"And that is the dangerous part."

"That is the whole danger. A man who lies leaves you something to catch him at." He put one finger on the corner of the page. "She has left nothing at all, and she has done it deliberately, and she has done it because she is certain she is right. She is also right."

---

Annie looked at him for a moment when he had finished saying that.

"What," said Georgij.

"Nothing," said Annie.

---

Annie unfolded her arms and put one hand on the back of the chair.

"How long did you need."

"Four days. Six at the outside."

"For what, precisely."

"Whoever took that lot home. With the fourth name I could have made the whole page dull inside a fortnight." He said it without any weight on it. "I got the near end this morning at eleven."

---

Annie did not say anything to that, which was the correct thing to do and cost her something, because she is not a person who leaves a silence where a sentence would be kinder.

---

"I am not going to answer it," she said.

"No."

"Say why, so that I know you have got there and not just agreed with me."

---

"Because an hour in a room is the confirmation." He turned the letter a quarter turn so that it lay square. "There is nothing on that page that says the veto is yours. There is a phrase in a book and a name she has attached to it herself, and until somebody responds to it, that is all she has. The moment a woman from your house sits down in front of six shippers in Ulsan, everybody in that room knows she was right."

"And if I do nothing."

"Then on the first of April she writes again, and she writes to six people, and she has been doing that for twenty years without once being told to stop."

---

"So it is the same box as the book," said Annie.

"It is exactly the same box. Everything you touch confirms it. That is now true twice and it is going to go on being true, because it is not a coincidence. It is the shape of what is being done to you."

---

She went to the window.

"Then somebody who is not out of this house is going to have to go to Ulsan," she said, and she did not turn round, and Georgij did not answer that, because it was not a question and because she already knew what he was.

---

He went up at about six and got the notebook out of the coat and turned back to the fifth of March.

He had read the association's constitution that Thursday, in the middle of three days of other people's paper, and he had made a note about it in his own hand.

*Mostly nothing. One clause about who may speak for the members in a dealing with a carrier.*

---

He sat and looked at that for a while.

He had held the only piece of standing that woman possesses, two weeks ago, in a room in this house, and he had written *mostly nothing* next to it and turned the page.

He did not tear it out and he did not correct it. He wrote the date underneath and put the notebook back in the coat.

---

# Book Two · Chapter 8: Nobody sent me
*Lot Fourteen* · Version 1.4 · EN

## Day One Hundred and Sixty-Eight · Friday 20 March

He read the letter for the fourth time on the Thursday night and found the word on the fourth reading, which is late for him.

*I want somebody to answer them.*

Not you. Somebody.

---

A woman who has written the same six people once a month for twenty years does not put a word in a letter by accident. She had built a door into it and had not drawn attention to the door.

---

"She is not asking for you," he said. "She has been careful about that. She asks for an hour, the six of them, and somebody to answer them, and the somebody is not specified anywhere on the page."

Annie read it again with her thumb on the line.

"She is leaving me a way to send a man."

"She is leaving anybody a way to come. Which is not the same, and I think she means the second one."

---

"Then the price," said Annie. "Before, and not after."

---

"If I go, I am the house. Whatever I say in that room is what your house said, and she will be entitled to that reading, because I have not got anything else to be." He set it out flat. "And I cannot sign anything. The paper in the drawer covers a man at a desk near the river and Park Sang-hoon and one month. It does not cover Ulsan and I am not going to stretch it."

"So you can promise her nothing."

"I can promise her that I will argue for something. That is the whole of it."

---

"And if it goes badly."

"Then she has spoken to somebody from this house at last, and she will say so in the letter she writes on the first, and the letter will be worse." He did not soften it. "It goes from a woman with a page to a woman with a page and a meeting."

---

Annie put the letter down.

"I am not going to tell you to go," she said.

"No, Mistress."

"I want that to be true when somebody asks you. Not a form of words. True."

---

"It will be."

"Then it is your afternoon and not mine, and you will tell me what it cost before you tell me what came of it, and you will do that tonight and not tomorrow."

---

He went to find Mrs Seo at about nine and asked her how a person gets to Ulsan.

He has never in his life bought a ticket for anything. He said so, in one sentence, without arranging his face about it, because she was going to work it out inside a minute anyway and it is quicker to say things.

---

She did not make a business of it.

There was an envelope on the table outside his room in the morning. A ticket, and a second ticket for the way back. A card with the platform on it, and the time of the last train that would still get him home the same night.

Nothing else was in the envelope. She had not put money in it, and he understood that she had thought about whether to and had decided against, and he was grateful in a way he did not examine.

---

The yard is behind a fuel depot on the north side of the harbour road, and there are four trucks in it and room for nine.

Nobody stopped him at the gate because there was nobody at the gate. A woman of about thirty came out of the office with a clipboard, looked at him, and went back in without saying anything, and half a minute later Nam Byung-hee came out instead.

---

She is fifty-eight and she looked at him the way you look at a delivery you did not order.

"You are not from here."

"No."

"Say your name."

---

He said it.

It meant nothing to her at all, and he watched it mean nothing, and for about a second he was a man standing in a yard.

---

"And what are you."

"I work in the house you wrote to on Wednesday."

---

Nam Byung-hee did not move for about two seconds.

Then she looked past him at the gate, and at the road behind the gate, and at the place where a car would be if there were one.

"Where is your car."

"I came on the train."

---

"Did she send you."

"No."

---

"That is the answer everybody gives."

"It is also the true one. I told her on Thursday night what it would cost her if I came. She declined to tell me to go. She said out loud that she wanted that to be true and not a form of words." He did not decorate it. "So it is. I am here because I read your letter four times and found the word *somebody* in it, and it is not there by accident."

---

She looked at him for a while after that.

"Come in out of the yard," she said. "I am not giving you an hour. You can have as long as it takes me to be sure you are wasting it."

---

The office is one room over the weighbridge and there is a calendar on the wall with nothing written on it since January.

She did not offer him anything and she did not sit down, so neither did he.

---

"You know what I have," said Nam Byung-hee.

"You have the back page of a tidied catalogue and somebody has told you what the phrase on it means."

"Then you know what I can do with it."

"I know what you said you would do with it, and I believe you. There is not one untrue sentence in that letter. A person who is going to lie usually puts the lie in the third paragraph." He kept his hands at his sides. "I am not here about the page. I am here about the first paragraph."

---

He had looked at the desk when he came in, because he looks at desks.

There is one sheet in a plastic wallet on the corner of it, and it is a photocopy, and it is a photocopy of a single page.

Every buyer at that house got the whole catalogue in March. Bound, posted, a printed label on the outside of a flat parcel. What is lying on that desk is not what those people got. It is one page out of it, taken out by somebody who had the rest of it.

---

"How long have you had that."

"Since the fourteenth."

"Thank you."

---

He did not ask her who had given it to her, and he had settled that in the car to the station on the Thursday night and did not reopen it in the room.

She would have told him. She is a woman who tells people things and has spent twenty years finding out that it does not help.

And she would have understood, in the two seconds it took her to answer, what she was saying. That somebody came a long way to put one page in front of a woman in Ulsan. That he chose the page, and chose the week, and chose her.

After that she is not a woman with a grievance any more. She is a woman who has been used, and a woman who has been used goes and finds out for herself.

There is exactly one person she could go to.

---

She waited.

"You wrote that nobody has refused you anything, because nobody has had to." Georgij did not look away from her. "That is the most exact sentence in it, and I do not think you knew how exact it was."

---

"Explain it to me, then. Since January nobody else has."

---

"There is a veto," he said. "It is not a rule and it is not a tariff and it is not anybody at the port disliking you. It is a private clause in an agreement between shareholders. It does not exist anywhere you could go and look at it. The people who use it do not have to say one word to anybody in order to use it. There is no office. There is nobody to write to. None of that is accidental. It is what the thing is for."

---

Nam Byung-hee sat down after all.

"Say the rest."

"It is not aimed at you." He said it carefully, because it was the part he had to get exactly right. "You are not in anybody's way, and taking your lane apart buys the person holding it nothing whatsoever. You are the floor of a room somebody else is standing in."

---

"That is worse."

"Yes."

"I have six firms and forty-one people and one of them is my brother's boy, and you are telling me we are underneath somebody's furniture."

"I am telling you what is true. If you want it to sound better than that, somebody will be along inside a fortnight who can do that for you."

---

She laughed at that, once, and it was not a nice laugh and she did not apologise for it.

---

"So what have you come to sell me."

"Nothing. I have nothing to sign with."

---

He used to put a second half on that sentence. It was taken off him on the second of March, in the small room, by the only person entitled to take it. He has not found anything to put there since, and in an office over a weighbridge in Ulsan the bare half of it sounded like exactly what it was.

---

"I cannot move a container and I cannot lift the veto," he said. "Nobody has given me the authority to offer you anything. You would find that out inside a week, and then every other thing I have said in this room today would be worth nothing."

---

"Then why are you standing in my office."

---

"Because there are two things I can do," said Georgij. "The first one I have already done. Nobody had told you what was happening to you, and now somebody has, and that is yours whatever else comes of today."

"And the second."

"I will go back tonight and argue that you are not in the way of anything. If I am right about that, it costs the person holding the veto nothing at all to stop standing on you, and people do not usually refuse to do things that cost them nothing."

---

"And if you are wrong."

"Then nothing happens, and you have lost eight days, and you write on the first exactly as you were going to."

---

Nam Byung-hee put both hands flat on the desk.

"You want me to hold it past the twenty-sixth."

"I want you to hold it past the twenty-sixth."

"For a man who came on the train and cannot sign anything."

"Yes."

---

"How will I know."

"You will know because cargo moves." He said it in the same voice as the rest. "Not because anybody writes to you. Nobody is going to write to you, and if somebody did you should not believe it. Watch the lane."

---

She was quiet for a while and the room was quiet with her.

"Twenty years I have written on the first," she said. "Do you know how many answers I have had."

"No."

"Four. All four of them were from the same man and he is dead." She said it without any self-pity in it at all, which made it worse. "You came on a Friday to a yard you had never heard of. You have stood in my office for twenty minutes and told me three things I did not know and nothing at all that I wanted to hear. And you have not once said the word *unfortunately*."

---

"I will give you until the first," said Nam Byung-hee. "Not the twenty-sixth and not longer than the first. On the first I write, and if the lane is still dead I will write about all of it."

"That is more than I came for."

"It is exactly what I was always going to do. I have simply told you." She stood up. "Write your name down for me before you go."

---

He wrote it on the corner of a delivery pad on her desk, in his own hand, with nothing under it.

No title. No house. No telephone number, because he does not have one that is his.

She tore the corner off and put it in the pocket of her coat, which was on the back of the chair, and she did not look at it again while he was in the room.

---

The train back was the one on the card and it was two thirds empty.

He sat with his back to the direction of travel and set it out both ways, because a man who only counts what a thing cost him has stopped doing arithmetic and started feeling sorry for himself.

---

What it had cost is short and all of it is real. She knows there is a veto and roughly where it lives, and on Friday morning she did not. She has spoken to somebody out of that house, which she had not. And she has his name in her coat.

---

What he had came to three, and he had gone down there for one of them.

The demand had changed shape in that room. She had written asking for an hour with six men in it. An hour with six men in it is the one thing in this that cannot be given, because it is the admission itself. She had let him leave asking for cargo instead.

Cargo admits nothing. Cargo is a lane going back to what it was, and not one person has to say a word out loud in order for it to happen. He had gone down there against a demand that could not be met and had come back with one that could.

---

The second is lying on a desk over a weighbridge in a plastic wallet.

Every buyer at that house has the whole catalogue. She has one page of it, photocopied, and she has had it since the fourteenth of March. So somebody took a single page out of a bound book that had been posted to him personally, and copied it, and got it in front of a woman in Ulsan inside a fortnight of its arriving.

That is not everybody in Korea. That is the list of people who buy at that house, which is not short and is not public and is a very great deal shorter than everybody in Korea.

---

And there is a date on it, which is worth more than the wallet.

The parcel came up the drive on the second of March. The page was on a desk in Ulsan on the fourteenth. Twelve days.

Everybody else who got that parcel opened it, looked at four references and a phrase, and put it away. Mrs Sunwoo put hers in a drawer under the tax papers and did not take it out again. Twelve days is not how long it takes a man to notice something. Twelve days is how long it takes a man to use a thing he has been waiting for.

---

And then the half of it that is worse.

The back page carries references and seasons. It does not carry one single name. A page with lot fourteen on it is worth nothing whatever to a person who does not already know whose lot fourteen it was.

So he knew before the parcel came. The parcel is not where he found it out. The parcel is how he could hand it over without being the man who knew.

---

Georgij looked out of the window at a town he could not name for a while after that.

The list of people who know what was struck on the nineteenth of February is short enough to write on the back of a card, and he could write it now, and he did not take the card out.

There are three names on it that he has eaten at a table with.

---

And it costs him a thing he has been carrying since Wednesday, which is that somebody sat down with her and explained it to her.

He has no reason left to think that. A page in a plastic wallet does not need a conversation, and two lines on a covering note would have done the whole of it.

A man who arranges a meeting can be found afterwards. A man who posts something cannot.

He wrote that down on the back of the card Mrs Seo had put in the envelope. It was the most useful thing he had learned all day, and it meant he had spent two days looking for the wrong shape.

---

The third he had not gone for and would not have got by asking.

She had looked at his name and it had meant nothing to her.

Whoever did this gave her the page and did not give her the man on it. That is not an oversight. That is a decision, and a decision of that kind tells you what somebody is keeping something back for.

---

There is a fourth item and he did not put it on either side of the line, because he had not decided which side it belonged on.

A woman who is drowning does whatever occurs to her, on any morning, without warning anybody. A woman who is not drowning any more stays where she has been put.

---

The last piece did not come out well.

---

She has a photocopy of a back page with four references on it and a phrase underneath.

She has now also got a name, written out by hand on the corner of a delivery pad, at her own request, from a man who walked into her yard and gave it to her.

She does not know that they are the same thing.

---

Somebody will tell her. Not this month and possibly not this year, but somebody will, because the sort of person who found her in the first place does not do that once and then leave the rest to chance.

And on the day she is told, she will remember that she asked for it.

---

He got home at twenty past eleven and stood in the doorway of the small room. He said what it had cost, and then what had come of it, in that order. That was the ceiling she had put on him on the eleventh, and he had begun to see the point of the ceiling.

---

# Book Two · Chapter 9: What you paid for
*Lot Fourteen* · Version 2.1 · EN

## Day One Hundred and Sixty-Eight · Friday 20 March

"Three things," he said from the doorway. "None of them can be taken back."

"Stand somewhere else and say it."

---

He came in and stood at the corner of the desk.

"She knows there is a veto and roughly what shape it is. She has spoken to somebody out of this house, which she had not. And she has my name in her coat, in my writing, because she asked for it after she had already agreed."

"You gave it to her because she asked afterwards."

"A man who says no at that point has told her that everything before it was a performance."

---

"Say what you told her about the clause. The words."

---

He gave it to her, and it ran to six sentences, and she stopped him on the fourth one.

"Shareholders."

"Yes."

"You named the instrument."

"I named the kind of instrument. And it is a leak. You are right that it is a leak."

---

"Why did you spend it."

"Because a woman who has been told for three months that nothing is happening will believe a specific thing and will not believe a general one." He kept his hands at his sides. "She stopped arguing on that one word."

"It was the right trade," said Annie. "It is still a leak. Those two are going to be true on the same day rather often from now on, and you will hear me say the second one every time."

---

"Now the other side," she said.

"She wanted an hour with six men in it. She will take cargo instead."

---

He watched her get it, and it took about two seconds.

"Say it out loud. I want to hear whether you got there the same way."

"An hour in a room is an answer. Cargo is weather." Georgij did not sit. "There is no letter, no meeting and no minute. It is the first thing in three months that leaves no mark, because it is not a thing that gets done. It is a thing that stops getting done. Somebody has to reach for that clause every time it is used, and if nobody reaches for it on that lane there is no document anywhere that says why."

---

Annie went round the desk and sat down, which she does perhaps twice a month.

"Then that is what happens," she said. "It costs me nothing and I was never getting anything out of it. Do not look pleased."

"No, Mistress."

"You are permitted to be pleased. Do not look it."

---

"There is a fourth thing," said Georgij, "and it is a question, and I have been not asking it since the second of March."

---

She looked up.

"Ask it."

---

"On the nineteenth of February, what did you buy."

---

Annie did not answer immediately, and it was not reluctance.

"That is not a question you have to work up to."

"It is yours and not mine, and there was no version of asking it that was not me asking about myself." He did not move. "I am asking now because I have spent a fortnight looking at the back of that catalogue and there is something wrong with it, and I cannot see what until I know what you were sold."

---

"A deletion," said Annie.

"Say it the way it was said in the room."

---

"I went four times over seven weeks. On the third occasion it was not money and I am not going to tell you what it was." She said it perfectly evenly. "On the nineteenth a man sat across a table from me and I made him say it in a full sentence, because I do not take a nod for that sort of thing and never have. He said the entry had been deleted."

"Deleted."

"Deleted. I made him use the word twice, and the second time I asked him whether he understood what he had just said, and he said he did."

---

Georgij stood absolutely still for about four seconds.

---

"Then they did not do it," he said.

---

Annie put both hands on the arms of the chair.

"Go on."

"The list at the back of that catalogue is a list of retained entries. Four of them, under a heading, and the phrase under the heading is retained and not disclosed." He said it slowly, because he was walking on it as he went. "A deletion is the other thing. A deleted entry is nowhere. That is the whole of what the word means, and it is the only reason anybody has ever paid for one."

---

"Say the rest."

"Two of those four are yours. October and February. You paid for October as well."

"I paid for October as well."

"So they took your money twice and they struck a line through a page twice and kept the record twice." Georgij did not soften any of it. "You were not sold silence. You were sold a piece of stationery."

---

Annie did not move at all for a while.

---

"There are four on the list," she said at last.

"Yes."

"Two of them are mine."

"Yes."

"Then it was not something they did to me."

"No, Mistress. It is what they sell." He let that sit for one beat and no longer. "A house that does it once has a dishonest man in it. A house that does it four times in four years has a price list."

---

Annie stood up and went to the window and did not draw the curtain.

"I have been robbed exactly once in my life before tonight," she said, "and I was twenty-two, and it was six hundred thousand won out of a coat."

---

"There is a part of this that is not analysis," said Georgij, "and I am going to say it before we go on, because if I leave it until the end it will look like decoration."

"Say it."

"What they took the money for was me." He did not put any weight on it at all. "You went into that building four times over seven weeks, and you gave up something on the third visit that you will not name, and what you were buying was a line with my number on it. They sat on the other side of a table and let you do it, and they had no intention of doing it, and they will do it again to somebody in June."

---

Annie did not turn round.

"Yes," she said.

---

She stayed at the window for perhaps half a minute, which in that room is a long time, and Georgij did not fill it.

---

"Now say what it changes," she said. "You have had four minutes and I have had two. Say it first, because if I say it first you will spend the rest of the evening agreeing with me."

---

"Everything," said Georgij. "And I want to start by saying what I have been doing wrong since the second of March, because it is the reason we are having this conversation in the third week of the month instead of the first."

"Go on."

---

"I have been working to a danger and not to a target." He said it flatly and did not decorate it. "Make the page dull. Find the other two lines. Get the entry pulled. Every one of those is a thing I was doing so that something would not happen to you, and a man working like that is only ever as good as the last thing somebody else did. I have spent three weeks answering. Tonight I stop."

---

Annie turned round.

"Say the target."

---

"That house is finished by the end of April, and it is finished by its own customers, and nothing about it comes back to this door."

---

"How."

---

"Not a newspaper. Not a ministry, and not a court." He set it out in the order he had built it in while she was at the window. "The only thing that house sells is discretion. It has no stock, no premises worth anything and no name outside the trade. Everything it is worth sits in the belief that what you pay it to bury stays buried."

"And it does not."

"And it does not, and I can prove it to any one of its customers in about ten seconds, using a document they already have in a drawer." Georgij put one finger on the desk. "They all got the tidied catalogue. Every buyer on the list, in March, in a flat parcel. The proof is on the back page of a thing the house posted to them itself."

---

Annie came back to the desk.

"Sunwoo."

"Mrs Sunwoo. Eighty-one, careful since before either of us was born, and on that list. She paid for a deletion three years ago and she has been walking around ever since believing she got one." He did not stop. "I do not have to threaten her, persuade her or trade with her. I have to say one sentence and let her go and look at her own post."

"And then."

"And then she is not a person on a page with you. She is the second-largest customer that house has ever defrauded, she is eighty-one, and she has forty years of knowing exactly who else buys there."

---

"You are going to hand a woman like that a reason to talk," said Annie.

"I am going to hand her the truth about a thing that was done to her. What she does with it is hers. I could not stop her, and I would not want to."

---

Annie looked at the desk for a moment.

"That house dies of its reputation."

"Inside a season. A house whose business is discretion cannot survive being the house that sells deletions and does not perform them. Nobody has to sue anybody. The next twelve people simply go somewhere else, and the fund that bought it in the winter finds it is holding a building with a lift in it."

---

"And the page."

"The page survives. It is in drawers all over this city and it always was." He did not pretend otherwise. "But a page from a dealer that no longer exists, that nobody stands behind and nobody will confirm, is a photocopy of an allegation. Somebody will believe it. Nobody will be able to check it. That is the whole of what was ever available and I have stopped pretending there was better."

---

Annie sat down again.

"There are two people who are going to be hurt by this who have not done anything," she said.

"Three."

"Say them."

---

"Mrs Jeon at the settlement desk, who is out in six weeks anyway and who I am going to be able to do something about, because I have somebody who owes me an introduction and it costs him nothing." Georgij did not look away. "The man who runs it now, who came back to a bad house in the autumn and has spent five months making it honest, and whose work is the only reason that page is dangerous at all. And whoever is on the fourth line, who nobody has ever billed."

"You are going to be able to do something about a clerk."

"I am going to be able to do something about a clerk. I am not going to be able to do anything about the man."

---

"You have not put the woman in Ulsan on that list," said Annie.

"No."

---

She let that lie there and did not pick it up, and neither did he.

---

"Then say that as a sentence," said Annie, "because you will otherwise be back in this room at four in the morning wanting to warn him, and I would rather you had said it in front of me at midnight."

---

"He cannot be warned. He does not hold things back. If I tell him, it is in a file with a date on it by four the same afternoon, because that is what he is for." Georgij said it without any relief in it at all. "There is no version where he gets to know and does not write it down. All of the doors are the same door, and I am going to walk through the one that ends the house."

---

Annie let that stand.

"That is the first thing you have brought into this room since the second of March that was not a bill," she said.

"I know."

"Do not say I know. Say what you want out of it."

---

"I want the paper in the drawer extended," said Georgij, "and I want Mrs Sunwoo added to it, and I want it to run to the end of April instead of the end of March."

---

"No," said Annie.

---

He waited.

"Not because of you." She pulled the left-hand drawer open and took the folded document out and put it on the blotter. "Because a power of attorney with three names and a month on it is a document that describes a man doing errands. You are not going to walk up that woman's drive holding a piece of paper that says what you are allowed to do. You are going to walk up it holding nothing, which is what you are for."

"Then I have nothing to sign with."

"You have never had anything to sign with. It has never once been the problem." She put the document back in the drawer and left the drawer open. "Go on the Tuesday. Take Mr Ku and let him park where she can see the car, because a woman of eighty-one who is being visited by somebody on foot in the rain will be frightened and will not listen."

---

"Mistress."

"And Georgij."

"Yes."

"You said the end of April." Annie closed the drawer. "Say it again on the first, after that woman in Ulsan has written her letters. If it is still the end of April, I will take it as a date and not as a thing a man says at midnight."

---

He said it again on the first, and it was still the end of April.

---

She sat down after that and read for a while and did not send him away, and he ended up on the floor at the side of the desk with his back against it, which is where he sits.

Somewhere in the middle of it she put her hand down without looking away from the page and left it in his hair.

Neither of them made anything of that either.

---

# Book Two · Chapter 10: The third line down
*Lot Fourteen* · Version 2.0 · EN

## Day One Hundred and Seventy-Two · Tuesday 24 March

He spent the Sunday deciding what he was not going to do, which took about forty minutes and was mostly crossing things out.

He is not going to threaten her. He has nothing to threaten her with and she has been threatened by better men than him since before he was born.

He is not going to trade. There is nothing she wants that he owns.

He is not going to persuade her of anything. A woman who has been careful for forty years has a lifetime of watching men be convincing, and it is the fastest way there is to be shown the door.

---

What is left is one sentence and a piece of paper that is already in her house.

---

The house is on a hill on the north side with a wall round it and a gate that is opened by somebody who comes out to look at you first.

Mr Ku parked where the gate could see the car, on the gravel, at an angle, with the engine off. That was Annie's instruction and it was correct. A woman of eighty-one who is called on by a man standing in the rain is frightened, and a frightened person does not listen to anything.

---

A woman of about sixty came out to the gate and did not open it.

"There is no appointment," said Georgij.

"No."

"I know. I am going to give you my name, and then one more sentence, and if she says no I will go, and I will not come back and I will not write."

---

He gave his name. It did nothing, as it had done nothing at a gate in Ulsan.

"And the sentence."

"Tell her we haven't met."

---

The woman looked at him for a moment.

Then she went back up the path, and Georgij stood at the gate in the rain for four minutes with his hands where they could be seen from the house.

---

The room she receives in has two chairs at a right angle and a low table between them and one lamp, which is on at eleven in the morning because the window faces the wall.

Mrs Sunwoo is eighty-one and stands up when somebody comes into a room, still, and made a small production of doing it slowly so that he would understand it was deliberate.

"That is a very good sentence," she said. "I have been saying it for a very long time and nobody has ever handed it back."

---

"Sit down. You have some part of an hour. It stops being an hour the moment you say something I have heard before."

---

"Three years ago you paid that house on the river to delete an entry," said Georgij. "They took the money and they did not delete it. It is on the back page of the catalogue they posted you in March. It is the third line down."

---

He stopped there.

That was the sentence. He had built it on the Sunday and taken two things out of it, and one of the two had been the word *sorry*, which would have been for him and not for her.

---

Mrs Sunwoo did not move.

"Say that again."

"They did not do it. It is on the back page. Third line down."

---

She sat entirely still for about six seconds.

Then she rang a bell that is on the table, an actual bell, and the woman from the gate came in. Mrs Sunwoo asked her for the auction catalogue that came in March, the tidied one. It would be in the middle drawer of the desk in the other room, on the left, underneath the tax papers.

She knew exactly where it was. She had put it there herself and had not looked at it again.

---

Nobody said anything for the ninety seconds it took.

---

She turned it over and went to the last page and found it with one finger, and she did not need to be shown which line.

She read it twice.

Then she closed the catalogue and put it on the low table between them, squarely, the way somebody puts down a thing they are not going to pick up again in front of a stranger.

---

"Forty-one years," said Mrs Sunwoo.

"Yes."

"I have bought from that house for forty-one years and I have never once been given a reason to raise my voice in it." She was quite steady. "And three years ago I sat with a man in a room on the first floor and paid him a great deal of money for a word. I have carried the word around ever since like a woman carrying a receipt."

---

"Two rooms along from where you sat," said Georgij, "is a settlement desk. Everything that house does goes across it. There are four lines on that page and every one of them was paid for."

"Four."

"Four that this man found. He came back to the house in the autumn and tidied thirty years of records into one series, and he put the retained ones on a single page because a thing you cannot find goes wrong later." He did not decorate it. "That page exists because somebody made the house honest. Nobody in it has understood that yet."

---

Mrs Sunwoo looked at the closed catalogue.

"How many of the four are yours."

---

"None of them are mine," said Georgij. "Two of them belong to the woman I work for. One of them is yours. One of them was never invoiced to anybody, which is a different thing and I do not have it."

---

"That is a careful answer."

"It is an exact one."

"It is both, and I am eighty-one and I have not got the afternoon." She put one hand flat on the arm of the chair. "Which line is you."

---

There it was, and he had known since the Sunday that it was coming, and there is only one thing a man can do with a question like that if he has never said anything untrue.

"The fourth of October," said Georgij. "Fourteen."

---

Mrs Sunwoo did not say anything for a moment.

"I was two rows in front," she said. "I turned round with everybody else."

"I know. I found that out in the second week of March, from a docket, in a firm in Jung-gu that keeps them for seven years."

---

"And in October, in that ballroom, you did not know what I was."

"No."

"You do now."

"I know that you paid that house to take an evening out of a book three years ago, and that they did not, and that is the whole of what I know." He did not look away from her. "I did not come here with the rest of it and I am not going to go and get it. It is yours, and it has nothing to do with why I am sitting in this chair."

---

She looked at him for what was probably eight or nine seconds, which at that age is a considered length of time.

"You have not asked me for anything yet."

"I am not going to."

---

"Say why not, and if the answer is a piece of chivalry you can have your coat back now."

---

"Because there is nothing you can do for me that you would not do for yourself inside a fortnight," said Georgij.
