"You have been robbed by a house whose entire business is that it does not rob people, and it is worth nothing else. It has no stock and no premises and no name outside the trade. You have forty-one years of knowing who else buys there, and you can end it by having lunch four times."

---

"And your employer would like that."

"Very much. She has two lines on that page and she paid for both of them and got neither."

"So you did come here to ask me for something. You have simply arranged it so that I have to think of it myself."

---

"Yes," said Georgij. "And I would rather you said that out loud than thought it on Thursday."

---

For the first time, Mrs Sunwoo smiled at him, and it was not the ballroom one.

"You are much less charming than you were in October."

"October was work."

"I know it was work. That is why I gave you six minutes of nothing and went home pleased with myself." She sat back. "I have thought about that evening perhaps four times since, which for me is a great deal, and every time I thought about it I decided you had not known what I was. I was right. You have found out since, and you found it out from a docket in Jung-gu and not because somebody told you, and that is the only reason you are in this room."

---

"There is one more thing and then I am going to go," she said, "and you are going to want to argue with me about it, and you will not win, so I would advise you not to spend the hour on it."

"Say it."

---

"There were others before that page existed."

---

Georgij did not move.

"How many."

"I do not know how many. I know of two, and I know of them because both of them are dead and one of them told me at a funeral in 2011 and thought it was funny." She said it without any expression at all. "Whatever your man in the autumn found, he found what was on paper. That house has been doing this since long before anybody in it wrote anything down."

---

"Will you give me the two names."

"No."

"Then will you use them."

---

Mrs Sunwoo picked up the bell and did not ring it.

"I am going to use them," she said. "I am going to use them badly and slowly and to people who matter, over the whole of April. At no point will your employer's name occur in any sentence I say. Not for her sake. Because a woman of eighty-one who has been personally cheated is the most credible witness in this city, and the moment there are two of us it becomes a dispute."

---

"That is better than anything I came here for."

"Of course it is. You came here to tell me the truth about a thing that was done to me, which is the only approach that would have worked. I would like to know whether you understood that, or whether you simply have no other method."

---

"I have no other method any more," said Georgij. "I had one until the autumn and it has stopped working, and what is left is this."

"Then you have been very lucky."

"I have been very lucky."

---

She rang the bell then, and the woman from the gate came, and that was the end of it.

---

At the door she said one more thing, without turning it into an occasion.

"I shall not mention you to anybody. That is not a favour and you are not to put any weight on it. I have never in my life told anybody anything the whole way through, and I am not going to start at this age with something as interesting as you."

---

Mr Ku had the car where he had left it and the engine off, and had been sitting in it for fifty-five minutes without a newspaper, because he does not read in a car outside somebody's house.

---

Georgij did the arithmetic before they were off the hill.

He had gone in with one sentence and had given away exactly one thing, which was a date and a number about himself, to a woman who has never finished a sentence about anybody in forty-one years.

He had come out with a campaign he does not have to run, in a month he does not have to be present for. It will be conducted by somebody who cannot be attached to that house on any paper anywhere.

And with the one fact that changes the size of it, which is that what Mr Hwang found was only what somebody had written down.

---

He looked at the last part of that for a while and did not like where it went.

A house that has been doing a thing since before it kept records has been doing it for somebody. And the one line on that page that was never invoiced has been sitting there for a fortnight, being the only piece of this he cannot make dull.

---

He told Annie all of it that evening, price first, and there was not very much on that side for once.

She listened to the end and then asked one question, which was not about Mrs Sunwoo.

"Say the fourth line to me again."

"No car. No dinner. No settlement. The house carried it."

"Yes," said Annie. "That is the one I want."

---

# Book Two · Chapter 11: What she cannot prove
*Lot Fourteen* · Version 2.0 · EN

## Day One Hundred and Eighty · Wednesday 1 April

The paper in the drawer ran out on the thirty-first and was not replaced, and nobody in that house mentioned it.

He noticed it at about four in the afternoon on the Tuesday, the way a man notices that a tooth has stopped aching. For four weeks there had been a document in the world with his name on it and three things he was permitted to do. On the first of April there was not.

He went back to being what he had been on the second of March. No line, no owner and no date anywhere in the world. He has had a good deal of cause to think about that phrase since the night he first used it.

---

Mrs Sunwoo had lunch twice in the last week of March.

He knows that because he spent forty minutes on the Thursday somewhere he had no business being, which is a thing he has not had to do since October and had not enjoyed being out of practice at. He knows the rest because on the Friday a firm that has bought at that house since 1988 cancelled a viewing without giving a reason.

That is one cancellation. It is also the fourth day.

---

The letter came on the Wednesday morning with the ordinary post and Mrs Seo brought it up without stopping in the hall this time, which is how quickly a house learns a thing.

The second letter he has ever received.

---

> The cargo moved on the twenty-fourth and again on the twenty-fifth, and there have been four more since, and none of it came with a letter attached, exactly as you said it would not.
>
> I have not written to my members and I am not going to. You may tell whoever needs telling.
>
> Now I am going to ask you something, and I would like you to notice that I am asking rather than finding out, because I could find out and I have decided not to.
>
> I have your name in my coat. I have had it there for twelve days. I told you in my office that I had four answers in twenty years. Since you left I have worked out what I was really telling you, which is that I have stopped expecting to be answered by anybody.
>
> So. What are you.
>
> You told me you work in the house I wrote to. So does a gardener. You came on a train. You would not promise me anything. You would not let a man at a desk write a figure beside your name at a funeral, which I know about because I make it my business to find out why a person does an odd thing.
>
> I am fifty-eight and I would rather be told an unpleasant thing on the first of April than a pleasant one in June.

---

He read it standing at the window in his own room and then went down and put it on the desk in front of Annie without being asked.

---

"She has done the work," said Annie.

"She has done four days of it in Seoul without leaving Ulsan, which is better than I did in a fortnight in March."

"And she has stopped."

"She has stopped, and she has said so in writing, in a letter that she knows I will show you." He put one finger on the third paragraph. "That is not a courtesy. That is a woman telling us exactly what she is prepared to do to us and inviting us to do the arithmetic."

---

Annie read it once more.

"What are you going to answer."

---

"All of it. And not on paper."

---

She did not react to that for a moment, which is the reaction.

"Say it as a sentence."

"I am going to Ulsan this afternoon and I am going to tell her what I am, out loud, in that office, with nobody else in the room and nothing left behind on the desk."

---

"You could write it in eleven minutes and have it there in the morning."

"I could write it in eleven minutes and she could keep it for the rest of her life." He did not soften any part of it. "I have spent one chapter of this year finding out what it costs to put my name in a book at a door. I am not going to sit upstairs and put it in an envelope."

---

"Then say the price before you spend it," said Annie, "because that is the arrangement and I am going to hold you to it on this one particularly."

---

"The price is that she will know, and she can say it to anybody she likes for the rest of her life."

"And what does the train buy that the envelope does not."

"She will not be able to prove one word of it." Georgij put one finger on the desk. "That is a very large difference and it is not a complete one. A woman of fifty-eight with no standing outside her own lane, saying that thing about this house, is a woman saying a thing. The same sentence in my writing is a document, and a document does not need her to be believed. It only needs to be found once."

---

"And what do you say it buys," said Annie.

"Two things, and I want to be careful about the second one because it is the one I would like to be true."

"Be careful about it out loud."

---

"The first is that she stops looking. She said she could find out and has decided not to. That decision lasts about a month if I do not answer. A stranger gave her the page. If the only person who ever came to her yard then says nothing at all, she goes back to the stranger."

---

Annie put the letter down.

"And that would be the second time he speaks to her."

"That would be the second time he speaks to her, and this time she would go looking for him, and that is the one thing in all of this I would pay almost anything to prevent." He did not stop. "The second is the one I would like to be true. A person who has been trusted with something that could ruin somebody does not usually use it. Not out of goodness. Because using it turns them into a different kind of person than the one who was trusted, and most people prefer not to find out that they are that."

---

"That is a hope."

"It is a hope. I have said it out loud and I have called it what it is." He looked at her. "It is also the only thing I have ever seen work on somebody like her, and I have watched it work on you."

---

Annie did not answer that.

She went to the window instead, and stood there for a while, and then said the thing she said in a different room in March.

"I am not going to tell you to go," she said. "And I am not going to tell you not to."

"Mistress."

"This is not the same as Ulsan in March and I want you to hear the difference. Then I withheld an instruction because it was useful for it to be withheld." She turned round. "This one is yours because the thing you are proposing to give away is yours. It is the only thing in the world that is. I have never had a claim on it and I am not going to invent one now in order to be helpful."

---

He stood there for a moment with that.

"Say the rest of it," he said, "because you have got a condition and you are deciding whether to make it one."

---

"It is not a condition." Annie came back to the desk. "It is a thing I am telling you so that you have it. If she uses it, I will not be able to protect you from most of what follows. I want that said in a room with the two of us in it and no paper anywhere. In about a month I may be saying something quite different to quite a lot of people."

"I know."

"Say that you know it in a way that means you have imagined it."

---

"Three people have known since the autumn and none of them has ever said it," said Georgij. "Park Sang-hoon since the fourth of October, because he was in the room. Chairman Woo since the end of that month. Hana Seo-yeon since December, and she has never once let it into her face."

"Go on."

"Every day since then, each of them has chosen not to say it. That is what it is at the moment. It is three people choosing." He said it in the flattest voice he owns. "If she says it out loud on a lane in Ulsan, it stops being a thing three people are keeping and becomes a thing anybody may repeat. Then the three of them have to decide it again in front of other people, and that is a different question with a different answer."

---

"And that is what you have imagined."

"That is what I have imagined. The rest of it I have not, and I am not going to sit here and say that I have."

---

Annie looked at him.

"You have got better at this in eleven days," she said, "and I would like to know what did it, because I would like more of it."

"I stopped working to a danger."

"Yes. Do not lose that again."

---

He went to find Mrs Seo about the train and did not have to ask her anything.

There was already an envelope on the table outside his room. Two tickets, the twelve forty and the last one back, and a card with the platform on it. She had put it there before nine.

---

"You went down there on a Friday in March and came back the same night," she said, when he came into the hall with it in his hand. "You have had a letter from that town on your face since breakfast. I am not clever, I am organised."

"You are both."

"I am both. Take a coat that is warmer than the one you are looking at, because it is on the water and you will be standing in a yard."

---

The yard was the same and there were still four trucks in it.

She was in the office over the weighbridge and she was not surprised to see him, which is a thing he had not expected and which cost him about two seconds to absorb.

---

"You could have written," said Nam Byung-hee.

"No."

"No," she said, after a moment. "I suppose not."

---

He told her standing up, in six sentences, in the order he had worked out on the train, and he did not decorate any of it and did not hurry.

No title. No salary, no account, no property. Nothing that is his and nothing he can sign.

He belongs to her. Not as a way of speaking and not the way a man belongs to a firm.

He was bought at auction on the fourth of October. He is the fourteenth lot of that evening. The record of it is in the same book as the page in her plastic wallet, three lines above her own.

---

Nam Byung-hee sat down at some point during the fourth sentence and he did not stop for it.

---

When he had finished she looked at the desk for a while.

"That is why you would not let the man at the funeral write a figure next to your name," she said.

"Yes."

"I have been turning that over for a fortnight and I had got nowhere near it. I had you down for a man with a criminal conviction."

---

"Why are you telling me this."

"Because you asked plainly, and you are the first person outside that house who ever has." He did not move. "And because you have had nothing but silence out of everybody since January, and it seemed to me that one person should tell you the whole of something."

---

She got up and went to the window, which is over the weighbridge and looks at a fuel depot.

"You understand that I could say this to a great many people."

"Yes."

"And that some of them would work extremely hard to find a way of proving it."

"Yes. That is why I came instead of writing."

---

"That is the most honest sentence anybody has said in this office in nine years," said Nam Byung-hee, "and I am including me."

---

At the gate she said the last of it, and she said it without making an occasion of it.

"I shall not repeat it. Not because I am fond of you, and you should not go home and tell anybody that I am." She had her hands in her coat. "Since January something has been done to me by people who never had to say one word out loud. I have found out this month that I do not want to be that. And it turns out to be a thing you decide on a particular afternoon, and not once and for all."

---

Georgij did not say anything at all to that.

There was nothing available to him that was both true and sayable, and he has been in that position perhaps four times in his life, and it has never once been on the receiving end.

---

He got the last train and was home before midnight.

---

He worked something out at about one, at the desk, with the ticket stub in front of him.

A person who has been told the truth about somebody stops going and looking for it. He had said that to Annie at midday and it had been true when he said it and it is true now.

It is also what you do to somebody you are going to want standing still.

---

Both of those are the case. He sat with the two of them for a while and did not put either one in front of the other, and then he went to bed.

---

Nam Byung-hee did not write to anybody that week, or the next.

By the time she did, the house on the river had stopped taking new consignments, and it was not to Georgij.

---

# Book Two · Chapter 12: Sixteen days
*Lot Fourteen* · Version 1.3 · EN

## Day One Hundred and Eighty-Two · Friday 3 April

He went down to the small room at nine with nothing to say, and that had not happened since the second of March.

Mrs Sunwoo is having lunch with people and does not need him. Cargo is moving on a lane in Ulsan and nobody is going to write about it. There is no document in the world that says what he is allowed to do, and there has not been since Tuesday.

For the first time in a month he stood in that room and there was no report.

---

Annie let it go on for about forty seconds, which is a long time in a room with two people in it and nothing on the desk.

"Sit down."

"I am all right standing."

"I know you are. Sit down."

---

He sat.

---

"You have been squaring things off since the eighteenth of March," said Annie.

---

"Mistress."

"Paper. Cups. The blotter, twice, in front of me. You did it to that letter in this room on the day it came, and I watched you do it, and I have watched you do it about a dozen times since." She was not making an accusation of it. "You do not fidget. You have never fidgeted in your life. You put things straight."

---

"Yes."

"Say the date out loud."

"The eighteenth of March."

"Sixteen days," said Annie.

---

He did not say anything to that, and she let it sit for a moment, and then she did the thing he had not expected, which was to try to take it off him.

---

"She is fifty-eight," said Annie. "She has four trucks and a yard with room for nine and one lane, and since January she has had nothing. You went down there and took the water off her and she stopped. That is normally the end of it."

"I know what she is."

"Then tell me what is left over, because from where I am sitting the matter is closed and has been closed for a fortnight."

---

Georgij put both hands on his knees and did not move them.

"She was right," he said. "Every word in that letter was true, and I said so in this room, and I have not changed my mind about one line of it. She was strangled by something she could not see and nobody would tell her and there was no office to write to. If somebody did that to you I would take that person apart and I would not need a second reason."

"Then we agree."

"We do not agree about what she did afterwards."

---

"Say it."

---

"She thought about it for four days." He said it perfectly evenly. "That is in her own writing, in the third paragraph, and she put it there because she wanted me to know she had not done it in temper. Four days. Then she took a sheet of association letterhead, which is not hers, and she wrote to you by name, and she signed it Chair, and she posted it registered so that there would be a record of it."

---

Annie did not move.

"That is what a careful person does."

"That is what a careful person does when she has decided." Georgij did not raise his voice at any point in the whole of it, and it is possible that he could not have. "She did not write to a company. She did not write to a lawyer. She sat in an office over a weighbridge and she thought for four days about how to put a hand on you, and then she did it in the way that could be proved afterwards."

---

"And the other half," said Annie. "You have got two and you have said one."

---

He looked at the desk for a moment.

"The thing she picked up and pointed at you was me."

---

Annie sat back very slowly.

---

"There are four lines on that page," said Georgij. "Two of them are yours. The one she was holding is the fourth of October. She did not know that, and she still does not know it in the way that matters, and none of that changes what the object was." He stopped, and then he did not stop. "She went looking for something that would make you do what she wanted. What came to hand was me. She picked it up and aimed it and wrote a letter with it, and she took four days over the wording."

"You are the weapon in that sentence."

"I am the weapon in every sentence. I have never minded that." He looked up. "I mind it when the hand is not yours."

---

Nobody said anything for a while after that.

---

Annie stood up and went round the desk and stood in front of him, which she does not do, and looked at him for a long time in a way that had nothing sentimental in it at all.

She was checking. He understood that she was checking and sat still for it.

---

"You are not angry," she said at last.

"Mistress."

"That is not a correction, it is a finding. Anger makes people quick and rough and I cannot use anybody in that condition." She had not stopped looking at him. "You have been carrying this for sixteen days. In those sixteen days you went to Ulsan and took the pressure off the one person who could hurt me. You gave an eighty-one-year-old woman one sentence and no more. You went back down there and said the thing that costs you everything you have out loud, in a room, so that she would have nothing to keep."

---

"Yes."

"You have not made one mistake." She said it flatly, and it was not praise. "That is not what a man in a temper does. That is what a man does who has decided something and is waiting."

---

"Yes," said Georgij.

---

He stood up then, because what came next could not be said sitting down in front of her.

---

"May I have this one, Mistress?"

---

"Say all of it."

"I want her. Not the letter, not the lane, not an apology, and not a settlement." He kept his hands where she could see them. "I want everything she has, and I want it done by people who are entitled to take it from her, and I want her to be somebody nobody in that trade will read a letter from ever again."

---

"And the price," said Annie. "Before you pay it. You agreed to that on the eleventh of March."

---

"The price is the arrangement."

---

She waited.

"I cannot tell you what a thing costs before I pay for it," said Georgij. "Not in this. Half of what I am going to do, I will not know I am doing until the morning it becomes possible, and the other half will be answering somebody else inside an hour. If I stop to come home and name a figure, I will lose the week." He did not soften it. "So I am asking you to take the ceiling off. You will have every part of it afterwards, in one piece, in order, and you can decide then what you think of me."

---

"That is the whole of what you are asking for."

"That is the whole of it."

---

Annie went back round the desk, which took her about four seconds, and he understood that she was buying them.

---

"What do I get," she said.

"The lane."

---

She looked up at that.

"Say it properly."

"There are six firms in that association and one of them is hers. When it is over the other five will be yours, completely, not as customers and not as an arrangement." Georgij did not decorate any of it. "They are dependent, they are frightened, and in about three weeks they are going to be extremely grateful to somebody. That somebody can be you and it will cost you nothing you have not already spent."

---

"You have thought about this for sixteen days."

"I have thought about nothing else for sixteen days, and I have not written any of it down."

---

"And you are giving me the lane so that this is not you spending my month on your own feelings."

"Yes." He did not look away. "It is also true that I want it. Both of those are the case, and the second one is not the smaller of the two."

---

Annie put her hand flat on the desk.

"Two conditions," she said, "and neither of them is about her."

---

"You will not say one untrue thing to that woman. Not one, not to make it faster, not to make it land harder, not at the end when she asks you why." She said it very precisely. "She does not come into it. That rule is the only thing you have ever had that is yours, and I am not going to watch you spend it on a haulier in Ulsan."

---

There was a pause of about two seconds.

"I will not need to," said Georgij.

"No," said Annie. "I know."

---

"The second."

"When it is finished you will come to this room and tell me whether you enjoyed it." She held his eye. "And you will tell me the truth about that, and I am aware of what I am asking for, and I am asking for it anyway."

---

"Yes, Mistress."

"Say it as a sentence."

"When it is finished I will tell you whether I enjoyed it, and it will be the true answer and not the one that is easier to say in this room."

---

"Then go," said Annie.

---

He was at the door when she said the last of it, and she did not get up to say it.

"Georgij."

"Mistress."

"I want her finished. I do not want her hurt for four months so that somebody can watch." She had picked up something off the desk and was not looking at it. "Park Sang-hoon takes eighteen months over a man because he enjoys the second year. You are not going to become that, and if you find that you are becoming that you will stop, and you will not wait for me to notice."

---

"It will not take four months."

"How long."

"Three weeks. Perhaps four if the second one is careful, and he will be, because he has been careful since October."

---

Annie put the thing down.

"You have two of them."

"I have two of them, and I am going to say the second one out loud in this room now." He did not look away from her. "She is first, because she is the one who touched you, and because taking her apart is the only way I get to look at the other one from underneath. I am not going to have you told about him in three weeks as a thing that has already happened."

---

"Say it, then."

"There are two men at that end and they are going to get very different things."

---

"Go on."

"The first one decided." Georgij said it in the flattest voice he owns. "He took that page out of a bound book, out of thirty years in one series. He took the fourth week of the worst quarter of that woman's life. And out of everybody in this country he took her."

---

"And."

"He loses everything she loses. He loses it afterwards, in that order, so that he has a fortnight to watch it come towards him and know exactly what it is."

---

"And the second."

"The second one carried it. He is going to be frightened, and that is the whole of it."

---

Annie did not move.

"That is a distinction most people would not stop to make."

"It is not mercy." He did not dress it up. "He keeps his firm and his name and every won of it. What he gets is that on an ordinary morning, when nothing at all is happening, he finds out that I know who he is and what he carried and which week he carried it in. After that he looks behind him at night for some years and nothing else ever happens to him. That will be worse for him than a bad month would have been."

---

"And you want him left standing."

"I want him left standing, in his own house, in one piece and extremely grateful." Georgij looked at her. "He is how I get the first one. There is no road to that man that does not go through somebody who has touched the paper."

---

"You are certain there are two of them."

---

"No."

---

He said that without any hesitation at all, and it is the only thing all afternoon that he took back before she could ask him for it.

"I am certain there is one who decided. I have never in my life seen a man of that sort carry his own paper to a post office. I am going to work as though there are two until somebody shows me one."

---

"And when you have them in front of you, how will you know which is which."

"By the week."

---

"A man who is carrying a thing gets rid of it on the day he is told to get rid of it." Georgij turned his hand over. "That page went to Ulsan in the fourth week, and not in January when she could still wait, and not in June when it would have been over. Somebody sat down with a calendar. Nobody sits down with a calendar on somebody else's behalf."

---

Jang was in the gatehouse at about ten with the log in front of him and nothing to write in it.

He has run the security of that house since the autumn, on an interim basis that everybody has stopped calling interim. He has worked for houses of that kind for thirty years and never once for a company that made anything.

---

"I am going to need people," said Georgij.

Jang closed the log and did not ask what for, which took a decision.

"What sort."

---

"Two sorts and I will take them in order." Georgij put his hands behind his back. "The first is somebody who can find out what six small firms on one lane are actually afraid of. Not what they owe. What they are afraid of. And who each of the six would follow into a room if it came to a vote."

"That is four days and two men and it is not difficult."

"And the second is somebody who sits in a car outside a yard in Ulsan and writes down who comes to it."

---

Jang looked at him for a moment.

"For how long."

"Until the end of the month. I do not think anybody is going to come. I would like to be wrong in a way I can read afterwards."

---

"You can have both by Wednesday," said Jang. "Now I am going to offer you the third sort, because you have not asked and because you will not ask, and I would rather it was in the room than in your head."

"Go on."

---

"I know three men. Two of them used to do what I do and one of them never worked for anybody at all." He said it without any particular weight. "They are not thugs and I would not have them in this house if they were. They are people who go and stand in front of somebody, and that is a different trade, and it works about half the time and the other half it makes everything worse for a year."

---

"Not yet," said Georgij.

---

He did not say no.

Jang heard him not say it, and put the log back in the drawer, and neither of them made anything of that either.

---

"One question," he said, "and then I will get on with it. Is any part of this going to arrive at that gate."

"No."

"Say why not. I have had two men in thirty years tell me no to that question and both of them were wrong, and one of them was me."

---

"Because nothing that happens to her will be done by anybody who can be traced back to this house," said Georgij. "The people who take it off her will be her own six, and they will be right to, and they will believe it was their own idea because it will have been. And a woman who has just lost everything to the people she has written to on the first of every month for twenty years does not get in a car and drive four hours to stand at a gate."

"What does she do."

"She goes home."

---

Jang thought about that for a moment and did not appear to enjoy it.

"That is worse," he said.

"Yes."

---

# Book Two · Chapter 13: What it costs him
*Lot Fourteen* · Version 1.0 · EN

## Day One Hundred and Eighty-Eight · Thursday 9 April

Jang came on the Wednesday, two days early, with a folder and no heading written on anything in it.

He does not put headings on things. He laid it out on the table in the small room in the order he wanted it read, and he stood while Georgij read it, and he did not talk over any of it.

---

Six firms.

One of them has three trucks and a son at a university in Australia and seven months left on a loan he took against the yard. He is not afraid of losing the lane. He is afraid of a term beginning in September.

One has nine trucks, no debt at all, and a partner who has wanted out since his wife died and is being held in by nothing except that there is nobody to sell to.

One is two brothers who do not speak to each other and have not since 2019, and both of them talk to the younger one's wife, who does the invoicing.

One has been quietly moving work off that lane since February and has not told the association, which under the constitution he is not obliged to do and under twenty years of habit certainly is.

One is a man of forty who took the firm over from his father in the autumn. He has spent six months finding out what his father was actually like, and he has not said one word at a meeting since he arrived.

And one is Nam Byung-hee, who has four trucks and a nephew.

---

The last sheet was the one Georgij had asked for and it had five names on it in a column.

Who each of them would follow into a room. Not who they like. Who they would look at first if somebody in that room asked a question nobody wanted to answer.

Four of the five names were the same name, and it was the man with nine trucks and no debt, and it was not Nam Byung-hee.

---

"That is better than I asked for," said Georgij.

"You asked for what they are afraid of, which is the hard half, and then you asked who they would follow, which is the half people get wrong." Jang did not sit down. "They are not the same thing and I have watched men treat them as if they were, in four houses, over thirty years."

---

"There is one more," he said, "and you did not ask for it and I am giving it to you because it will save you a week."

"Go on."

"None of the six has met your employer or anybody who works for her. Not at a function, not at a port, not once." Jang put his hands behind his back. "There is no photograph of any of them next to anybody out of that house. I had that checked twice, because if I am wrong about it you will find out at the worst possible moment."

---

"And the man outside the yard."

"Since Monday. Nothing yet. He has a reason to be on that road and he is being paid by somebody in Busan who thinks he is doing something else, which is the arrangement I would want if it were me."

---

The terminal at Yeongjong is ten months old and looks it, in the way a thing looks new when the man who built it will not have anything on it repainted before it has earned the paint.

Chairman Woo came out to the car himself, which he did in October as well.

---

"The twentieth of November," he said. "That is the last time you stood on this ground. You telephoned in December and you did not come. Now come in and look at the second berth, they finished it in February and nobody has been out to see it."

---

They looked at the second berth for twenty minutes and Georgij asked four questions about it, and all four were real.

That is the part he could not have faked and did not have to.

---

"Now," said Woo, in the office at the end of the shed with the sea on three sides of it, "you want something. You have wanted something since you got out of the car and you have been extremely well-mannered about it for a quarter of an hour."

"Yes."

"Say it."

---

"There is an association of six independent shippers on one lane out of Ulsan," said Georgij. "Five of them are going to need work from the middle of this month and they are going to need it from somebody who does not care what the sixth one thinks. I would like it to be you."

---

Woo did not answer for a moment.

"Coastal feeder," he said. "Ulsan up to Incheon and back."

"Yes."

"About a fifth of my southeast leg goes through a broker in Busan who takes twelve per cent for arranging what he does not own. I have been irritated about him since 2021 and have never done anything, because the alternative was six telephone calls a week." He put one hand flat on the desk. "You are telling me the six telephone calls are about to be one."

"Yes."

---

"And the rate."

"At the floor, for the first quarter. They have had nothing since January and they have three months of it behind them."

---

Woo sat back and looked at him.

---

"You have not asked me for anything," he said.

"Chairman."

"That is not a complaint, it is a description. You have brought me a firm's worth of capacity on a leg where I am being fleeced, at the bottom of the market. You have arranged it so that I am better off on the day I say yes." He said it without any warmth in it at all, which is unusual for him. "You could have come here and asked me for a favour. I would have given it to you and I would have been pleased to."

"I know."

---

"You are not going to let me pay you back."

---

There it was, and there is only one thing a man can do with that if he has never said anything untrue.

"No," said Georgij.

---

Woo did not say anything for a while.

"Some men would call that a very cold way to treat somebody who likes them."

"Yes."

"Are you going to tell me it is not."

"No."

---

Georgij did not move from where he was standing.

"You are the only man in this country who has ever talked to me for three hours without wanting anything at the end of it," he said. "I am not going to spend that on a lane. If I ever have to spend it, it will be on something worth what it is worth, and I would sooner come out here four more times and never spend it at all."

---

"That is either the most honest thing anybody has said to me this year or it is the most efficient," said Woo, "and I have been in this trade since I was twenty-four and I cannot tell which, and that is a very rare condition for me."

"It is both. They are not in competition."

---

"Now tell me what it is for," said Woo, "because five firms do not need work in the middle of April unless somebody has decided that the sixth one is not going to have any."

---

Georgij told him.

He did not arrange it and he did not put it in an order designed to land. A woman on that lane wrote to the house he works in, in March, on association letterhead, signed as chair, about a private matter of her own that she had no business touching. He is taking her firm, her chairmanship and her standing, in that order, over the next three weeks, and it will be done by her own members and they will be right to do it.

He used the word finished. He did not use any of the softer ones and there are four or five available.

---

Woo listened to the whole of it without moving.

"And her people."

"Forty-one of them. Forty of them will be working for the other five inside a fortnight, and better paid than they were in December. I am not telling you that to make it sound better. It is the mechanism." Georgij did not soften it. "The forty-first is her nephew and he will be placed by name, at the nine-truck firm, and I will know it has happened because I will ask."

"And her."

"Her nothing."

---

The sea did something against the piles under the second berth and neither of them looked at it.

---

"I am seventy-five," said Woo. "I have finished four men in my life and I remember the names of all four and two of them deserved it. So do not stand in my office looking as though you expect me to be shocked, because it is discourteous and it is also wrong."

"I did not expect you to be shocked. I expected you to want to hear it from me and not in October from somebody else."

---

"Yes." Woo pulled a pad towards him. "Seven years."

"Chairman?"

"Seven years, the same as the anchor cargo, the same paper, and I will have it drawn by the same man. If I am going to put my name on that lane I am not going to do it for one quarter and then find out in August that somebody else has bought them with a better rate." He was writing while he said it. "You came here to ask me for something you thought I owed you. What you are getting instead is a customer, and I would like that on the record, because I know exactly what you are doing and I am going to let you do it."

---

Georgij did not say anything for a moment.

"Then you still have the three hours," he said.

"I still have the three hours. I am seventy-five and I would rather be owed than paid, and now we are two men who both think that, and one of us learned it from the other in November."

---

At the car, at the end, Woo did the thing he did in October, which is to give away something nobody has asked him for.

---

"Somebody was asking about that lane in January," he said.

Georgij stopped with his hand on the door.

---

"At a lunch. Six of us, one of those things the port authority does in the new year." Woo was frowning at a bollard. "A man asked me whether I had ever used the Ulsan feeder and what I thought of the woman who runs the association there. He asked it the way you ask about a house you are thinking of buying."

"What did you say."

"I said she writes to me every month and I have never once needed her, and that she is honest and that the lane is too small. He was extremely interested in the word honest and I did not think anything of it at the time."

---

"His name."

"I do not have it and I did not ask for it, because he was not asking me for anything and there was no reason to." Woo was still frowning. "That is January. Nothing had happened on that lane yet. It was still moving in January."

---

Georgij stood at the car door for slightly too long.

"Chairman."

"Yes."

"Who else was at the lunch."

---

Woo told him, and it took about fifteen seconds, and there were five names in it.

He got in the car with all five of them and did not write any of them down until he was past the bridge.

---

He worked the other thing out somewhere on the bridge and then sat still with it for about four minutes.

The clause was signed in December. It began to be used in January.

In January there was nothing wrong on that lane. Nobody on it had been hurt, nobody had complained, and there was nothing whatever about that woman to interest a man at a port authority lunch.

---

So he was not looking for somebody who had been hurt.

He was looking for the person who was about to be. And to do that in the first week of the year he had to know what had been signed in a room in Seoul in December, and he had to know it inside a fortnight of the signing.

---

Georgij put the notebook away at Yeongjong and did not take it out again in the car.

That is a very short list as well.

---

# Book Two · Chapter 14: Ask her
*Lot Fourteen* · Version 1.2 · EN

## Day One Hundred and Ninety-Three · Tuesday 14 April

Mr Kwon has nine trucks and no debt and a yard with a concrete apron that was laid properly. The apron is the first thing Georgij looked at, and the reason he went there on the Tuesday and not the Monday.

A man who lays an apron properly does it once and pays for it and does not talk about it afterwards. That is a whole way of being in the world and it takes about four seconds to read off a floor.

---

He came out himself because there is nobody to send.

"You are the man who has been asking about my drivers."

"No," said Georgij. "Somebody has been asking about your drivers and I am the reason he was, and I am not going to pretend it was anybody else."

---

Mr Kwon looked at him for a moment.

"Come inside then. I have got twenty minutes and a man coming at eleven who is not going to buy anything."

---

The office has a wall of ring binders in date order and a calendar with things written on it, which is a different calendar from the one in the office over the weighbridge.

He did not offer anything to drink and he did not apologise for not offering.

---

"Chairman Woo Jae-sung is going to put seven years of coastal feeder work on this lane," said Georgij. "He is going to do it with five firms and not six. He is going to sign them one at a time, and the first one he signs will set the rate that the other four are offered."

---

Then he stopped.

He had built that on the Sunday, the same way he built the sentence for the house on the hill, and he had taken two things out of it and one of them had been the word *opportunity*.

---

Mr Kwon did not say anything for about eight seconds.

"Say the first part again."

"Seven years. The same paper as the anchor cargo at Yeongjong, drawn by the same man."

"I know what that paper looks like. A cousin of my wife's carries under it." He had not moved. "That paper is why he has a terminal and everybody else has a berth."

---

"Why five."

"Because I am not going to bring him the sixth one."

---

Mr Kwon put both forearms on the desk.

"That is a very careful sentence."

"It is an exact one. I am telling you what I am going to do and not what is going to happen to her, because I do not know the second thing and I would be guessing."

---

"Then why."

---

"Ask her."

---

The room did the thing rooms do.

---

"That is all you are going to say."

"That is all I am going to say about it, and I would like you to notice that I have not said one word against her and I am not going to." Georgij kept his hands where they could be seen. "Ask her. She will tell you the truth, because she does not lie, and you have known that about her for twenty years and it is the only reason this is going to work."

---

Mr Kwon sat back.

"You are a very unpleasant man."

"Today, yes."

---

"I do not know what you are," he said. "You came in a car that costs more than my house. You knew about my apron before you knew about my trucks. You will not say what you want and you will not say who you are, and I have been in a room with two men in my life who did that and both of them were selling something."

---

"I have no title," said Georgij. "No company, no salary, no account, and nothing at all that I can sign. There is no piece of paper anywhere in this country with my name on it and a position underneath it."

"That is not an answer."

"It is the whole of the answer. There is nothing about me to check." He did not soften it. "Chairman Woo will confirm every word I have said to you about the seven years and about the five. He will not confirm anything about me, because there is nothing to confirm, and if he tries he will say something vague and you will hear him do it."

---

Mr Kwon looked at him for a long moment.

Then he picked up the telephone on the desk, which is a real telephone with a cord, and he did not go into another room to do it.

---

It took nine minutes.

Georgij sat where he was and looked at the ring binders and listened to one half of it, which is the half a man can afford to let somebody hear.

He heard Woo say the number of years, twice, because Mr Kwon made him say it twice.

He heard the phrase *a young man who works for somebody I do business with*.

He heard *I am not going to tell you any more than that*.

And after something he could not hear at all, he heard *I would not believe me either*.

He heard Mr Kwon laugh at that, once, and it was not a friendly laugh but it was a real one.

---

"He says the rate is real," said Mr Kwon, putting it down.

"It is real."

"He also says that if I ask him about you again he will hang up on me, and that I should ask you, and that you will tell me the truth and it will not help."

"Both of those are correct."

---

Mr Kwon got up and went to the window, which looks at nine trucks and room for eleven.

"My partner wants out," he said. "He has wanted out since his wife died and he cannot go, because there is nobody on this coast to sell his half to and I cannot buy it with what we make on this lane in a year."

"I know."

"Of course you know. Somebody asked my drivers." He did not turn round. "With seven years of that paper he can go. Somebody in Busan buys the half at a price that is a real price. He goes and lives near his daughter. And I stop watching a man of sixty-three come to work every day for a reason he stopped having."

---

Georgij did not say anything to that at all.

There was nothing available that was both true and useful, and everything available that was merely useful would have been a sale.

---

What he did instead was write four words in the notebook in the car, and they are the second half of a thing he said to Annie in the small room on the third of April.

Seven years of that paper does not only feed a firm. It makes a firm worth something. It makes a half of a firm saleable, and it does that to five firms inside a fortnight.

And Jang found the way into every one of them, in four pages, without being asked for it.

---

The man with nine trucks has a partner of sixty-three who has wanted out since his wife died and cannot go, because there is nobody on that coast who buys anything.

The man with three trucks has seven months left on a loan against his own yard and a son whose term begins in September.

The two brothers have not spoken since 2019 and would each of them sooner sell to a stranger than agree with the other about anything, and the invoice clerk has understood that for four years.

The fourth has been quietly moving work off that lane since February, which is a man who has already decided to go and is waiting to be able to afford it.

And Cho, who is forty, took a firm from his father in the autumn, and has spent six months finding out what his father actually was, and has not wanted it since about January.

---

Five men and five separate afternoons, and on every one of those afternoons there is going to be exactly one buyer on that coast with money in her hand.

She will pay a real price each time, because a bad price is a thing a man tells people about for twenty years. Every one of the five will believe it was the best fortnight of his working life, and every one of them will be right.

---

*Not customers. Not an arrangement.* All five, and not four, and not the two easy ones.

That is what he promised her on the third of April. It was not a figure of speech, and Woo's seven years are what make it possible, and Woo will never know that part. There is nothing in it that costs him anything and no reason to tell a man a thing he cannot use.

---

"You have not offered me anything," said Mr Kwon after a while.

"No."

"Why not."

"Because there is nothing I could offer you that you will not do for your own reasons inside a week. And because a man who takes something from me on a Tuesday spends the rest of his life wondering what it cost." Georgij stood up. "You have got a man coming at eleven who is not going to buy anything."

---

At the door Mr Kwon said the thing that Georgij had been waiting for since the apron.

"The constitution," he said. "Ours. There is a clause in it about who may speak for the members in a dealing with a carrier."

"There is."

"I have never read it. Twenty years and I have never read it, because there has never been a carrier."

---

"There is one now," said Georgij.

---

He got as far as the car.

"One thing," said Mr Kwon from the doorway. "You said ask her. You did not say what to ask her about."

"No."

"Because if you had, it would be your question."

"Yes."

---

Mr Kwon stood in his own doorway with his hands in his pockets and looked at him for a moment.

"I said you were an unpleasant man and you said today. Was that true."

---

There it was, and Mr Kwon had built it well and had probably not meant to.

"No," said Georgij. "I have been this for about three weeks and I do not know yet whether it goes away."

---

He got in the car and Mr Ku pulled out onto a road with a fuel depot on one side of it, and neither of them said anything for a long time.

---

Then Mr Ku said something on the expressway, about forty minutes out, which he has not done since October.

"That was a good yard."

"It was."

"My wife's brother drove for a firm with a yard like that. Twelve years, in Gimhae." He was watching the road and not the mirror. "Then it was a different firm and the yard was the same and he was not there any more."

---

Georgij looked at the back of his head for a moment.

"Is that a question."

"No," said Mr Ku.

---

He did not say anything else for the rest of it, and he was not sulking, and at the house he got out and opened the door the way he does.

Georgij carried that up the drive with him and has not put it down since.

---

He did not go and tell her about the day.

That is the arrangement now, and it is eleven days old, and this is the first week since October in which he has done a week's work and said nothing at all about it to anybody.

It turns out to be quieter than he had expected, and not in the way he had expected.

---

The four remaining firms did not need a visit and did not get one.

Woo's lawyer wrote to all five of them on the Thursday, on the same day, in the same words, and asked each of them the same three questions. What tonnage can you commit. What is your insurance position. **And: who is authorised to enter into an agreement on your behalf, and has anybody done so in the last six months.**

That is the third question every lawyer asks and nobody thinks about, and it went out to five men who had never in their lives been asked it.

---

By the Friday afternoon three of them had gone and read the constitution.

By the Saturday two of them had telephoned Nam Byung-hee.

---

Georgij does not know what she said on either call and did not try to find out.

He does not need it. She told them the truth, because that is what she does, and she did not soften it, because she has never once in twenty years softened anything.

It will have taken her under a minute each time.

---

The man outside the yard reported on the Sunday morning and had nothing, which is what Georgij had expected and had asked for anyway.

Nobody came. Nobody has come to that yard in a week who was not delivering something or driving for her. There is no car on that road that does not belong on it.

---

One line at the bottom of it, put there because the man is good at his job and not because anybody wanted it.

On the Saturday evening she did not go home. The light in the office over the weighbridge was on until half past ten and the yard was empty. At some point in the middle of it she came out and stood on the apron. She looked at four trucks for a while, and then she went back in.

---

By the Monday all five had written back to the lawyer, and four of the five had answered the third question in more words than it needed.

The fifth was Mr Kwon. He answered the third question in nine words. Then he telephoned the chair of his association and asked for a meeting of the members. In writing. Under a clause he had read for the first time on the Friday morning, at his own desk, with the door shut.

---

# Book Two · Chapter 15: One word, and it was true
*Lot Fourteen* · Version 1.0 · EN

## Day Two Hundred and Two · Thursday 23 April

The meeting was at two in the afternoon in a room over a bank in Ulsan that the association has used since 2006 and pays four hundred thousand won a year for.

Georgij was in Seoul, at the desk with the east window, and did not go anywhere near it.

There was no version of that room with him in it that was not the whole thing ruined. He had known that since the fourteenth. He had still spent about four minutes on the Wednesday night finding out whether there was one.

---

What he did with the afternoon was the other half.

---

A bound catalogue went out in the first days of March to every buyer that house has. One page of it was photocopied and was on a desk in Ulsan on the fourteenth.

Between those two facts there is a machine that somebody used, and machines leave marks, and the marks are not on the page.

---

The house uses a mailing bureau in Mapo, because a firm of that size does not put six hundred parcels in the post itself and has not since the nineties.

A bureau of that kind runs on a spreadsheet with an address list in it, and it keeps the list, because the client always comes back next season and nobody wants to key it again.

He had that list at about eleven on the Thursday morning.

It took him a little over two hours and he did not enjoy it as much as he had expected to, which he noted and did not examine.

---

Six hundred and forty names.

A hundred and nine of them are companies with no natural person on the line at all, and those he set aside. A man who is careful about four layers of ownership does not have an auction catalogue posted to a shell.

Of the rest, one is Annie. One is Mrs Sunwoo. One is a woman in Daegu who has bought porcelain there twice a year since 1991 and has never bought anything else.

---

And that is where it stopped, because six hundred names is not a list of suspects, it is a telephone directory.

He sat and looked at it and understood that he had been going at it from the wrong end for four days.

---

The right end is not who received a catalogue.

The right end is who received a catalogue and was also at a lunch given by the port authority in the first week of January.

That is a room with six men in it, and Woo gave him five of the names at a car door on the ninth of April, and he has had them in a notebook for a fortnight.

---

He put the five names next to six hundred and forty at about half past one.

Three of the five are on the mailing list.

---

Three is not an answer. Three is a shape.

All three of them are exactly the sort of man who would be at that lunch and would also buy at that house, which is another way of saying that nothing has been eliminated. What he has is that the two lists touch, and that they touch in three places and not in none.

He wrote the three on a separate page and put the six hundred and forty in a drawer, and he did not look at either again that day.

---

The telephone went at twenty past four.

---

"It is done," said Mr Kwon.

"Say it in the order it happened, and take as long as you like."

---

There was a pause of the sort a man makes when he has been asked for something he did not know he wanted to give.

---

"Six of us and her. She chaired it, because she is the chair. She opened it herself and read out the request for the meeting and my name at the bottom of it. She read it in the same voice she reads everything in."

"Go on."

"Then I asked her. I had it written down and I did not need it." He stopped. "I asked whether she had written to anybody on association letterhead in the last six months."

---

"And."

"She said yes."

---

"Straight away," said Mr Kwon. "Not a second. Not one of those things where a person takes a breath first."

---

"Then I asked her what was in it and she said that was hers and she was not going to say. And Gil asked whether it concerned the business of the association."

---

Georgij did not say anything.

---

"She said no."

---

"That is all she said," said Mr Kwon. "One word. She could have said it was association business and none of us would have been able to prove otherwise for a month, and by then it would have been over. She had a way out and it was standing open and she looked straight at it and said no."

"She does not lie."

"She does not lie. I have known that for twenty years and I have never once had it cost me anything." He said that part quite slowly. "It cost her everything in about a second and a half and she did not even look surprised."

---

The room over the bank in Ulsan did the thing rooms do.

---

"After that it was not really a discussion," said Mr Kwon. "Somebody has to say the words, so I said them. That she had used the one thing the six of us own together, for something that was not ours, and had not told us for five weeks."

"And she said."

"She said that was correct."

---

"Four for. One against. And Cho abstained."

---

"Cho is the man of forty," said Georgij.

"Cho is the man of forty who has not said one word at a meeting since October, and he said one today." Mr Kwon's voice did something. "He said that she had written to six people on the first of every month for twenty years, and that nobody in that room had ever once written back." Mr Kwon stopped. "And that he did not think a person should lose everything for the first thing she ever asked for herself."

---

"And then he abstained."

"And then he abstained. He said the words and then he put his hand down and did not vote. I have been thinking about it for two hours and I cannot decide whether it was the most honest thing I have ever seen a man do or the most useless."

---

"Both," said Georgij. "They are not in competition."

---

He did not say the rest of it.

A man who says a true thing in a room and then does not act on it has made himself feel better and changed nothing whatever. It is the commonest thing in the world. He has used it in nine rooms since October and has never once had to work for it.

---

"Who is chair," he said.

"Me. I did not want it and there was nobody else and it took four minutes."

---

"And her firm."

"Her firm is a member. It is still a member. Nobody moved to expel her and I would have voted against it if they had." Mr Kwon said it flatly. "She has four trucks and one lane and no work on it. The rest of us are about to have seven years. Everybody in that room understood exactly what that means, and not one of us said it out loud."

---

"No," said Georgij. "They never do."

---

There was a longer pause after that.

---

"You were not there," said Mr Kwon.

"No."

"You were not there and it took forty minutes and every single thing that happened in that room happened because a man came to my yard on a Tuesday and said two words to me."

"Three, if you count the first one."

"Do not do that." It came out harder than the rest of it. "I have got nine trucks and a partner who can go and live near his daughter. I am going to sign that paper on Monday and be pleased about it for about a year. Do not stand at the other end of a telephone and be clever at me about it."

---

"I am sorry," said Georgij.

---

He does not say that often, and he had not planned to, and it came out before he had decided whether it was for Mr Kwon or for himself, which is the reason he almost never says it.

---

"What did she do afterwards," he said.

"She stayed and helped stack the chairs. It is what she has done after every meeting for twenty years and I do not think it occurred to her to do anything else." Mr Kwon stopped. "Then she said goodnight to each of us by name, and she said mine last, and she has never done that before, and she was not being pointed. I do not think she is capable of being pointed."

---

"Thank you," said Georgij.

"I do not want to be thanked by you."

"No."

"I will take the seven years and I will not take the thanks, and I would like that to be the arrangement between us from now on."

"That is the arrangement."

---

He put the telephone down and sat with the chairs for a while, which he had not intended to do.

A woman loses everything at two in the afternoon and then stays behind and stacks the chairs, because it is a Thursday and that is what happens after a meeting, and there is no procedure anywhere for stopping.

---

He has watched a great many people take bad news since October and he can predict most of what they do with the first ten minutes of it.

He did not predict that, and he cannot make it be anything other than what it is, and it does not alter one single thing about the rest of the month.

---

He wrote the date in the notebook after that, and underneath it, in five words: *four for, one against, one abstained.*

He did not write down what Cho said. He could have, and he can still remember the whole of it.

A thing that goes in the notebook stops having to be carried. That is the entire point of the notebook and he has known it since October, and he left it out anyway.

---

He went down at about seven.

Annie was in the small room and the door was open and she looked up when he came into it, and he had not been into that room to say anything since the third of April.

"It is not finished," he said. "That was the first half and it took twenty days."

"I know. I have had the Yeouido people watching the filings since Tuesday and I did not tell you, because you would have wanted to know why and the answer is that I wanted to see it happen." She put down what she was holding. "Sit down and do not report to me. You do not have to and I have not asked."

---

He sat down on the floor at the side of the desk, which is where he sits.

"There is a man called Cho," he said, "and he is forty, and he said the only true thing in that room, and then he put his hand down and did not vote."

---

Annie did not answer for a moment.

"Do you want me to say something about that."

"No, Mistress."

"Then I will not," she said, and went back to what she was doing, and left her hand where it was for the next hour.

---

# Book Two · Chapter 16: A hand
*Lot Fourteen* · Version 1.0 · EN

## Day Two Hundred and Four · Saturday 25 April

The house on the river stopped taking new consignments on the twentieth of April.

There was no announcement, because a firm of that kind does not announce anything. It tells the fourth person who telephones that the spring sale is under review, and by the eighth person it has stopped being a rumour.

---

Mrs Sunwoo has had a dozen lunches since the twenty-fourth of March.

Georgij knows the number because it is in a diary that a woman of eighty-one has never in her life hidden from anybody. He has looked at it twice and both times felt something he decided not to name.

Two of the people she has eaten with are on the mailing list, and one of those two is on the port authority's January list as well. He did not do anything with that. A coincidence in a city of that size is not a fact, and a man who forgets that ends up with the wrong person.

---

Three names, and two of them came off on the Friday evening without anything clever being done to them at all.

---

The first is a man who owns two feeder vessels and has been trying to buy small operators on that coast for six years, and everybody in the trade knows it, including the small operators. He asks about lanes the way a man with a bag asks about houses. If he had put a page in front of Nam Byung-hee she would have telephoned four people about it before dinner.

The second sits on the port authority's own committee and has done since 2014, which means he was at that lunch because it is his lunch. A man who is at a thing because it belongs to him does not use it to ask a question he wants nobody to remember.

---

The third is Mr Ok.

---

He is fifty-six. He runs a marine insurance brokerage that his father ran before him, from two floors of a building in Jung-gu that the family owns and does not let. Eleven staff. He has bought at that house since 2003, mostly ceramics, occasionally a scroll, never anything anybody would talk about.

He has no interest in coastal feeder capacity. He does not broker it, insure much of it, or own any of it. There is no lane in this country that his business would be better or worse off for.

He was at the lunch because somebody at the port authority puts the same forty people on the same list every January, and he goes because his father went.

---

That is the whole of it and it took Georgij about an hour to establish, and it is not evidence of anything.

A man is allowed to ask a question at a lunch.

---

What he did on the Saturday morning is the thing he has not done properly since October, and he had been putting it off for a week without admitting that was what he was doing.

---

He is not going to write down how, and it does not matter.

A brokerage of eleven people that has been in the same two floors since 1978 keeps everything in one place, because that is what a firm does when nobody has ever forced it to think about the question. There is a machine in the corridor that scans, copies and sends. It has been there since 2019. It keeps a log, because everything of that kind keeps a log, and nobody in that building has ever once looked at it.

It took him fifty minutes and most of that was reading.

---

The catalogue came into that office on the third of March, because the mailing bureau's list has the office address on it and not the house.

On the twelfth of March at four forty in the afternoon, one page was put on the glass of the machine in the corridor and copied twice.

---

The log says the page was copied. It does not say what happened to the copies after that, because a machine in a corridor does not follow paper out of a building.

Somebody put one of them in an envelope. That is in no log anywhere and it never will be, and it is the reason a page arrives in Ulsan and a man in Jung-gu has no idea that he has done anything at all.

---

Twice.

He sat with that for a moment, because two copies is a fact and one copy is an errand.

---

The account the machine was signed in under belongs to a woman who has done the filing in that office since 2004.

She copied it at four forty on a Thursday, in a corridor, on the machine everybody uses. She did not think about it for one second. A person who is asked to copy a page copies the page.

---

Georgij went back through the log for six months either side and found nothing else that was not invoices, survey reports and one birthday card.

Then he went and looked at what else that office holds, which took the rest of the morning, and the finding is an absence and it is the whole chapter.

---

There is no instruction anywhere.

No message, no note, no letter, no entry in a diary. Nothing on the eleventh, nothing on the tenth, nothing in the week before it. There is no lunch in the calendar that is not one of the four he has every month with the same three men. There is no telephone number in that building that received a call it should not have.

The page went on the glass at four forty on the twelfth of March and nothing at all happened in that office before it.

---

Georgij sat back from the desk at about half past one on the Saturday and understood what he had.

---

In the small room on the third of April he had said it out loud to Annie, and she had made him say it twice.

*A man who is carrying a thing gets rid of it on the day he is told to. Somebody sat down with a calendar. Nobody sits down with a calendar on somebody else's behalf.*

---

There is no calendar in that office.

Mr Ok did not choose the week. Mr Ok did not choose the page and did not choose Nam Byung-hee and almost certainly could not tell you today what an association chair is. Somebody asked him at a lunch in January whether he knew that coast, and he said what a man says. Eight weeks later somebody asked him for a favour that cost a woman in his corridor about four minutes.

---

He is a hand.

---

Georgij sat with that for a long time and did not enjoy any of it.

He had wanted a man to take apart. He had said so in that room, in front of her, in the flattest voice he owns, and he had been carrying it since the eighteenth of March, and it had been the shape of his whole month.

What he has is a broker of fifty-six who buys ceramics and does what he is asked by somebody at a lunch.

---

He wrote it down at about two, because a thing that is not written down has to be carried and he has been carrying enough.

*Ok. A hand. Nothing before the twelfth. Frightened only.*

He looked at the last two words for a while and then did not change them.

---

That is his own rule and he made it in front of her before he knew who it was going to protect, which is the only condition under which a rule is worth anything at all.

If he takes that man apart now, he is a man who said a thing in a room and meant it right up until it was inconvenient. He has watched forty people do that since October and he has used every single one of them.

---

There is a second thing in it and it took him until about three to see it, and it is worse than the first.

---

Two copies.

A page that is copied twice at four forty on a Thursday afternoon is a page that goes to two places.

One of them was on a desk in Ulsan on the fourteenth.

---

And a man who makes two copies of a thing is a man who does not expect the first one to be enough.

Whatever he thought he was setting off in Ulsan, he had already decided in March that she might not do it, and that it would not particularly matter if she did not.

---

He does not know where the other one went.

He does not know whether it has been used or is going to be. It may be sitting in a drawer in a house somewhere in this country, belonging to somebody who has been waiting since March for a reason to open it.

---

That is the first thing in eight weeks that has actually frightened him.

---

He went down at about six and stood in the doorway of the small room, and Annie looked up and saw his face and put down what she was holding before he had said anything at all.

"Say the bad one first," she said.

---

"There is a second copy," said Georgij. "It was made on the same afternoon on the same machine and I do not know where it is."

---

Annie did not move for a moment.

"And the good one."

"There is no good one. There is a smaller one." He came into the room. "I have the man who made the copies. He is a broker in Jung-gu who buys porcelain and he did not choose anything. There is no instruction in that building anywhere, and there is nothing in his calendar, and by my own test he is a hand."

"By your own test that you gave me on the third of April."

"Yes."

---

"Say what you are going to do about him."

---

"Frighten him," said Georgij. "Nothing else. Not his firm, not his family and not one won."

---

Annie looked at him for a while.

"You have wanted somebody for five weeks."

"I have wanted somebody for five weeks and I have got a man who copies pages in a corridor." He did not soften it. "If I take him apart because I have been carrying something since March, then I am doing it for me, and I told you on the third of April that I would tell you the truth about that afterwards. This is the afterwards arriving early."

---

She did not say anything for a moment.

"Then that is the second thing you have brought me in a month that I did not have to ask for," said Annie. "Sit down. Tell me about the second copy from the beginning, and do not leave out the part where you are frightened, because I would rather have it from you than watch you carry it around the house for a fortnight."

---

He sat down on the floor at the side of the desk, which is where he sits, and told her the whole of it, including that part.

---

And somewhere in the middle of it, without stopping, he understood what the second copy actually means, and he did not say it out loud until he had gone through it twice.

---

The man who chose the week did not want one page delivered.

He wanted two, from a machine in a corridor in a building he has never entered, by a woman who will never be able to say who asked her, on a Thursday afternoon in March.

One of them went to a woman in Ulsan who was drowning and who does not lie.

The other one is somewhere, and it has been somewhere for six weeks, and it did not have to be used yet.

---

# Book Two · Chapter 17: Four minutes of somebody's time
*Lot Fourteen* · Version 1.0 · EN

## Day Two Hundred and Six · Monday 27 April

Mr Ok leaves his building at twenty past six and walks to the corner and waits for a car that is always eight minutes late. The driver takes his own mother somewhere at six and has done for four years, and Mr Ok has never once mentioned it.

That is the second most useful thing Georgij learned about him and he learned it on the Sunday.

---

He was on the corner at ten past.

---

"Mr Ok."

"Yes."

"My name will not mean anything to you. I would like four minutes and I am not selling anything and I am not from a newspaper."

---

Mr Ok looked at him the way a man of that sort looks at a stranger who knows his name, which is politely and with the whole of his attention.

"Four minutes," he said. "My car is eight minutes late."

"I know. That is why I am here now."

---

There is a low wall outside that building with a hedge behind it, and they stood at it. Mr Ok put his case down on it the way a man does when he expects to pick it up again shortly.

---

"On the twelfth of March," said Georgij, "at twenty to five in the afternoon, a page was copied twice on the machine in your corridor."

---

Mr Ok did not say anything.

"It was the last page of the tidied catalogue that the auction house on the river sent to its buyers in the first week of March. Yours came into the office on the third. It has four references on it and a phrase underneath them."

"I am not going to discuss my office with a man on a wall."

"No. I am not asking you to discuss anything. I am going to tell you what happened and then I am going to ask you one question, and you can decide about the question when we get there."

---

Mr Ok picked his case back up.

"Then be quick."

---

"Mrs Uhm did the copying," said Georgij. "She has done your filing since 2004 and she did not think about it for one second, because a person who is asked to copy a page copies the page. She is not going to be troubled by anybody, ever, and I want that said before the rest of it."

---

That is the sentence that stopped him, and Georgij had known on the Sunday that it would be, and had put it there for that reason and had also meant it.

---

"One of those copies went to a woman in Ulsan called Nam Byung-hee. She runs four trucks and she chaired an association of six small shippers for twenty years." He said it evenly and did not hurry. "On the twenty-third of April her six members voted her out of the chair. Her lane has no work on it. By the middle of May she will not have a firm."

---

The traffic did what traffic does.

---

"That has nothing to do with me," said Mr Ok.

"No," said Georgij. "It has nothing to do with you at all, and that is the exact truth, and I am not going to improve it."

---

"Then why are you standing at my wall."

---

"Because it went through your machine," he said. "It did not go through anybody else's. Somebody asked you for something small, in a way that made it small, and it was small, and it is the reason a woman of fifty-eight stayed behind on Thursday and stacked the chairs."

---

Mr Ok put the case down again.

He did not do it deliberately. That is simply where his hand went.

---

"I want to be clear about what is not going to happen," said Georgij, "because you are about to spend the night working out what it is, and you will get it wrong, and I would rather you slept."

"Say it, then."

"Nothing is going to happen to your firm. Nothing is going to happen to your family. Nobody is going to speak to your clients, your insurers, your bank or your brother-in-law in Suwon." He did not soften it. "You will not lose one won over this and neither will anybody who works for you. That is not a negotiating position and there is nothing you can do that would change it."

---

Mr Ok looked at him for a long moment.

"Why."

"Because you did not choose anything." Georgij kept his hands where they could be seen. "You were asked and you said yes, the way a man says yes to a small thing, and I do not take people apart for that. It is the only rule I have made this month and I made it before I knew it was going to be about you."

---

"You are telling me I am not worth it."

"I am telling you that you are a hand."

---

That landed harder than any of the rest of it, and Georgij watched it land and did not enjoy it and did not look away either.

---

"Now the question," he said.

---

"Who asked you."

---

Mr Ok did not answer for about six seconds.

"I do not know his name."

---

"Say the whole of it and let me decide what it is worth."

---

"There was a lunch in January.
